As of 2025-09-25, this report presents a comprehensive plan for Geode's next two releases (v0.18 & v0.19) focused on closing the post-v0.17.0 review gaps. We prioritize distributed query federation, cloud backups, cost-based optimizer (CBO) improvements, CDC webhook hardening, security enhancements (TDE & MFA), full-text search upgrades, and concurrency/throughput gains. Each section below details **why** it matters (user impact), **what** to do (design options aligned with standards), and **how** we'll measure success (acceptance tests, benchmarks, rollout safeguards). The evidence-backed strategy balances ambitious feature goals with risk mitigation, ensuring Geode continues its trajectory toward a fully **ISO GQL**\-compliant, production-grade distributed graph database.

## Executive Summary (≤200 words)

**Geode v0.17.0** delivered critical features (complete MATCH semantics, DML with RLS, initial CBO, CDC sinks, Raft clustering, QUIC/TLS) and achieved a major **feature-completeness milestone**[\[1\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L9-L17)[\[2\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L25-L32). However, a post-release gap review identified several **must-address items** that, while not undermining core correctness, are vital for Geode's maturity in real-world deployments. These include: - **Distributed Query Federation:** Enabling cross-shard graph queries with **global ORDER BY/LIMIT** for deterministic results (to unlock horizontal scaling and consistent pagination)[\[3\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L37-L40)[\[4\]](https://planetscale.com/blog/mysql-pagination#:~:text=Pagination%20in%20MySQL%20,same%20way%20every%20single). - **Cloud Backups (S3):** Integrating with S3-compatible storage for durable backups with integrity checks, encryption, and tested restore processes[\[5\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L38-L40)[\[6\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L164-L169). - **CBO Enhancements:** Calibrating the cost-based optimizer for small datasets and establishing confidence to **enable it by default** (post extensive testing)[\[7\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L160). - **CDC Webhooks Hardening:** Supporting **custom HTTP headers** (for auth and routing) and improving reliability (retries, backoff, metrics)[\[7\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L160). - **Security Upgrades:** Implementing **Transparent Data Encryption (TDE)** for data-at-rest protection and optional **Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)** in user login flows (meeting enterprise compliance standards)[\[8\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=Transparent%20Data%20Encryption%20,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS)[\[9\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=The%20recent%20Oracle%20Database%2023ai,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c). - **Full-Text Search Improvements:** Adding phrase queries, result highlighting, and tunable ranking (beyond basic BM25)[\[10\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L34-L39)[\[11\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=,tags%20or%20otherwise%20emphasizing%20them). - **Concurrency & Throughput:** Introducing **multi-threaded execution** and better scheduling to utilize modern multi-core servers, improving throughput and fairness under load[\[12\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L24-L32)[\[13\]](https://tidb-blog.vercel.app/posts/the-most-underrated-tidb-feature#:~:text=1,when%20comparing%20MySQL%20to%20TiDB).

This update plan **ranks federation and backups as top priority (Must-Have)**, followed closely by CBO readiness and CDC/header fixes, with security and FTS as strong Should-Haves. Concurrency improvements are slated as a parallel effort to de-risk scaling. For each theme, we outline design options (with pros/cons and compatibility considerations), user personas benefitting, compliance implications (ISO GQL rules, security standards), complexity and failure modes, acceptance criteria, benchmarking approach, and rollout strategy (feature flags, canary releases, and observability metrics).

**In summary,** v0.18.0 (target Q4 2025) will focus on **foundational distributed capabilities (federated queries, S3 backups)** and critical fixes (CDC headers, CBO tuning under flag), while v0.19.0 (Q1 2026) will push **performance and security** (enable CBO by default, multi-threading, TDE/MFA, advanced FTS). By following this plan, Geode will substantially reduce its remaining gap to a fully enterprise-ready, distributed graph database platform.

## Background & Context

Geode is an emerging **graph DBMS** aiming for full ISO/IEC 39075 GQL compliance[\[14\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L5-L13). The recent **v0.17.0 release (2025-09-25)** marked a pivot from prototype to a feature-complete core, adding production-grade implementations of graph pattern matching, DML, cost-based optimization (CBO v1), change-data-capture (CDC) sinks, and Raft-based clustering[\[1\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L9-L17)[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48). This was a significant leap in functionality, backed by an evidence-driven development approach (CANARY requirements tracing and extensive testing) to ensure correctness[\[16\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L4-L12). Geode v0.17 introduced **MATCH support** for complex graph patterns (including nested optionals and cycle detection), **WHERE clause enhancements** (subqueries, quantifiers), **DML operations** with row-level security (RLS) enforcement, a rudimentary **Cost-Based Optimizer** (statistics registry and join reordering, gated behind an env flag), **CDC sinks** for Kafka and HTTP (with at-least-once delivery semantics), and **Raft consensus** for replication (durable log, snapshots, dynamic membership)[\[17\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L11-L19)[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48). The transport layer was hardened by mandating QUIC+TLS only (no plaintext TCP)[\[18\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L61-L69). Performance metrics showed promising gains (e.g. 10× faster complex joins with CBO on, 50× faster recovery via snapshots)[\[19\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L75-L83).

_Post-Release Review Findings:_ Despite these advancements, an internal **gap analysis** and code review identified areas where Geode still lags behind its product vision. As of v0.17, Geode remains **single-node oriented** in query execution - while Raft provides replication, there is no support for _sharding_ or federated queries across multiple nodes (the system "effectively runs as a single-node" cluster for queries)[\[3\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L37-L40). The optimizer, though present, was flagged for **suboptimal plans on small datasets (<100 rows)**[\[7\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L160) and kept off by default (requiring GEODE_ENABLE_CBO=1)[\[20\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L25-L31), indicating the need for further calibration before it can be the default planner. The **CDC Webhook sink** was missing support for custom headers[\[7\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L160) - a limitation for real-world integrations where authentication or routing info must be passed in HTTP headers. In the **security** domain, Geode implemented TLS in transport and basic auth/roles, but more advanced features like **encryption at rest (TDE)** and **MFA for logins** were not yet in place (though code stubs for MFA exist)[\[21\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L12-L15). **Full-text search (FTS)** was only partially done: an inverted index with BM25 scoring exists, but key capabilities like phrase queries, proximity search, and result highlighting were not implemented[\[10\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L34-L39). Additionally, **concurrency** was pinpointed as a concern - the server executes queries essentially single-threaded, limiting throughput and leaving multi-core performance on the table[\[12\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L24-L32)[\[22\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L14-L15). These gaps, while _non-critical for correctness_, are critical for **usability, performance, and meeting user expectations** in production settings.

_Why Now?_ Each gap corresponds to a capability expected by target users (enterprise and large-scale deployments) or required by industry standards. For instance, **distributed query federation** is needed for Geode to scale horizontally; without it, Geode cannot effectively handle very large graphs partitioned across servers or enable cross-graph analytics, limiting adoption in big data scenarios. **Cloud backup integration** (e.g., saving snapshots to Amazon S3) is essential for disaster recovery in cloud deployments - currently, Geode's "no hot backups" gap[\[5\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L38-L40) means administrators lack a reliable, cloud-friendly way to safeguard data. The **cost optimizer improvements** are a natural follow-up to v0.17's introduction of CBO: we must refine it so that by default it consistently chooses good plans (especially avoiding regressions on small or skewed datasets). Similarly, the **CDC webhook enhancement** is a targeted fix to make change-feed integrations more flexible (e.g., include auth tokens in webhook calls). **Security hardening** responds to rising compliance demands: encryption at rest and MFA are increasingly mandated in sectors like finance and healthcare[\[8\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=Transparent%20Data%20Encryption%20,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS)[\[23\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=transaction%20records%2C%20and%20personal%20identifiers,standards%2C%20including%20the%20Payment%20Card). **Full-text search improvements** address user-facing query capabilities - without phrase search or highlighting, text queries are less useful (users expect Google-like search features). Lastly, **multi-threaded execution** will unlock significant performance improvements: other systems (e.g., **TiDB**) have shown orders-of-magnitude better query latency by utilizing multi-core parallelism[\[13\]](https://tidb-blog.vercel.app/posts/the-most-underrated-tidb-feature#:~:text=1,when%20comparing%20MySQL%20to%20TiDB).

This plan uses the v0.17 review as the baseline requirements document and draws on industry best practices and analogies from systems like Neo4j (Fabric), Amazon Neptune, TigerGraph, JanusGraph, CockroachDB/Spanner, and Postgres to inform solutions. Our approach is evidence-driven: each recommendation is backed by references (standards, vendor docs, or engineering reports) to ensure feasibility and alignment with state-of-the-art. The upcoming sections detail **Core Findings by Theme**, answering key sub-questions (SQ1-SQ7) for each gap area, followed by cross-cutting considerations (methodology, risk, and outlook). All information is current _as of 2025-09-25_.

## Core Findings by Theme

### 1\. Distributed Query Federation

**What & Why:** _Distributed query federation_ is the ability to execute a single GQL query across multiple shards/partitions of the graph, possibly on different nodes, yielding results as if over a single unified dataset. This includes cross-shard **joins**, **aggregations**, and properly handling **ORDER BY/LIMIT** on a global result set. This feature primarily benefits **enterprise deployments** and **large graph use-cases** (SQ1: User Impact). For example, a social network graph too large for one server can be partitioned by user; federation allows queries (like friend-of-friend or community detection patterns) across all partitions. Personas that gain include **DB admins/DevOps** (who can scale out horizontally, avoiding one-machine limits) and **Data analysts** running global queries without manual data collation. It also unlocks P0 use-cases like **global graph analytics**, **multi-tenant analytics** (where each shard is a tenant's subgraph, but one can query across tenants if permitted), and cross-region data federation. Without this, Geode remains effectively siloed per node, whereas competitors (Neo4j Fabric, Amazon Neptune's partitioning) offer ways to **query a sharded graph transparently**[\[24\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=This%20capability%20can%20be%20used,processing%2C%20or%20different%20hybrid%20deployments)[\[25\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=Finally%2C%20we%20will%20show%20how,sharded%20versions).

**Design Options (SQ2):** Architecturally, enabling distributed queries in Geode can follow a **Coordinator-Worker pattern** or a **Decentralized scatter/gather** approach: - **Option 1: Dedicated Coordinator Node** - Introduce a query coordinator role (could be a special Geode instance or an elected leader in the Raft cluster) that receives the client's query, devises a distributed execution plan, and orchestrates execution on multiple shard nodes. This is akin to how systems like **Citus (Postgres)** or **CockroachDB** operate: a coordinator parses and plans the query, then sends sub-queries to shards and merges the results[\[26\]](https://docs.citusdata.com/en/v9.5/develop/reference_processing.html#:~:text=A%20Citus%20cluster%20consists%20of,brief%20by%20the%20diagram%20below)[\[27\]](https://docs.citusdata.com/en/v9.5/develop/reference_processing.html#:~:text=Citus%E2%80%99s%20distributed%20query%20planner%20takes,plans%20it%20for%20distributed%20execution). The coordinator would handle global operations such as final sorting/limiting and join result merging. This design aligns well with Geode's existing **Raft/2PC** infrastructure: since v0.17 already has "2PC over Raft" for distributed transactions[\[28\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L34-L38), the coordinator can leverage the Raft membership and consistency mechanisms to dispatch read queries safely (and use 2PC for cross-shard updates if later extended to writes). - **Option 2: Decentralized Query Federation** - Alternatively, any node could act as a query router: the client query is broadcast or partitioned among shards in a peer-to-peer fashion, and the initiating node gathers responses. This is how some **NewSQL** systems and **Neo4j Fabric** operate: the client connects to a fabric that knows all member databases and splits the query accordingly, with possibly each shard doing partial work and sending back to the client or primary node[\[29\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=Sharding%20graph%20data%20is%20a,for%20the%20specific%20use%20case). This avoids a single coordinator bottleneck, but adds complexity in ensuring all nodes have a consistent view of cluster metadata and in handling partial failures.

Given Geode's architecture and relatively small cluster sizes (initially), **Option 1 (Coordinator)** is preferable for simplicity and control. It centralizes the logic for distributed planning/execution, reducing duplicated logic on every node. We can implement the coordinator as an extension of the current server: perhaps designate the Raft leader as the query coordinator for now (since it's already handling cluster-wide decisions). This plays nicely with the **Raft-based shard membership** Geode has[\[28\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L34-L38) - the coordinator can use the Raft log to store or coordinate metadata about shards, and use Raft consensus for consistency where needed (e.g., consistent snapshot reads across shards). The execution flow would be: 1. **Parsing & Global Planning:** The coordinator parses the GQL query and generates an initial logical plan. It then consults a **shard catalog** (mapping data to shards) to determine which shards hold the data relevant to each part of the query (e.g., which shards to scan for each MATCH pattern or table). 2. **Fragmentation:** The plan is divided into sub-plans per shard - e.g., each shard gets the portion of the query it can execute on local data (such as matching a subgraph pattern within that shard). Joins that require data from multiple shards may need either _repartitioning_ or collecting data to the coordinator. 3. **Distributed Execution:** The coordinator sends subqueries (with filters, projections, etc.) to each target shard (over the existing QUIC/TLS network). Shards execute them (leveraging their local query engine) and stream results back. 4. **Global Merge:** The coordinator merges these results. For simple UNION/DISTINCT operations, this might just be concatenation followed by deduplication if needed. For joins across shards, the coordinator might have to perform the join of partial results (unless a smarter pushdown can be done - see trade-offs below). For **ORDER BY + LIMIT**, the coordinator must perform a **global sort/merge**: each shard can sort its results locally, but the coordinator reads the top-K from each and then determines the overall top-K correctly[\[30\]](https://www.yugabyte.com/blog/optimize-pagination-distributed-data-maintain-ordering/#:~:text=0%20and%20event_ts%20,infinity%27%20ORDER%20BY%20event_ts%20DESC)[\[31\]](https://www.yugabyte.com/blog/optimize-pagination-distributed-data-maintain-ordering/#:~:text=match%20at%20L383%20shard_id%3D0%20and,2%20ORDER%20BY%20event_ts%20DESC). There are known algorithms for distributed top-K: e.g., each shard returns K results, the coordinator finds the global K-th largest and asks shards for any additional results above that threshold (to ensure none are missed)[\[32\]](https://crypto.stanford.edu/~cao/topk.pdf#:~:text=%5BPDF%5D%20Efficient%20Top,in%20a%20distributed%20network). 5. **Consistency:** For read queries, Geode can use _snapshot isolation_ across shards by having each shard execute at the same Raft log index or timestamp (to avoid reading partially committed data from one shard and not another). Our Raft already ensures a causally consistent state across nodes.

**Trade-offs:** The coordinator approach introduces a potential **bottleneck** (one node doing the merge and final compute). However, for moderate cluster sizes and typical query result sizes (often much smaller than raw data), this is acceptable. In later versions, we could allow multiple coordinators or a hierarchical query plan (coordinators of coordinators) to scale out the merging step if needed (this is how systems like BigQuery or Spark scale distributed joins). The decentralized approach (each node talking to others) might reduce coordinator load but complicates failure handling (who retries if one shard is slow?) and determinism. Given Geode's stage, a **central planner/executor** is the straightforward path.

**Global ORDER BY/LIMIT semantics (SQ3 - Standards):** One crucial semantic issue is ensuring a **deterministic global order** when queries use ORDER BY ... LIMIT/OFFSET. ISO GQL and standard SQL require that if a query uses OFFSET/LIMIT, the ordering of results must be fully specified to make the pagination deterministic[\[4\]](https://planetscale.com/blog/mysql-pagination#:~:text=Pagination%20in%20MySQL%20,same%20way%20every%20single). Geode v0.17 already enforces that **an ORDER BY is required with LIMIT/OFFSET** (returns an error otherwise)[\[33\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L70-L74), which is a compliance with ISO and a protection against nondeterministic paging. In a distributed context, this becomes trickier: we must ensure that ordering is **global**, not just per shard. By default, if each shard sorted and we concatenated, the result would not be globally sorted. We have to perform a **merge sort** of shard results or a similar algorithm to produce a globally sorted stream. Determinism also extends to tie-breaks: if two records have equal sort key, we need a consistent secondary order. Geode addressed tie-break rules in v0.17 for single-node (the release notes mention "UA005/UA006 tie-break rules for consistent results"[\[34\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L14-L17)). We should carry that approach over to the distributed case: e.g., include the shard ID or a unique internal ID as a hidden secondary sort key to break ties consistently across shards (this ensures that pagination doesn't unpredictably swap equal-ranked results). This approach is consistent with ISO semantic expectations that query results, given the same data and query, should be reproducible and well-defined.

Another compliance angle: **global aggregates and grouping**. If a user does MATCH ... RETURN x, count(\*) GROUP BY x, and data is sharded, the engine should compute this as if it scanned a single dataset. Typically, this means doing local aggregation on each shard and then a final aggregation merge (a two-phase aggregation). That is an implementation detail, but we must ensure the output is identical to a single-node execution, fulfilling the ISO GQL semantics for grouping and duplicates.

**Complexity & Risk (SQ4):** Implementing distributed queries introduces several complexity points: - **Metadata Management:** We need to know which shard holds which part of the graph (likely partitioning by some key or by relationship types, etc.). A **catalog** or shard map must be maintained, and kept consistent as the cluster changes (Raft dynamic membership is in place[\[35\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L158-L161), but true sharding might need more metadata). Failure to route a subquery to the correct shards would yield incomplete results or errors. - **Query Planner Changes:** The planner must be able to _fragment_ queries. Geode's current planner is cost-based (left-deep join enumeration) but only for single node. Extending it to distributed planning is complex - we have to consider data shipping costs. There's a risk of performance pitfalls like _data fan-out_: e.g., if the planner naively pulls huge intermediate results to the coordinator when a different join strategy could minimize data movement. A known issue in distributed systems is choosing the right join approach (e.g., _distributed hash join_ vs. _broadcast join_ vs. _rebalance partition join_). If not done carefully, a query could become network-bound and slow. Initially, we might restrict some queries (like prohibit joins without a common partition key unless small) to avoid worst-case scenarios. - **Global Order Performance:** Sorting a huge global result is expensive. However, typically users will LIMIT results (e.g., top 100). We should implement **distributed top-K** algorithms to minimize data transfer: each shard can compute its top K, then the coordinator computes a cutoff and requests only necessary additional rows[\[30\]](https://www.yugabyte.com/blog/optimize-pagination-distributed-data-maintain-ordering/#:~:text=0%20and%20event_ts%20,infinity%27%20ORDER%20BY%20event_ts%20DESC)[\[31\]](https://www.yugabyte.com/blog/optimize-pagination-distributed-data-maintain-ordering/#:~:text=match%20at%20L383%20shard_id%3D0%20and,2%20ORDER%20BY%20event_ts%20DESC). Still, in worst-case (no LIMIT), the coordinator may have to merge all data - which could be slow and memory-intensive. We need to ensure we stream results and possibly use external sorting or incremental output to avoid memory blow-ups. - **Failure Modes:** In a distributed query, one or more shards might fail or time out during execution. We must design for **partial failure**: e.g., if one shard node crashes mid-query, does the whole query fail? Probably yes for now (with an error indicating shard failure). In future, we might retry that fragment on a replica (given Raft replication, a follower could potentially serve a read). At minimum, we need robust timeout and cancellation logic: the coordinator should be able to cancel sub-queries on other shards if one fails, to avoid orphan work. - **Transactional Consistency:** For now, we are focusing on _read-only federated queries_. If we later allow multi-shard write transactions, we must integrate with our existing 2PC mechanism (which v0.17 has prototyped with a durable coordinator log)[\[28\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L34-L38). That entails additional risks like distributed deadlocks or two-phase commit failures. But in the v0.18 timeline, read queries are the primary target, which simplifies things.

**Mitigation of Risks:** To handle these, we will: - Start with **simpler use-cases**: e.g., implement cross-shard _UNION/concatenation_ and _global sorting/limit_ first, then add support for distributed _JOINs_ step by step. We may initially require that the join key is the partition key (so that we don't have to shuffle data between shards), which is a common approach in early distributed DB implementations. - Use **observability** (detailed logs or metrics on distributed query execution time, data moved, etc.) to detect performance issues. We'll define a threshold for acceptable network overhead per query and flag if exceeded. - Ensure **safe fallback**: If a query is too complex to distribute well (e.g., a complex join of the entire graph), we could (as a last resort) either disallow it with a meaningful message or pull all data to the coordinator. The latter is obviously not scalable but ensures correctness; we'd log a warning that the query ran in a degenerate mode. This prevents silent incorrect results. - Thoroughly test failure scenarios: kill one shard during a query to verify the coordinator times out and returns a graceful error. Also test network partition scenarios if possible.

**Benchmarks & Success Criteria (SQ5):** We will validate distributed query support with benchmarks inspired by real-world scenarios: - _Functional Correctness:_ Using the **LDBC Social Network Benchmark (SNB)** data or similar (which is a standard graph benchmark) split across shards (e.g., by person ID as in Neo4j's Fabric example[\[29\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=Sharding%20graph%20data%20is%20a,for%20the%20specific%20use%20case)). We will run known complex queries (e.g., "find friends-of-friends of X with certain criteria across the network") and verify that results are identical to a single-node execution (this ensures no data loss or duplication in merging). - _Deterministic Pagination:_ Create a scenario with data deliberately spread on different shards but with overlapping sort keys. For example, 1000 "Person" nodes evenly split into two shards; run a MATCH ... RETURN name ORDER BY age LIMIT 10. The same query repeated should consistently return the same 10 results in order (no random flips). Also, test that offsetting into the second page returns the correct next set. We will inspect that no duplicate or missing entries occur between page transitions. This validates global ORDER BY correctness. - _Performance Benchmarks:_ We'll measure: - **Latency scaling with shards:** Take a query (without LIMIT) that touches all data (like a full graph scan with a simple filter). Run it on 1 shard vs 2 vs 4 shards of equal size. Ideally, with parallel processing, the query should finish as fast or faster on multiple shards (super-linear is possible if each shard's work is smaller and done in parallel). We set a target that a distributed query on N shards should complete in ~1/N of the time of a single shard (plus some overhead). A more concrete metric: if 1 shard = 100k nodes query takes 10s, 2 shards of 50k each should ideally be ~5-6s (some overhead for coordination). We target <2x slowdown compared to ideal linear speedup. - **Throughput under concurrency:** Fire multiple distributed queries concurrently to ensure the coordinator can handle it. This is partly a concurrency test but also relevant here: we want the cluster to sustain a certain QPS of multi-shard queries. - **Tail latency:** If one shard is slower (perhaps simulate one shard with more load or data), ensure the coordinator can still return results reasonably. We define success as P95 query latency not more than, say, 20% worse than the median, when one shard has 2× the data of others. Essentially, we don't want a single slow shard to completely stall all queries; some amount of load balancing or parallelism can mitigate that. - **Limit & Top-K tests:** On a large dataset, run queries with a LIMIT (e.g., top 100 by some score) and ensure we do not fetch all data unnecessarily. We can measure network traffic: it should be significantly less than total data volume if K is small. For instance, if each of 10 shards has 1M items but we ask for top 100 overall, the system might fetch maybe 1000 (100 from each) or a bit more, but nowhere near 10M. We'll instrument to verify data shipped is O(shards \* K) not O(total). Meeting that ensures our distributed top-K algorithm is working efficiently.

The acceptance criteria for federation will include: - **Correctness:** Cross-shard joins and aggregations produce the same result as if data were on one node (verified by diffing outputs). - **Deterministic ORDER/LIMIT:** Queries with ORDER BY + LIMIT give consistent results across repeated runs and across cluster reconfigurations (no duplicate/missing items when paginating). - **Fault Tolerance:** A query will fail gracefully (and not hang indefinitely) if a shard is unreachable; in cluster failover scenarios (one node goes down mid-query), either partial results are merged with a warning or the query is aborted with an error - but no silent wrong results. - **Scalability:** Verified by measuring that adding shards can reduce query latency for large data volumes (within 2× of ideal linear scale, as noted). - **Global secondary effects:** e.g., ensure that **global uniqueness constraints** or ordering does not assume a single node. (Though in read queries this is less of an issue, it might arise in writes which we are not covering yet.)

These criteria tie back to the evidence: e.g., CockroachDB and Spanner literature emphasize maintaining consistency and determinism across distributed queries[\[36\]](https://softwareengineering.stackexchange.com/questions/418223/is-deterministic-select-possible-without-specifying-an-order-by#:~:text=Is%20deterministic%20SELECT%20possible%20without,without%20proper%20ORDER%20BY%20clauses). Neo4j's Fabric documentation stresses that relationships do not span shards and require careful query design[\[29\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=Sharding%20graph%20data%20is%20a,for%20the%20specific%20use%20case); we'll adopt similar awareness.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** To implement this, some pre-work is needed: - **Sharding Strategy & Catalog:** We need to decide on a sharding key or strategy (could be explicit by the user or automatic). For v0.18, we might take a simple approach: e.g., each graph (or each label type) is wholly assigned to a shard, or hash partition nodes by an ID. The _catalog_ of shards (which node has which partition of which graph) must be maintained. This likely means extending the existing metadata store (maybe using the Raft leader or a small distributed KV table). - **Query Planner Refactor:** The planner must become aware of distribution. Possibly, we implement a new **DistributedPlanner** component (as indicated in our roadmap notes[\[37\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L19-L25)) that wraps the existing cost-based planner. One approach is: for each subquery, call the existing planner to get a local plan, and then compose these. We might also need a **Stats registry extension**: the CBO should know the data distribution (e.g., how many rows per shard) to cost a distributed plan properly. This might require augmenting the statistics with per-shard breakdowns. - **Network RPC layer:** We have QUIC/TLS networking now[\[18\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L61-L69). We'll need an RPC mechanism for the coordinator to send query fragments and retrieve results. Potentially, define a new message type in our protocol (e.g., RUN_FRAGMENT) that carries a query plan or GQL string plus parameters to a shard. The shards need a handler to execute that and stream back data. Reusing existing CDC or query protocol messages (like frames that already exist for queries) might be possible with minor adjustments. - **Parallel execution support:** On the coordinator, it will need to handle multiple shard responses simultaneously. That introduces concurrency on the coordinator side (multiple network reads), which overlaps with the "Concurrency" theme. We likely need to use multi-threading or async IO for the coordinator to listen to many shards at once. This dependency ties in with our concurrency improvements - we might implement the coordinator using async awaits or spawn threads for each shard query. - **Testing harness:** Possibly extend our integration test framework to spawn multiple Geode instances in a cluster (the scripts/cluster_spawn.sh used for Raft testing could be extended for query tests[\[38\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L43-L46)). This is necessary to automated test distributed queries.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** Given the complexity, we will roll out distributed query federation carefully: - In **v0.18.0**, we will mark this feature as **experimental** and off by default (maybe behind a flag like GEODE_ENABLE_FEDERATION). Users can opt-in. We will include it in release notes as a tech preview, with warnings about known limitations (e.g., might not support arbitrary joins, or performance might degrade in some cases). - We will conduct **internal canary tests** by enabling it in our staging environments, running a battery of queries. Observability hooks (detailed timing logs for each stage, maybe a debug option to log the distributed plan) will help us fine-tune before broad enabling. - Telemetry/metrics: the coordinator should record metrics like "distributed query count", "shards touched", "bytes received per query", etc., and expose them. This allows monitoring in production for any issues (e.g., if one query causes huge data transfer). - For **graduation to default-on** (perhaps by v0.19 or later), we will set criteria such as: pass all acceptance tests, demonstrate on benchmarks that common queries run efficiently, and ensure no major correctness issues open. When confident, we remove the flag and let any multi-node cluster automatically use federation. - We will also add a configuration to **opt-out** even after default-on, in case some users hit an edge case and need to fall back to single-node mode for a particular query (in worst case, they could direct all queries to one node explicitly as a workaround).

### 2\. Backups to Cloud Object Storage (S3)

**What & Why:** Implementing **backups to cloud object storage (e.g., Amazon S3)** means Geode can export its data (snapshots or incremental logs) to durable, external storage and later restore from it. This is essential for **disaster recovery, cloud deployment, and compliance**. Users (DBAs, DevOps) benefit from automating backups to a service like S3 which provides high durability (11 nines) and geographic redundancy. It unlocks P0 use-cases such as: - **Point-in-Time Recovery:** If a production Geode cluster's data is corrupted or lost (node failure, user error), having an S3 backup allows restoration to a recent point with minimal data loss (RPO - Recovery Point Objective). - **Cloning & Testing:** Teams can take a backup from production and restore it in a dev environment (e.g., to test a migration) by pulling from S3. - **Long-term retention & Compliance:** Many regulations require keeping data snapshots for X years. Cloud storage is cheaper and easier for this than on-node disks. S3 offers features like object lock (WORM storage) to ensure backups are tamper-proof for compliance.

As of v0.17, Geode had **no hot backup** capability (only the live WAL and snapshots on local disk)[\[5\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L38-L40). This is a serious gap compared to other DBs (Neo4j, Cockroach, etc., all have backup tools, and cloud databases like Neptune have automated snapshot to S3). In fact, **future work** in v0.17's notes explicitly lists _"S3-compatible backup destinations"_[\[6\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L164-L169) as a planned item, underlining its priority.

**Design Options (SQ2):** There are a few aspects to consider: backup format, triggering mechanism, and integration with S3 API. Options include: - **Full Snapshots vs Incremental Backups:** A full snapshot means periodically taking a consistent image of the entire database (all nodes). Incremental means after an initial full, only changes (like WAL segments or diffs) are uploaded regularly. We likely want to support both: e.g., weekly full snapshots and continuous incremental (like daily or even streaming). For design, we can start with **on-demand full snapshots** to S3 (easier to implement), then add incremental (perhaps by leveraging the WAL or Raft log segments). - **Consistency Point:** In a cluster, we must coordinate so that a backup represents a **consistent cut** of the data (no half-committed transactions). With Raft, one approach: trigger a Raft snapshot at the leader (Raft already can produce a snapshot of state)[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48) and use that as the data to upload. Alternatively, pause writes, gather a snapshot from each node at the same log index, then resume (this causes a brief write stall but ensures consistency). - **Direct S3 Integration vs External Tool:** We could implement backup as an internal feature (Geode nodes directly upload to S3 via S3 API calls) or provide hooks for an external tool (e.g., Geode dumps a file and an external script uploads it). The trend in modern DBs (Cockroach, etc.) is to have built-in S3 integration for ease of use[\[39\]](https://n2ws.com/blog/aws-backup-best-practices#:~:text=Top%2010%20AWS%20Backup%20Best,RTO%29)[\[40\]](https://dev.to/arvind_toorpu/enabling-database-backup-and-restore-to-s3-for-sql-server-in-aws-rds-a-step-by-step-guide-5hem#:~:text=Enabling%20Database%20Backup%20and%20Restore,required%20S3%20bucket%20and%20operations). We'll likely integrate via an S3 client library (or REST calls) within Geode.

Given user convenience, we lean towards **direct integration**: e.g., a GQL command or admin REST call BACKUP TO 's3://bucket/path' that causes each node to upload its part. There might be an internal "BackupService" that orchestrates it cluster-wide.

**Key design points:** - **Data Format:** Reuse the existing snapshot format that Geode uses for Raft snapshots (if any). v0.17 mentions _"snapshots with compression"_[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48). Possibly, each node can produce a compressed snapshot file of its store. We should ensure the format includes metadata like cluster topology, Raft term/index of snapshot, etc. Alternatively, a logical backup (like exporting GQL data as CSV/JSON) is possible but not as efficient for full restore. So we likely prefer **binary snapshots** (perhaps RocksDB files or Geode's page files). - **Multipart Upload & Large Files:** S3 has a limit of 5GB for single PUT; larger must use multipart. We should plan to use AWS S3's multipart upload for big backup files[\[41\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=,the%20system%20also%20tracks%20changes). Or we chunk our backups into e.g. 100MB pieces. The acceptance criterion mentioned _"Multipart upload"_ explicitly[\[42\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L22-L25), so this is needed to ensure reliability for large backups. - **Integrity Checking:** It's critical that we verify backups are not corrupted. S3 provides an ETag (which for single-part uploads is often the MD5) and now has a **CRC and MD5 checksum API**[\[43\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/introducing-default-data-integrity-protections-for-new-objects-in-amazon-s3/#:~:text=Amazon%20S3%20has%20always%20verified,before%20they%20are%20written). Geode should compute a checksum for each backup part and either compare with S3's checksum or store it as metadata. Our test in v0.17 included _"backup/restore with CRC"_[\[44\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L100-L105), indicating we already intended to validate integrity via CRC. So design: after upload, Geode can call S3's GetObjectAttributes to get the checksum (with the new S3 MD5 or SHA256 ETag features[\[45\]](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/checking-object-integrity.html#:~:text=Documentation%20docs,without%20copying%20or%20downloading%20data)) and compare, or store our own CRC in a manifest. The backup manifest file (maybe a JSON listing all files, their sizes, checksums, and a cluster snapshot ID) can be written and verified on restore. - **Encryption:** Backups often contain sensitive data, so encryption is needed. There are two layers: - **At-rest in S3:** Many use server-side encryption (SSE-S3 or SSE-KMS), which AWS can handle if we just set a flag and have IAM permissions[\[40\]](https://dev.to/arvind_toorpu/enabling-database-backup-and-restore-to-s3-for-sql-server-in-aws-rds-a-step-by-step-guide-5hem#:~:text=Enabling%20Database%20Backup%20and%20Restore,required%20S3%20bucket%20and%20operations)[\[46\]](https://repost.aws/questions/QUjZrarwG_TOOoPZeGmIeVzQ/aws-kms-for-database-tde#:~:text=AWS%20,be%20stored%20in%20AWS%20CloudHSM). We likely should allow the user to specify an S3 SSE option (S3-managed keys or a KMS key ID). That covers encryption in S3. - **In-transit:** We should use HTTPS for S3 upload (which is default for S3 APIs). - Possibly, if extra security needed, allow **client-side encryption** (where Geode encrypts data before sending). This complicates restore (must have keys in Geode). But if using KMS-managed keys is feasible, better to offload to AWS KMS via SSE-KMS. As an initial step, supporting S3's native encryption might suffice. - **Retention & Lifecycle:** We won't reinvent S3's lifecycle management, but we should provide configurability like "keep last N backups" or allow users to leverage S3 lifecycle rules (perhaps just document that old backups can be auto-deleted by bucket policy). However, an important aspect is **retention of backups vs deletion of data**: if a user drops the DB or deletes some data, the backup remains unless cleaned - for compliance, sometimes backups must be pruned too. We might add a simple retention config (like Geode can delete backups older than X days in the bucket if enabled). But perhaps out-of-scope for first iteration; administrators can manage that manually or via AWS Backup service[\[47\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=Many%20organizations%20are%20protecting%20important,can%20save%20time%20and%20money). - **Restore Process:** Equally important: we need to implement restore (loading from S3). This likely will be offline (stop the cluster, load snapshot, then restart) for v0.18. In future, point-in-time recovery or online restore could be explored. But acceptance criteria definitely include verifying we can successfully restore a backup from S3. - **Integration with Cloud Services:** If running in AWS, using IAM roles for S3 access is ideal (no embedding long-lived credentials). So Geode should support using either: - Environment-provided AWS credentials (IAM instance profile, etc.). - A config with an AWS access key/secret (for non-AWS environments or non-EC2 usage). - Possibly support other S3-compatible stores (MinIO, etc.) by letting endpoint and credentials be configurable.

Comparators: CockroachDB has BACKUP TO 's3://...' and uses KMS for encryption, and verifies backups with checksums. Postgres with tools like WAL-G also do continuous archiving to S3 with CRC checks. Our design aligns with these best practices[\[39\]](https://n2ws.com/blog/aws-backup-best-practices#:~:text=Top%2010%20AWS%20Backup%20Best,RTO%29)[\[48\]](https://serverfault.com/questions/999359/does-aws-cli-do-a-data-integrity-check-on-sync-with-s3#:~:text=Does%20AWS%20cli%20do%20a,retry%20up%20to%20five%20times).

**Standards & Compliance (SQ3):** There isn't an ISO standard for backups, but **industry norms** demand certain behaviors: - **Durability & Atomicity:** A backup taken at time T should reflect the state at T _consistently_. We must document how it achieves consistency (likely quiescing writes or using Raft snapshot which is atomic as of an index). - **Retention & Security:** For regulatory compliance (e.g., GDPR, HIPAA), backups must be encrypted and access-controlled[\[8\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=Transparent%20Data%20Encryption%20,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS)[\[23\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=transaction%20records%2C%20and%20personal%20identifiers,standards%2C%20including%20the%20Payment%20Card). Using TDE ensures the backup files are encrypted at the source, but if we implement before TDE, we rely on S3 encryption. This is acceptable if properly configured (AWS KMS keys can be used to meet compliance standards). - **Backup integrity:** Many enterprise standards (like PCI DSS) require verifying backup integrity upon creation and periodically. Our inclusion of CRC or checksums fulfills that expectation. - **Point-in-time restore**: While perhaps not immediate, eventually we want to support a continuum of backups (like continuous log archiving). In AWS's cloud databases, _point-in-time recovery (PITR)_ is a selling point (Neptune or DynamoDB continuous backups allow restoring to any second in last 24h, etc.). We probably can't achieve that fully yet, but designing with incremental backups in mind (perhaps shipping Raft logs to S3 continuously) sets the stage.

**Complexity & Risk (SQ4):** - **Consistency risk:** If we mishandle the snapshot coordination, a backup might be inconsistent (e.g., some shards at different times). That could lead to an unrecoverable or, worse, subtly corrupted restore. To mitigate, we'll rely on the Raft leader's log index: instruct all followers to take a snapshot at the same index (followers can apply up to that index, then pause). This ensures a uniform cut. There is a risk if one node fails to snapshot - we should have a timeout and possibly retry or skip that node if down (assuming replication, we might proceed with majority). - **Performance impact:** Taking a backup can be I/O intensive (reading all data, compressing, uploading). If done online, it could degrade performance or even cause latency spikes. We might need to rate-limit the upload or do it in chunks to avoid saturating disk or network. Also using **multi-threading** here (to compress and upload parts in parallel) would speed it up - relevant when we have concurrency improvements. - **Failure during backup:** If the process interrupts (node crash, network glitch), partial files might be on S3. We need to handle resume or cleanup. Possibly use S3 object naming with timestamps and don't overwrite older backup unless fully successful. A manifest can have a "complete" flag at end; if not present, we know backup incomplete. - **Restore risk:** Must ensure that a backup from version X can be restored on version Y of Geode (backward compatibility). Given the rapid dev, maybe we constrain to same version for now. Also, a big risk is user error (restoring wrong backup or to wrong environment) - outside our direct control, but we can include backup metadata to prevent, e.g., label backups with cluster ID and warn if mismatch. - **Security credentials:** Storing AWS keys in config is sensitive. If we do that, we must ensure they are protected (maybe in an encrypted form or require environment var injection). Misconfiguration could also point to wrong bucket (potential data leak if someone else's bucket). We should validate the URL carefully. - **Data size and cost:** Graph databases can be huge; uploading TBs to S3 costs time and egress bandwidth. We might need to advise users on this. Possibly integrate compression (v0.17 snapshots are compressed[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48), so that helps). Still, a risk is a backup taking so long that by the time it finishes, it's already out of date. We mitigate by enabling incremental approach for large DBs and by documenting throughput expectations (maybe multi-part parallel upload yields decent speed, but if not, consider splitting the graph and uploading shards concurrently).

**Benchmarks (SQ5):** We will measure the backup feature on various scales: - **Functionality test:** create a known small graph, back it up to a local MinIO (for testing), wipe the DB, restore, and diff the data. This verifies correctness exactly. - **Backup/Restore time:** For a larger dataset (e.g., 100GB), measure how long a full backup takes and the restore time. We will set targets: e.g., backup throughput of at least _50 MB/s per node_ to S3 (which is modest given network bandwidths). If a cluster has 4 nodes, effectively 200 MB/s aggregated. So a 100GB backup might target ~10-15 minutes. This is just a ballpark; actual might be slower with compression CPU overhead. We also measure restore time similarly. - **Impact on running workload:** Run an OLTP workload (some mix of queries) during a backup and see the latency penalty. Ideally, backup should be in a low-traffic window, but we aim for e.g. <20% throughput reduction while backup runs. We can adjust concurrency or throttling to achieve that. - **Space efficiency:** Confirm that backups are stored compressed and maybe deduplicated for incremental. E.g., take a backup, then a second backup after few changes - ensure the second incremental is much smaller (we'll simulate changes to ~5% of data, incremental backup should be roughly 5-10% size of full). - **Reliability tests:** Corrupt a backup file intentionally (e.g., flip a byte) and ensure restore detects an error (via CRC) - i.e., does not silently restore bad data. Also, test abort scenarios: cancel backup mid-way, ensure partial objects either get removed or marked incomplete. Then see that a restore from an incomplete backup is prevented (should error out gracefully). - **Automation:** If possible, test integration with AWS Backup or lifecycle by scheduling multiple backups and verifying older ones get pruned if configured.

Success criteria: - _Completeness:_ After restore, all nodes/edges and properties are present as of backup timestamp (no missing or extra). - _Integrity:_ Backup files pass checksum validation on restore. If any corruption, restore aborts with an error (no silent bad data). - _Performance:_ Backup can complete within a window suitable for typical DB sizes. We may define an RTO/RPO: e.g., RPO could be 1 hour (meaning we support at least hourly increments), RTO maybe target less than 30 minutes for a full restore of 100GB (just an example). - _Cloud integration:_ Verified that backing up to an actual S3 bucket works with both access key and instance role. If in our test environment we can't use real AWS, use a simulator (MinIO). - _Encryption:_ If configured with SSE-KMS, verify the S3 objects show ServerSideEncryption: aws:kms and are indeed encrypted (we can't directly read without permission, etc.). If TDE is implemented concurrently, test that a backup from an encrypted DB produces encrypted files. Actually, if TDE is file-level, the snapshot itself might already be encrypted bytes - which means even if someone gets the backup from S3, they need the key to decrypt (an added security layer). - _Usability:_ Test error paths like invalid bucket name, wrong credentials - ensure clear error messages.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** - We might need a **library for S3** (unless writing raw HTTP). Possibly use AWS SDK (C or Zig analog) - ensure license compatibility. Alternatively, minimal S3 API calls (PUT, List, GET) can be done over HTTPS fairly simply. - **Raft snapshot feature:** if not already exposed for use, we need to expose an API to trigger a snapshot and retrieve its bytes. The release notes show snapshots exist[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48). We might need to adjust how snapshots are taken (maybe historically triggered by log size). Possibly reuse the snapshot mechanism: call InstallSnapshot RPC to each follower - but here we want to gather snapshots, not send them among each other. - **Storage Abstraction:** To keep it open, we might abstract a storage interface so that Azure Blob or GCP GCS could be used in future with different implementations. But that might be over-engineering now. However, writing code specifically for S3 AWS and perhaps generic "S3-compatible" endpoints (like MinIO, Wasabi, etc.) via configurable endpoint and credentials is doable. - **Scheduling**: Possibly integrate with a scheduler or provide a CLI so users can cron a BACKUP command daily. Not critical for internal implementation, but something to think about (maybe just document usage with external scheduler for now). - If TDE is implemented in parallel, coordinate such that backup either dumps encrypted pages (which is fine, then restore needs the key present to read them) or we decrypt before sending (less ideal as it exposes plaintext in transit). The preference: treat the backup as part of the DB files, thus TDE would mean they're encrypted by default - which is secure, but then restore requires keys to be present (we have to be careful to not lose those keys!). This interplay will be considered in the Security section too.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - Introduce the backup feature as **disabled by default** until thoroughly tested. Actually, unlike federation, backup doesn't affect runtime unless invoked, so we can have it available but under a "beta" label in docs. - Provide it as an **explicit command or tool** in v0.18 (no automatic scheduling yet, to avoid surprises). E.g., a geodec admin backup --dest s3://... command that triggers the process. - Document clearly that restores should ideally be done on same version. If we expect possible format changes in v0.19, we might include a converter or at least note incompatibility. - As a safeguard, maintain backward compatibility in format if possible. E.g., test that a backup taken on v0.18 can be restored on v0.19 if no major breaking changes. - Logging: During backup, log progress (e.g., "uploaded 1GB of 5GB…" or percentage) so operators have insight. Also log any slowdowns (if a shard is much slower to snapshot). - Canary testing: We will test backup/restore internally on staging clusters of various sizes (small and large). Possibly engage one or two friendly early users to try it on non-critical data and provide feedback (particularly on performance and ease of restore). - By v0.19, after feedback, we can refine and possibly integrate automated scheduling (maybe an option in config like "daily backups on at 2am" or integration with something like AWS Backup via documentation). - Monitoring: Provide metrics like "LastBackupDuration", "LastBackupSize" in our monitoring (maybe as part of cluster status metrics) so users can set alerts if backups fail or take too long.

### 3\. Cost-Based Optimizer (CBO) Improvements

**What & Why:** Geode's **Cost-Based Optimizer v1** was introduced in v0.17 as an optional feature (off by default)[\[20\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L25-L31). It brings table statistics, cardinality estimation, and join reordering - classic components of a relational query optimizer. The known gap is that for **small datasets or edge cases, the CBO can pick suboptimal plans**[\[7\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L160). This is common when an optimizer's cost model isn't well-calibrated; e.g., the overhead of an index or join might outweigh benefits on tiny tables, but the model might not account for that, thus picking an unnecessarily complex plan. The objective now is to **improve plan quality heuristics, especially on small-N scenarios, and establish guardrails to enable the CBO by default** in production (meaning the optimizer's decisions should be reliable enough that users don't need to manually turn it off).

**User Impact (SQ1):** Who benefits? In short, _all Geode users writing non-trivial queries_. The CBO is key to **query performance**: a bad plan can mean orders of magnitude slower query, so improving it ensures consistent performance. Specific personas: - **Graph query developers** (writing MATCH queries with complex patterns, joins, etc.) benefit from not having to manually tune or hint the execution - the optimizer will choose a good plan automatically. - **DB admins** benefit because once CBO is default-on, they can trust the DB to handle diverse workloads without knob-tuning; plus, it reduces surprises (if someone forgets to set GEODE_ENABLE_CBO, they won't accidentally run everything with the naive planner). Key use-cases unlocked: enabling CBO by default means new clusters or queries get the performance improvements (e.g., that 10× join speedup)[\[49\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L75-L79) without user action. It de-risks heavy analytical queries and multi-join patterns. Also, if we incorporate more advanced optimization (like distributed plans or multi-threaded plans), a reliable CBO is prerequisite.

**Design Options (SQ2):** Improving the CBO involves multiple possible strategies: - **Refine the Cost Model:** Adjust how costs are calculated for various operations, especially for small N. For instance, incorporate a heuristic that if a table (or intermediate result) has < X rows, treat certain plans (like nested loop joins) as cheaper regardless of what the cost formula says. This kind of hybrid rule ("if very small, do the simple thing") can avoid worst-case outliers[\[50\]](https://www.cockroachlabs.com/blog/building-cost-based-sql-optimizer/#:~:text=nested%20loop%20join%20if%20an,is%20called%20a%20heuristic%20optimizer)[\[51\]](https://www.cockroachlabs.com/blog/building-cost-based-sql-optimizer/#:~:text=minimizing%20memory%20usage). Many optimizers implement a **"fudge factor"** or special-case for small tables, since pure cost estimation can be unreliable at small scale (estimation errors percentage-wise can be huge when actual row count is tiny). - **Collect more statistics or calibrate using runtime feedback:** The known issue might be due to misestimation of cardinalities. For small data, even a slight overestimation could lead to a wrong join order. We can improve by adding **dynamic sampling** (e.g., if a table has no stats, quickly sample it) or adjusting the default assumptions. Also, implementing **plan feedback loops**: run a query, see actual vs estimated rows, and adjust stats accordingly (many modern systems do this). - **Guardrails in plan search:** We might restrict the search space for certain cases. Example: if the cost difference between two plans is within a small margin and one is much simpler, choose the simpler. Or avoid extremely bushy joins if number of tables is small, etc. - **Enabling by default criteria:** We may not want to flip the default on immediately in v0.18. Instead, one design is to implement an **automatic enable**: for queries above a certain size/complexity, use CBO, otherwise use the simpler rule-based plan. This hybrid approach ensures small queries (where overhead of CBO might not pay off and risk misestimation) use a simple plan, whereas big ones use CBO. Some databases have configuration for this threshold (Couchbase's CBO can be toggled; Oracle's optimizer can choose dynamic sampling on big queries, etc.). However, maintaining two planners is overhead. Another approach: keep one CBO but ensure it basically replicates the rule-based decisions in trivial cases. - **Plan stability:** Another improvement: ensure that the CBO doesn't produce radically different plans on each run due to small cost differences. If we found that "small datasets cause suboptimal plan", maybe the cost model's fixed startup cost parameters are off. For example, CBO might think an index scan is always better, but on 5 rows a full scan is fine and simpler. We can incorporate minimal thresholds (like never choose index if expecting to scan > say 10% of table, or always do something straightforward if table < 100 rows, as noted). - **Calibration of Stats:** Possibly the current histograms need better handling for small distributions. We might incorporate a special distribution handling - e.g., if a column has low cardinality or few rows, treat it distinctly. Also, merging rule-based insights: e.g., if a join is expected to yield 0 or 1 row (like key lookup), nested loop is great; if thousands, maybe hash join, etc. We can embed such logic by learning from other DBs (the Cockroach blog[\[52\]](https://www.cockroachlabs.com/blog/building-cost-based-sql-optimizer/#:~:text=The%20first%20approach%20is%20what,is%20called%20a%20heuristic%20optimizer) describes how their heuristic rules had to give way to cost-based, but they probably still keep some safeguards for extremes).

**Compatibility & Stack (SQ2 cont'd):** The CBO improvements mostly involve the **planner module**; they should not conflict with other subsystems (Raft, QUIC, etc.) except positively - e.g., if distributed queries (federation) are introduced, the CBO should eventually cost distribution as well. For now, focusing on single-node cost. There's no conflict with MVCC or CDC; those operate at execution/storage layer. Possibly a slight interaction with concurrency: if multi-threading is introduced, the cost model might later need to account for parallel execution costs. But that's future.

**Standards & Compliance (SQ3):** This is more about internal quality than external standards. ISO GQL doesn't mandate how queries are optimized, only the results. However, for user expectations: an "optimizer" should follow the principle of least surprise (not produce extremely inefficient plans without reason). Ensuring the CBO meets a quality bar is part of product readiness. One compliance angle: if we eventually claim ACID/OLTP performance, the optimizer should choose plans that meet transaction latency SLAs. This is indirectly ensuring compliance with performance SLAs or at least not violating any GQL semantic (which it wouldn't, since any correct plan yields correct results, just slower or faster).

**Complexity & Risk (SQ4):** - **Regression risk:** Changing the optimizer can introduce new plan choices that might actually _worsen_ some queries if we're not careful. We need extensive testing on a variety of workloads (hence benchmarks below). There's a risk of chasing improvements for a subset of queries but harming others (the classic "optimizer regression" problem). That's why we need guardrails and possibly gradually enabling features. - **Bias & small-sample issues:** On very small datasets, any statistical estimate might be inaccurate due to lack of data. There's also a phenomenon where the cost difference between two plans is negligible for small N (both are super fast), but the optimizer might arbitrarily pick one - which might not matter for speed, but could for consistency or debugging. We should aim for plan stability where differences are tiny. - **Maintenance complexity:** A cost model can become very complex, with many parameters (CPU cost, I/O cost, etc.). Overfitting to our current usage could make it brittle. Another risk: enabling the CBO by default means more people will use it, possibly uncovering cases we didn't anticipate. So we must have a way to quickly disable or adjust if a severe issue arises (maybe keep the env flag a bit longer as emergency kill-switch). - **Integration with new features:** E.g., once multi-threading exists, the cost model might need an update. If not, the CBO might not utilize parallel query even if available (not critical now, but to consider). - **Plan cache**: v0.17 mentioned a plan cache with 8% collision[\[53\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L26-L31) and 90% hit on repeated queries[\[49\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L75-L79). If we change the planner, ensure the plan cache still functions (i.e., two logically identical queries still hit cache). If we add randomness or feedback adaptation, caching might need careful keys.

**Benchmarks & Acceptance (SQ5):** We will use a mix of analytical and transactional query workloads to evaluate improvements: - **Benchmark Query Suite:** Assemble a set of queries (perhaps from LDBC, TPC-H (translated to GQL), and some typical graph patterns). Include both **small-scale** tests (tiny data sets where a perverse plan could have overhead) and **large-scale** ones (where the wrong join order could blow up CPU or memory). - **Plan Quality Metrics:** For each query in the suite: - Compare the execution time with CBO vs with the simpler rule-based planner (or vs CBO disabled). We expect CBO to be as good or better in most cases. Specifically, measure the ratio: time_with_CBO / time_without_CBO. Our aim is to avoid any major regressions (say no query should be >2× slower with CBO; ideally all are equal or faster). If any outlier exists, analyze and adjust. - Another metric is **cost estimation accuracy**: For key plan nodes, see estimated rows vs actual. We could instrument the plan to output these or use a plan EXPLAIN that includes estimates vs actual (maybe not built yet, but possibly log after execution actual cardinalities and compare). We set a goal like >85% of estimates are within a small factor (maybe within 2×) of actual on test workloads, as hinted in planning docs[\[37\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L19-L25) (the "prediction accuracy >85%" in the Next doc likely refers to cardinality estimation success). - **Small Data Cases:** Create scenarios specifically to test small dataset decisions. E.g., a tiny graph with 10 nodes and a query joining a couple of patterns. The CBO should ideally not choose any overly complex ops. Even if both rule and cost plans run <1ms, we verify it doesn't do silly things like use an external sort for 10 rows, etc. We might introspect the chosen plan (could output an EXPLAIN graph). Acceptance: On tables <100 rows, the chosen plan is basically the "obvious" one (like no partitioning or etc.). - **Stress Testing Join Orders:** We can use the **LDBC SNB BI workload** or similar which has complex multi-join queries. Compare execution with various join orders. If possible, cross-check what a known DB would do (like run equivalent on Postgres to see the join order chosen - if ours matches, it's a good sign). The aim is the CBO finds the optimal or near-optimal join order. We measure performance relative to an ideal (maybe manually tune a plan to see best-case). - **Plan Stability Over Time:** Use the queries on evolving data: load 1000 rows, then 10000, etc., and see if the optimizer smoothly changes strategy when appropriate. For example, with 1000 rows maybe nested loop is fine, but at 10000 it should switch to hash join. We ensure it does switch at some point (meaning cost model thresholds make sense). - **Default-On Safety:** Simulate being a naive user: run a variety of queries without enabling any flags (so using default settings after we potentially flip it to on). They should all run in reasonable time. This is more integration test than benchmark.

Acceptance criteria: - **Quantitative:** - At least X% of our benchmark queries run faster or equal with the improved CBO than with the old version. For example, aim 90% of queries have no regression, and maybe an average speedup of e.g. 2× on complex ones. - No query has catastrophic regression (we can define catastrophic as >5× slower). - Cardinality estimation accuracy improved (perhaps measured by mean error factor). - **Default-on gating:** We will define that before making CBO default, it must pass a soak test (run a mix of queries for some hours without issues) and meet the above no-regression condition. Possibly keep it default-off through v0.18 but encourage usage, then default-on in v0.19. The actual "exit criteria" could be: 1. No open critical bugs in optimizer (like wrong results or extreme slowness). 2. At least one successful pilot user report with CBO on in a real workload (if available). 3. Internal test coverage of common patterns (match with filters, join, aggregation) showing good performance.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** - **Stats Gathering Improvements:** The CBO relies on stats; if those aren't updated or accurate, the best cost model fails. We might need to build a background job or on-demand command to update statistics (like histograms). Possibly integrate with the data ingestion or provide ANALYZE command as Postgres does. This might be needed if small tables had no stats before (maybe default to 10 rows estimate, which could be off). - **Catalog Changes:** If we store plan hints or adjustments, might need to persist some of that (though likely not). - **Testing Tools:** Possibly implement an EXPLAIN command to output the chosen plan and cost. This isn't strictly needed to run the optimizer, but is invaluable for debugging and for users to trust (especially if default-on, they might want to see what plan is). Could be minimal (just some JSON or text tree with costs). This is in line with usual DB features and can be a side outcome of working on the optimizer. - **Integration with Federation:** Eventually, the CBO should be aware of distributed costs. If federation comes concurrently, maybe not fully integrated by v0.18 - might assume single node. But by v0.19, we might incorporate network cost in decisions (like avoid sending huge data across nodes by preferring push-down). This is a dependency in the sense that if not done, the CBO could pick a plan that is theoretically optimal on one node but terrible in distributed context (like joining two large shards at coordinator). We might mitigate by adding basic rules for distributed queries if CBO isn't distribution-aware initially (like always do local aggregation if possible, etc). - **Parallel/Concurrent Plan Support:** If multi-threaded query execution becomes available, the optimizer might need to choose whether to use parallel operators. This likely will come later, but we should structure the optimizer code to allow that extension (maybe a simple flag or cost benefit when parallelizing a scan). - **Query Cache and Optimizer toggles:** We should ensure that the plan cache keys incorporate whether CBO was on or off (so we don't reuse a plan from rule-based when CBO is on if that yields different plan - although ideally on vs off wouldn't cache same query at the same key, because the flag difference should be considered). - Possibly consider upgrading the algorithm for join enumeration beyond left-deep if beneficial, but that is maybe too advanced for now and can explode search space. For near term, left-deep is okay (though suboptimal for certain queries, but one step at a time).

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - In **v0.18**, we will keep CBO **opt-in** but heavily encourage it. Possibly change the env var default in our development builds but not in release, to get more testing. Provide in docs recommendations for users to try GEODE_ENABLE_CBO=1 on staging and report any issues. - Use feature flags or configuration to allow quick disable. Actually, we might invert the flag logic: instead of ENABLE_CBO, maybe a DISABLE_CBO to easily turn it off if something goes wrong once it's default. - For internal testing, run with CBO always on to catch regressions early. Also add tests that specifically cover both code paths (with and without) to ensure no divergence in results. - Observability: add a metric like "optimizer plan type chosen" (e.g., number of times each join type used, etc.) or at least log warnings if a cost estimate was very off (maybe too much detail for now). - Beta user program: identify some known query patterns from any friendly users and test them with CBO vs without, to build confidence. - Documentation: clearly document how to interpret EXPLAIN output, how to update stats, and if any queries are known to confuse the optimizer (with workarounds or hints if absolutely needed). - Aim to **enable by default in v0.19** if v0.18 feedback is positive. Mark that as a highlight (because it signals the system is production-ready with its optimizer). - In rollout, if after enabling by default an issue emerges, be ready to release a minor patch that flips it off or address quickly. That's why keeping the config knob is prudent until we're very stable.

### 4\. CDC Webhook Sink Hardening (Custom Headers & Reliability)

**What & Why:** Geode v0.17 introduced CDC (Change Data Capture) "external sinks" including a **Webhook sink** that POSTs change events to an HTTP endpoint[\[54\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L32-L40). It supports at-least-once delivery with retries and a dead letter queue[\[55\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L32-L38). The identified gap is the lack of **custom HTTP header support**[\[7\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L160), which many real integrations need - e.g., to include an API key (Authorization: Bearer ...), tenant ID, or any custom metadata required by the endpoint. Without this, the webhook sink is of limited use beyond trivial scenarios, as most web services secure their endpoints via headers or require specific content-type etc.

In addition, "operational hardening" suggests improving **reliability and observability** of the CDC sink: even though basic retry logic exists, we should consider features like configurable backoff strategy, maximum retry attempts, idempotency considerations, and monitoring metrics (so ops can see if the sink is falling behind or failing).

**User Impact (SQ1):** The CDC webhook is used by developers integrating Geode with downstream systems (e.g., sending graph changes to an indexing service, cache invalidation service, audit log, etc.). Personas: - **Integrators/API Developers:** They might set up Geode to call their microservice on every change. They benefit from being able to authenticate the webhook calls (via headers) and trust that the mechanism is robust (doesn't lose events, handles failures gracefully). - **Operations/DevOps:** They need to monitor that CDC is working (no huge backlog, failures visible) and be able to configure retries so as not to overload the target or the database. Key use-cases unlocked: - **Secure webhooks:** e.g., sending changes to a SaaS endpoint that requires an API key header or a signature - custom headers enable that. - **Partitioned routing:** Some advanced patterns might use headers to route events (like a multi-tenant system might include a tenant ID header for downstream). - Ensuring **idempotent delivery**: If the target expects an Idempotency-Key header to deduplicate retries[\[56\]](https://hookdeck.com/webhooks/guides/implement-webhook-idempotency#:~:text=How%20to%20Implement%20Webhook%20Idempotency,multiple%20times%2C%20ensuring%20data%20integrity)[\[57\]](https://www.moderntreasury.com/journal/webhook-idempotency#:~:text=Webhook%20Idempotency%20,which%20webhooks%20have%20already), Geode should facilitate that. - **Observability:** operations can set alerts if, say, the webhook endpoint has >N failures or events in DLQ.

**Design Options (SQ2):** - **Custom Headers Configuration:** Provide a way to specify static headers (key-value pairs) to include in every webhook request. This could be in config file (e.g., cdc.webhook.headers = "Authorization: Bearer abc123, X-Client: Geode"), or via DDL (if we treat sinks as objects in the DB metadata). A flexible approach is allow both _static_ and possibly _dynamic_ headers. Dynamic might be too advanced (like including some data from the change event in a header), but at least static ones cover most needs (auth tokens, etc.). - We should store these securely if they contain secrets. At least avoid logging them.
