We might integrate with a future secrets storage; for now, mark sensitive ones so not to log. - **Idempotency and Retries:** Each change event already has an ID (likely the transaction or sequence number). We could send an Idempotency-Key header (many APIs use this standard header for deduplication)[\[57\]](https://www.moderntreasury.com/journal/webhook-idempotency#:~:text=Webhook%20Idempotency%20,which%20webhooks%20have%20already) containing, say, the event UUID or combination of (node, LSN). This would help the receiver to ignore duplicates if a retry happens. This can be optional or always on if not harmful. Alternatively, simply documenting that the payload has an event ID that the consumer can use might suffice. But since they explicitly mention custom headers, maybe they want the user to be able to set such things. - **Backoff & Retry Config:** v0.17 uses exponential backoff with fixed limits[\[58\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L34-L38), presumably with some defaults. We should expose config for: - initial retry delay, max delay, backoff multiplier (or a few preset strategies like exponential vs linear). - maximum retry count or time-to-live for retries before DLQ. - Possibly a timeout for the HTTP call (if not already set). - Concurrent delivery control: maybe whether events are delivered sequentially or can be parallel (though sequential is safer to preserve order). - **Observability:** - Emit metrics: e.g., cdc_webhook_sent_total, cdc_webhook_failures_total, cdc_webhook_latency_ms, cdc_webhook_retries_total, cdc_webhook_dlq_size. Having these as part of Geode's metrics (maybe via JMX or an HTTP endpoint) allows ops to see if the webhook is failing often. - Logging: ensure each failure is logged with reason (HTTP status or exception), maybe with truncated response if available. - Possibly provide a simple internal endpoint or CLI to peek at DLQ events (so user can inspect what got dropped or held). - **Delivery ordering and concurrency:** If events are being retried for one update, new events might pile up. We likely already queue them. We should confirm whether out-of-order delivery is allowed or not. At-least-once might deliver out of original order if a retry is lagging. Perhaps document that possibility or ensure single-threaded dispatch to preserve order (with the downside that one stuck event blocks the pipeline). - **Alternate modes:** (just consideration) Some systems allow specifying if a 4xx from server means don't retry (client error) vs 5xx means retry. We might implement that logic: e.g., if we get a 410 Gone or 400 Bad Request, maybe it's not transient - so optionally drop to DLQ immediately. Or allow user to configure which status codes to retry on. That might be overkill now, but a simple version: retry on network errors and 5xx, but not on 2xx (obviously) or maybe not on 4xx. Actually, if an auth header is wrong (401), retrying won't fix it, so best to DLQ those after one attempt. We can follow common webhook patterns: treat 2xx as success, 3xx maybe treat as success (or follow redirect a couple times), 4xx as non-retryable (maybe except 429 too many requests which might be retried after delay), 5xx as retryable. This nuance can be implemented as sensible default behavior (and document it).

**Standards & Expectations (SQ3):** No formal ISO standard for CDC (it's an implementation detail), but common _practices_: - **Webhooks security:** OWASP guidelines suggest including HMAC signatures or auth headers rather than open endpoints. We want to allow that. For instance, Stripe webhooks include a signature header and timestamp. If our user's endpoint expects that, they likely will provide an endpoint that calculates HMAC with a secret. Possibly in future we could automatically sign payloads, but out-of-scope now. At least allow sending a secret. - **Idempotency:** The IETF has an Idempotency-Key header standard[\[59\]](https://www.ietf.org/archive/id/draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header-01.html#:~:text=The%20Idempotency,)[\[57\]](https://www.moderntreasury.com/journal/webhook-idempotency#:~:text=Webhook%20Idempotency%20,which%20webhooks%20have%20already). We should consider using it or enabling it easily, because it's increasingly used in web API design to handle retries safely. - **HTTP compliance:** Ensure we follow HTTP best practices: correct content type (likely application/json for payload - we should allow that header too if user wants a custom content-type). - **Observability standards:** Use standard metrics exposition if possible (maybe integrate with whatever metrics output we have). - **Regulatory:** If CDC might send sensitive data outside, security like encryption in transit (HTTPS is a must - we should strongly recommend using an https URL; perhaps even warn if http is used). - Also, maybe data minimization: ensure PII in payload is handled as per policies (though that's user's responsibility what to capture in CDC).

**Complexity & Risk (SQ4):** - **Misconfiguration risk:** If user supplies headers incorrectly (typos, etc.), requests may fail (e.g., auth fails). We can't fully prevent, but we should make it easy to configure properly (maybe via a structured config or not requiring them to write raw HTTP header syntax). - **Performance impact:** Each change event triggers an HTTP call. If the endpoint is slow or unreachable, and we queue many events, memory usage could spike or backlog cause issues. There's a risk of the CDC pipeline blocking the main transaction (depending on how it's implemented - hopefully it's async and decoupled so writing to DB doesn't wait on webhook). We need to ensure a backlog doesn't exhaust memory - maybe have a bounded queue and if full, either drop events or apply backpressure to producers (which might slow the DB). - **Idempotency & duplicates:** At-least-once means duplicates can happen. If the receiving system doesn't handle dedup, it could result in double processing. That's why idempotency keys help. However, implementing perfect dedup on our side is hard (we'd have to store state of delivered events). We rely on downstream or the fact that the events themselves carry unique IDs. - **Failure handling edge cases:** Suppose the endpoint is down for an extended time. Our DLQ catches events after X retries. If it comes back, do we have a mechanism to replay from DLQ? Possibly manual for now. There's risk of data loss (if events languish in DLQ and no one notices). We mitigate with metrics/alerts as said. - **Threading issues:** The webhook sending might run in a separate thread or use an async mechanism. Ensure thread safety (especially if we allow concurrent sends). - **Security:** Storing an API key header in plaintext in config could be sensitive. We might document to use environment variables or vault integration if available. At least ensure not to log it. Maybe provide an option to mark a header as secret (so not printed). - **Testing complexity:** Reproducing failure scenarios (like slow target, partial failures) requires integration test with a dummy HTTP server. - **Third-party dependencies:** If using an HTTP client library, ensure it's robust (and doesn't leak memory or block threads incorrectly). We might use a simple built-in HTTP in Zig or similar.

**Benchmarks & Tests (SQ5):** - _Functional tests:_ Simulate a webhook endpoint (e.g., a local HTTP server that records requests). Trigger some graph changes, verify that the server received matching JSON payloads with all configured headers. Include custom header with special characters to ensure formatting works. - _Auth header test:_ Use an endpoint that expects a header with a token and rejects if missing. Configure Geode with that header and verify it succeeds (HTTP 200) whereas without it would fail (we can simulate by turning the config off and expecting a 401). - _Retry logic test:_ Make the dummy endpoint respond with failure (500) a couple times then success. Check that Geode retried the correct number of times and eventually delivered once endpoint succeeded. Check that only one final delivery is done (no duplicates beyond the retried ones). - _Non-retry on 4xx test:_ Make endpoint return 400; ensure Geode does not retry infinitely (preferably moves to DLQ after perhaps 1 attempt since it's client error). This behavior needs to be decided and tested. - _Performance under load:_ Generate a burst of changes (e.g., 1000 small transactions). If endpoint is reasonably fast, ensure Geode can keep up. If endpoint is slow (simulate ~200ms per call), see if events queue up. We might measure how many events per second can the CDC sink handle with default config. This determines if we need to allow parallelism or if user should scale endpoint. Possibly we can allow configurable concurrency (like up to N parallel HTTP calls). But that complicates ordering. Probably keep single-thread for now. - _Backlog behavior:_ Simulate endpoint down (all requests fail). Observe memory usage and log warnings. The system should not crash or OOM. Possibly after queue fills, we drop events (maybe to DLQ) - we should define what happens. We might have to put a hard cap (say 100k events) then start dropping oldest to DLQ. Test that scenario to ensure no memory blow-up. - _Dead Letter Queue handling:_ Confirm events appear in DLQ after exhausting retries. Possibly provide a way to inspect them. At least verify count. If we implement DLQ as e.g. a local file or separate topic, ensure it writes correctly. Could try to manually re-ingest them for testing (not automated in system, but user might want to). - _Metrics correctness:_ If we add metrics, start a test that generates known number of events, cause some fails, then query metrics to see if counts line up (like 100 events, 5 failed, metrics show 95 successes, 5 failures). - _Thread safety:_ Hard to benchmark directly, but maybe run with sanitizers or stress concurrency by enabling parallel (if we do). - _Latency overhead:_ Measure how much overhead CDC introduces to a transaction commit. Ideally, if CDC is async, committing a transaction shouldn't wait for the HTTP call. If currently it does, that's a problem for performance. We should ideally confirm it's decoupled (perhaps it writes to an internal queue and returns, letting a background thread handle sending). If not decoupled, that's an improvement to make. We can measure commit latency with and without CDC enabled to ensure minimal difference.

Acceptance criteria: - **Correctness:** 100% of change events that should be delivered are delivered (eventually, unless target is persistently down in which case they end up in DLQ). No event is silently lost without being in DLQ or logs. - **Custom Headers:** Users can configure arbitrary headers (within a reasonable size and format) and they appear exactly in requests. Sensitive headers are not logged. - **Retries and Order:** The retry policy works as expected: transient failures lead to retries with exponential backoff (we can check timestamps between tries increase exponentially roughly). No infinite retry loops. Order is maintained for each unique partition (if single thread, then obviously maintained). If we allow some concurrency, ensure either no ordering issues or clearly document it. - **No Crash/Leak:** The CDC mechanism should handle target failures without crashing Geode or running out of memory. E.g., if target is down for 1 hour and 10,000 events occur, Geode should still be up (with events maybe queued or DLQed). - **Performance envelope:** Achieve a sustainable throughput of CDC events (like at least hundreds per second with minimal performance impact). If an endpoint can handle it, Geode shouldn't be the limiting factor (except maybe CPU for JSON serialization - which could be optimized if needed). If our tests show a significant slowdown with CDC on, we may need to optimize how we generate JSON or use batch requests. - **Observability:** All key metrics and logs are in place. For example, if we deliberately cause a failure, an operator should see log "Webhook delivery failed for event X: HTTP 500, will retry" and eventually "moved to dead letter after Y attempts". And metrics reflecting that.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** - Likely minimal external dependencies. We might use an HTTP client library (if not already present). Possibly reuse our internal HTTP code used for something (if any). - JSON serialization for the payload is presumably done (v0.17 had JSON payloads for CDC[\[54\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L32-L40)). We ensure any improvement needed (like more compact JSON or including maybe event metadata in headers vs body). - If we consider including _custom payload fields_, that's separate - but maybe someone might want to add an HMAC of payload as a header. That's advanced, likely not now unless trivial to implement a secret + HMAC field. - Integration with metrics library or logging improvements if needed. - If in future, we have a dedicated **CDC management UI or API**, not immediate but possibly plan to introspect DLQ. For now, maybe just CLI or logs.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - This is a contained feature improvement - relatively low risk to enable by default since it just adds capabilities. Custom headers will be simply not used unless configured. - We will implement and test thoroughly by v0.18 and include in release notes as a new feature "Webhook sink supports custom headers and improved retry config". - If any behavior changes (like not retrying 4xx), document them clearly so users relying on old behavior are aware (though since it was new in v0.17 and likely not heavily used yet, impact is minimal). - Provide sample usage in documentation (e.g., how to set an Authorization header, how to interpret the idempotency key, etc.). - Monitor usage or feedback after release for any edge cases (maybe someone wants dynamic header per event - could consider if requests). - Possibly mark the CDC feature as beta until it's used more, but given it's fairly straightforward, we can consider it stable. - Ensure backward compatibility: if someone upgrades from v0.17 to v0.18, their existing CDC config continues to work (just now they can add headers if desired). - Add an integration test to our CI that runs a mini HTTP server, as part of continuous tests, to guard against future regressions (like if changes break the HTTP posting). - In v0.19, we can further enhance if needed (e.g., maybe add batching of multiple events into one request as an optional optimization, but that's beyond current scope unless asked).

### 5\. Security Hardening (TDE & MFA)

**What & Why:** Security hardening focuses on **Encryption at Rest (Transparent Data Encryption, TDE)** and **Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)** for Geode. These features address different threat vectors: - **TDE** protects data files and backups from being read if an attacker gains access to disk/storage. It's often required by compliance standards (PCI DSS, HIPAA, etc.) for databases containing sensitive data[\[8\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=Transparent%20Data%20Encryption%20,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS)[\[23\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=transaction%20records%2C%20and%20personal%20identifiers,standards%2C%20including%20the%20Payment%20Card). Given Geode now has persistent storage and backups, TDE ensures that even those files are encrypted. This is crucial for cloud deployments (multi-tenant or simply to mitigate cloud provider risk) and on-prem security (physical theft of drives, etc.). - **MFA** adds an extra layer for authentication of database users (especially administrators). It mitigates credential compromise risk - even if a password is stolen, an attacker can't login without the second factor. Oracle's recent introduction of DB MFA (for 19c/23c) shows industry movement this direction[\[9\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=The%20recent%20Oracle%20Database%2023ai,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c).

**User Impact (SQ1):** - **Compliance Officers / Security Teams:** They require these features to certify the database for sensitive data. Not having TDE/MFA might block adoption in certain industries. - **DBAs/DevOps:** They benefit from easier compliance and stronger security defaults, reducing the chance of breaches. For example, an ops team can enable TDE and not worry that backup files on S3 might be accessed by an unauthorized party - they'd be encrypted. - **End Users (indirectly):** They get assurance their data is safe and access to the DB is tightly controlled.

Use-cases: - **Encryption at Rest:** A company using Geode for customer data can tick the checkbox for data encryption, often a contractual requirement. Also, if using Geode in cloud VMs or containers, TDE prevents cloud staff or other tenants from reading the raw files. - **MFA for DB access:** Particularly for interactive access (like an admin connecting with credentials), requiring MFA (e.g., a one-time code or push) can stop many intrusion attempts. It's mostly for administrative access, since app connections often use service accounts (for which MFA is less relevant). - Also, optional MFA could integrate with single sign-on (SSO) eventually, but likely out-of-scope now.

**Design Options (SQ2):** _TDE:_ Typically implemented either at database-level (DB engine encrypts pages before writing to disk) or relying on filesystem encryption. Since Geode is custom storage, we implement at engine level: - Use a **Data Encryption Key (DEK)** to encrypt the database files (WAL, snapshots, page files). That key is stored encrypted by a **Master Key**. The Master Key could be stored in an external Key Management Service (KMS) or in a local secure store. - Option 1: **Local key, passphrase protected** - e.g., user provides a passphrase or the key is stored in a file the admin secures. Simpler, but less enterprise-grade. - Option 2: **Integration with KMS (AWS KMS, HashiCorp Vault, etc.)** - the ideal is to use a managed KMS: on startup, Geode calls KMS to retrieve or decrypt the master key. Oracle TDE uses a wallet, SQL Server TDE often uses the Windows DPAPI or Azure Key Vault, etc[\[60\]](https://repost.aws/questions/QUjZrarwG_TOOoPZeGmIeVzQ/aws-kms-for-database-tde#:~:text=KMS%20cannot%20be%20used%20directly,be%20stored%20in%20AWS%20CloudHSM)[\[61\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/database/things-to-consider-when-choosing-between-oracle-tde-and-aws-kms-for-encryption-of-data-at-rest-for-amazon-rds-for-oracle/#:~:text=Things%20to%20consider%20when%20choosing,focusing%20on%20these%20key). Perhaps we can initially support AWS KMS (since many will use AWS for S3 as well) by storing the master key in KMS and retrieving it via API. If that's heavy, at least structure the design to allow plugin of key provider (in-memory, file, or KMS). - At runtime, when Geode needs to read/write data, it decrypts/encrypts using DEK. We can use standard AES (256-bit AES in XTS or GCM mode). The overhead typically is small with modern CPUs (AES-NI instructions). - We must ensure all sensitive files are covered: data pages, WAL (write-ahead log) entries, Raft log, backup files. Possibly the WAL and snapshot can be encrypted too - or snapshots maybe we can treat similarly to backups (which we plan to encrypt via SSE or if local, then by TDE). - Key rotation: We should plan for rotating the DEK periodically. Usually done by generating new DEK, re-encrypting the data, or gradually re-encrypting pages. This is complex and might be deferred, but at least allow re-encrypting new backups with a new key or manual re-encryption offline. - For now, perhaps we implement one static key per cluster that can be changed by user via full re-encryption downtime procedure.

_MFA:_ - There are a couple of ways to require MFA: 1. **DB-driven MFA**: The database itself after password auth, challenges the user for a second factor code. That implies Geode needs to support storing MFA secrets (like TOTP secret for each user or integration with a push service). 2. **Integration with external IdP**: e.g., use an OAuth/OIDC token that already required MFA. Oracle's approach uses integration with OCI IAM and authenticator apps[\[9\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=The%20recent%20Oracle%20Database%2023ai,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c) - perhaps too heavy for Geode's stage.

Given resources, a simpler approach is to support **TOTP (Time-based One Time Password)** MFA for local users: - At user creation, allow linking an MFA device (e.g., generate a QR code secret shared). - On login, if MFA enabled, Geode requires a 6-digit code from user's authenticator app. The server verifies code (using known algorithms e.g., RFC 6238). - Alternatively or additionally, support **Duo Push or WebAuthn** is more complex (need external service or hardware). Possibly out-of-scope now. - If Geode already has an MFA module placeholder[\[21\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L12-L15), maybe it intended TOTP style or pluggable. - Option: For v0.18, perhaps implement TOTP MFA for console/CLI logins. The user experience: user runs geodec login -u alice -p password and if MFA is required, they're prompted for code. The CLI then prompts and sends it. This means our protocol needs to handle an authentication flow with an extra step (like first response "MFA required"). - Another route is to integrate with PAM or OS-level which can incorporate MFA. But that's heavy and not cross-platform easily. - Considering Oracle implemented it, possibly focusing on TOTP or certificate-based (they mention certificate-based as an alternative)[\[62\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=Database%20owners%20can%20now%20opt,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c). For Geode, certificate-based could mean requiring a client TLS certificate in addition to password - that's a form of 2FA (something you have and something you know). We already have TLS for connections; we could allow mutual TLS such that the client must present a certificate (that acts like MFA if managed properly). But that might be more 'instead of password' if done fully.

So possible plan: - Implement **TDE** with a configuration like geode.encryption.enable=true and either provide a key or integrate with KMS. Possibly generate a random key and encrypt it with a user-supplied passphrase. We store that encrypted key file (like how MySQL TDE can use a master key per instance stored securely)[\[63\]](https://www.mysql.com/products/enterprise/tde.html#:~:text=MySQL%20Enterprise%20Transparent%20Data%20Encryption,rotation%20and%20storing%20historical%20keys). - Implement **MFA** for database users via TOTP: - Extend user management to have an optional MFA secret. - Provide command to enable MFA for a user (generates secret, outputs a QR code or base32 string for user to scan with Google Authenticator or similar). - On login, if user has MFA, the server sends a response "MFA code required". The client (geode CLI or driver) needs to prompt the human. For non-interactive (application logins), those accounts wouldn't have MFA enabled typically. - Validate code and complete login if correct. - Maybe allow backup codes or a way to recover if lost device, but that might be too deep. - Optionally allow integration with an external OTP provider or RADIUS for MFA if one exists. But likely not now. - Provide config whether MFA is optional or enforced for certain roles (maybe by default enforce for admin user).

**Standards & Compliance (SQ3):** - **TDE:** As mentioned, considered essential for compliance with regulations like PCI DSS (which requires cardholder data at rest encrypted)[\[23\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=transaction%20records%2C%20and%20personal%20identifiers,standards%2C%20including%20the%20Payment%20Card), HIPAA (patient data encryption), etc. Many security frameworks also demand encryption of backups (which TDE covers if backup is taken from encrypted data, or we also ensure backup process uses encryption as previously planned). Our design should align with typical TDE of other databases: AES-256, separate master key management, minimal performance impact. We can cite that TDE is "transparent" - meaning it doesn't require query changes, it's all internal. - **Key Management:** We should follow best practice of key hierarchy (master vs data key)[\[64\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=match%20at%20L277%20Furthermore%2C%20TDE,access%20to%20critical%20encrypted%20data) and principle that keys are not stored with data in plaintext. Also, consider recommending periodic key rotation (maybe at least provide guidance that user can re-encrypt offline every year etc.). - Possibly implement according to standards like NIST SP800-57 for key management (if we want to mention credible practices). - **MFA:** Align with common MFA methods (TOTP is an IETF standard algorithm). Oracle's addition of MFA in 23c indicates an emerging standard for DB access. Our solution should at least meet the basic expectation: something like TOTP or a WebAuthn device. - Provide secure handling: e.g., if storing TOTP secrets, encrypt them with a server-side key so if someone dumps system tables they can't get the secret to generate codes. That might require an internal secret though - could use the same master key used for TDE or a separate config key. - Also, ensure time synchronization issues are considered (TOTP usually allows 30s window). - We should log or audit MFA events (like user X enabled MFA, user Y login failed due invalid code, etc.) because security auditing often requires that trail.

**Complexity & Risk (SQ4):** - **TDE Performance:** Encrypting/decrypting every page and WAL record has some overhead. On modern CPUs, AES encryption can be quite fast (tens or hundreds of MB/s per core with AES-NI). The risk is some queries (especially full graph scans) become CPU-bound due to encryption. We mitigate by using efficient libraries or possibly multi-thread encryption (though disk I/O might be bottleneck anyway). We set an acceptable overhead envelope, perhaps <5-10% overhead on throughput as target, as many vendors claim for TDE[\[65\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=Due%20to%20the%20encryption%20and,tuning%20database%20settings%20and%20regularly). - **Implementation risk:** If we implement incorrectly, could corrupt data (e.g., if not handling IVs properly), or we could leave something unencrypted (like if we forgot to encrypt an ephemeral temp file or a new component's data). Thorough testing is needed. - **Key management risk:** Losing the master key means total data loss (which is by design in encryption). We must emphasize to users to back up their keys securely. Also, if KMS is unreachable (for example, if relying on AWS KMS and the network is down at startup), the DB might not start (since it can't get key). That's a trade-off: availability vs security. Usually, KMS are highly available though. But we need a plan e.g., cache the decrypted key on disk encrypted with a passphrase as fallback? That gets complicated. Alternatively, require manual intervention to re-enter key if KMS fails. - **MFA UX risk:** If not designed well, MFA could lock out DBAs. For example, if the only admin enabled MFA and then lost device, they could be locked out. We might need a backdoor procedure (like start server in single-user mode to disable MFA, akin to some systems). - **Integration with existing authentication:** If Geode supports multiple auth methods (LDAP, etc.), how MFA fits? Perhaps for local password auth only. If LDAP is used, maybe rely on LDAP's MFA if any (so Geode might not enforce on those). - **Testing difficulty:** For TDE, simulating key loss to ensure it indeed denies access and see performance overhead at scale. For MFA, testing interactive flows in automated tests (maybe doable with CLI). - **Interplay with backup/restore:** If a backup is taken from an encrypted DB, presumably it's still encrypted. If someone tries to restore it, they'll need the key. The restore process must prompt or have access to keys. If KMS-based, ensure the new instance can fetch from KMS using proper credentials. A risk is someone might restore to a different environment where KMS key ID differs. We might want an option to re-encrypt with new key on restore if needed. - **Partial implementation risk:** Possibly we implement TDE for data, but if logs or certain caches remain plaintext on disk, that could be a loophole. We should be thorough: ephemeral files or swap not much under our control, but maybe mention to use encrypted swap or memory lockdown if super secure environment. - **MFA adoption risk:** DBAs may find MFA a hassle and not use it unless required. That's fine as long as optional. If they do use, it's another thing that can cause support issues (lost device, time drift). - **Downtime for enabling TDE:** Likely enabling TDE on an existing database might require rewriting data (unless the implementation can encrypt in place live). Possibly, if from start it's encrypted, fine. If enabling later, maybe do it backup-restore style. We should clarify how to enable it (maybe only at database creation or require full export/import to turn on encryption). - **Complexity:** TDE touches low-level IO code and buffer management - need to be careful not to double-encrypt or skip encryption in some paths. Also random vs deterministic encryption choices (we should use non-deterministic encryption for stronger security, meaning you can't detect patterns from cipher text). - **Memory usage:** Encrypted pages might not compress as well in memory, but that's minor. Also, keys management in memory - ensure keys are kept in memory only as needed (but practically the DEK will always be in memory for use; we can at least avoid swapping it out by pinning memory if possible).

**Benchmarks & Criteria (SQ5):** - **Encryption Overhead Test:** Benchmark read/write throughput on a dataset with TDE on vs off. For example, measure transaction rate or query scan speed on a moderately large graph. We set a threshold: overhead should ideally be <10%. If it's more, check if using AES hardware, if can optimize block sizes, etc. Also test bulk operations like backup: how much slower to produce an encrypted backup vs a plaintext one (expected small overhead). - **Correctness under TDE:** Insert known data, flush to disk, then directly read the disk file with an external tool to ensure it's not plaintext (i.e., cannot find the string). Then restart DB, query data to ensure it reads back correctly (decryption works). - **Restart with wrong key:** Try to start DB with an incorrect master key (simulate a key rotation gone wrong). It should fail to open (and not corrupt anything, just denial). - **Key rotation test (if supported):** If we allow rekey, test re-encrypt process yields same data accessible. - **Multi-node scenario:** If Geode cluster has multiple nodes each with encryption, ensure consistency (should be fine, each node encrypts its own data with maybe the same cluster key). - **Backup/Restore with encryption:** Backup an encrypted DB to S3 (the data in backup is encrypted due to TDE). Then restore to a new instance with the same key: verify data intact. Try restore with different key (should fail or produce gibberish - likely fail decryption). - **MFA functional tests:** - Enable MFA for a user, attempt login with correct password but no code -> should be prompted (in test, maybe simulate by providing code in second step). - Test login with correct code vs wrong code (3 tries with wrong code should fail). - Test disabling MFA or using a fallback admin account if available. - Time skew: check that code that's slightly old/new might be accepted (some tolerance by design, often ±30s). - Ensure that service accounts (if we have the concept) can still work without MFA (like connections from application with stored credentials should not require MFA). - If mutual TLS is considered as MFA alternative, test that scenario (client with certificate can login without additional code, if that's how we implement). - **MFA performance:** Not a big issue, only on login. But maybe measure if enabling it adds any overhead to connection establishment (should be negligible). - **Usability:** Possibly test that the MFA enrollment process (exposing secret QR code) works by scanning with an authenticator and verifying the code produced. Hard to do automated, but maybe using a TOTP library we can simulate what the authenticator would generate from the secret to verify our server side uses same algorithm.

Acceptance criteria: - **TDE:** - Data files, logs, backups are encrypted such that unauthorized access to them yields no useful information (strong encryption). - All tests of reading/writing with correct keys succeed, with wrong key fail - ensuring security works as intended. - Performance overhead is within acceptable range (document if any small overhead). - The feature is transparent: aside from a slight startup cost for key loading and slight performance, nothing changes in how queries or transactions behave. - Key management: keys can be changed or configured easily, with clear instructions; secrets are not exposed in logs; if using KMS, proper integration (tested maybe with a stub or actual KMS with minimal cost, e.g., a developer can simulate KMS by using a local encryption). - Conforms to security best practices: e.g., uses AES-256 in a secure mode (like AES-GCM or AES-CBC with proper IVs). We should avoid developing our own crypto beyond using known libraries or primitives. If possible, use something like libsodium or OpenSSL. - **MFA:** - Usability: Admin can enable it and use it without undue friction. E.g., documentation how to use Google Authenticator or similar. - Only authorized users (with second factor) can login; an attacker with just password cannot. - It's optional per user (so backward compatibility - existing users not forced to have it). - No major bugs like code reuse (one code works twice within the window maybe or after login code not invalidated - TOTP by nature changes so fine). - The system handles edge cases: e.g., if time on server is off, mention requirement to sync time. Possibly an acceptance criterion is instructing to use NTP. - The second factor can be required only for interactive/CLI access. For app connections, perhaps those use a different auth mechanism. - **Audit & Logging:** For both features, ensure relevant events are logged (e.g., "TDE enabled" at start, "MFA enabled for userX", "Failed login for userX due bad MFA code"). This helps security audits.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** - **Crypto library:** Possibly integrate a proven library for encryption (Zig might have built-in or use something like BoringSSL via FFI). We need AES and a secure random source for IVs. Also need HMAC-SHA256 for TOTP and possibly for integrity of encrypted pages (some systems also use cryptographic page checksums to detect tampering). - **Key storage:** If integrating with AWS KMS, need AWS SDK or use their REST API. Might postpone direct KMS integration, instead require the admin to provide a decrypted key at startup (like via environment variable or file). Simpler route: user supplies a passphrase via console at server startup which decrypts the master key (this is what Oracle does if no HSM: it requires opening the wallet with a password). - **User management enhancements:** Geode has auth modules (maybe just basic local accounts or can integrate to external). The MFA addition might require adding a field in user records for the MFA secret. If using local file or internal catalog for users, modify that. Ensure to store secret hashed or encrypted (maybe using the same master key). - **Client changes:** The CLI or drivers may need to handle MFA challenge. For instance, the JDBC driver or Geode CLI protocol should be extended to allow an extra step. We'll implement at least in the CLI (geodec). If any language driver exists, document that they need to handle an MFA-required error code by prompting user. - **Testing harness:** possibly use an OTP library to simulate authenticator. Python has pyotp, maybe Zig has one or we can implement TOTP easily (it's small - basically HMAC-SHA1 on time steps). - **Documentation updates** and maybe a small tool to generate QR code (for user to scan easily). Could just instruct to use an online QR generator or print base32 string.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - Likely introduce in v0.19 (since these might be larger changes, might come slightly later than v0.18 which focuses on dist queries and backups). But if schedule allows, we could have preliminary TDE in v0.18 behind a flag (off by default). - For TDE: Because enabling it on an existing DB may require re-encryption, we might tie it to creation (like if you want encryption, enable it before storing data). Provide migration steps for existing data (maybe take backup, wipe data, enable encryption, restore backup - effectively re-encrypting in the process). - Provide thorough documentation and emphasize backing up keys. Possibly provide a geode-keytool to manage keys (like generate, rotate). - Beta test: It's hard to test security features in production without actually trusting them, but we can perhaps get a security review (maybe have an external security engineer evaluate our TDE implementation). - In release notes, mark these features as **experimental security features** initially, suggesting to test in non-prod. As confidence builds, remove experimental tag. - Plan for compliance: possibly get a third-party audit or certification if needed (out of scope for development tasks, but mention it if relevant to stakeholders). - If any issues (like performance or bugs) found by users, we can suggest temporary workaround (e.g., disable TDE, or use OS-level encryption as fallback if TDE not stable - but hope it is). - For MFA: ensure there's a failsafe to disable MFA in case of emergency (like if all admin locked out, maybe a server command-line option to bypass auth just for one session, similar to recovery mode). - This obviously is sensitive so we'll carefully gate that (maybe only run with physical access or something). - Observability: include an entry in our system status or metrics that encryption is enabled (so ops can verify at glance that a database is encrypted, and not accidentally running unencrypted).

### 6\. Full-Text Search (FTS) Enhancements (Phrase Queries, Highlighting, Ranking Controls)

**What & Why:** Geode's FTS currently offers basic tokenization and BM25 scoring for text properties, but misses key features: - **Phrase queries:** The ability to search for exact phrases "like this" in text, not just independent words. This requires indexing the positions of words to check that certain words are adjacent in correct order. Without it, searching for multi-word terms could yield many irrelevant results (words scattered). - **Proximity and phrase operators:** e.g., find words within N words of each other, or exact sequences. - **Highlighting:** Returning snippets of text with the query terms highlighted, so users can see context of matches (common in search UIs). - **Ranking controls:** BM25 is implemented, but perhaps no way to tweak (some systems let boosting certain fields or adjusting BM25 parameters). Or supporting alternative ranking algorithms if needed, or at least the ability to weight terms. - Possibly also synonyms or language-specific analysis beyond current scope, but the prompt specifically mentions phrase, highlighting, scoring controls and "language/locale coverage" in acceptance. This implies we should consider how to support different languages (like stemming rules for languages, or at least ensure we can configure analyzers per language).

**User Impact (SQ1):** - **End users of applications that use Geode's text search** will see improved search relevance and usability. E.g., in a knowledge graph, searching "New York Times" as a phrase vs just any occurrence of 'New', 'York', 'Times'. - **Developers** building search features on Geode need these to implement user-friendly search bars. Without phrase search, they might have to integrate an external search engine (Elasticsearch, etc.), which is heavy. So providing it in Geode simplifies their stack. - **Analysts** might use phrase search for precise text filters in their graph queries (like find nodes where description contains "climate change" exact phrase). - **Locale support** matters for global users - e.g., searching Chinese requires different tokenization than English, etc. Or at least the ability to handle accent insensitivity, etc.

Use-cases: - Searching quotes or exact names in text fields. - Building a simple full-text search query UI where highlighting the matching terms in results is expected (like how search engines highlight). - Adjusting search ranking when default BM25 doesn't fit (e.g., boosting recent documents or certain fields like title over body). - Multi-lingual datasets where one might need language-specific analysis (maybe outside immediate scope but mentionable as future).

**Design Options (SQ2):** - **Phrase query implementation:** Typically, phrase search uses an inverted index that stores **position information** for each term in each document (i.e., the term's positions in the text). If Geode's FTS index currently only stores term frequencies, we need to extend it to store positions (and possibly start positions to allow highlighting snippet extraction). - Option 1: Adapt the current index structure to include positions. If the index is built in-house, we add a list of positions for each term-posting (like classic Lucene does). - Option 2: Integrate an existing search library (like using Lucene's analyzers and index, or something like tantivy in Rust or search algorithms from SurrealDB or others) - might be heavy. But SurrealDB boasts integrated FTS features including highlighting[\[11\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=,tags%20or%20otherwise%20emphasizing%20them)[\[66\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=,highlight%20matched%20terms%20in%20queries). Since Surreal is open source, potentially glean approach: they define analyzers, build index with BM25 and highlights. Possibly we can follow a similar approach, or even reuse some code logic (license permitting). - We'll likely keep it in-house for now due to dependencies. So plan: modify our index builder to record positions (maybe as delta-encoded ints for efficiency). - **Highlighting:** Once we have positions and have identified which documents match (and where within them), we can produce highlight snippets. Approaches: - On query, after finding a doc matches, fetch the stored text (if we have it stored or the original text is accessible from the node) and then use the positions of query terms to extract context. - For efficiency, some systems store a separate structure (like a vector of term positions with offsets) or even store a pre-computed small snippet for each term. But simpler: we can just store the text itself (which we do in the node property anyway) and when needed, do an algorithm: find first occurrence of any query term, take X characters around it, mark it up. Possibly do this for each term or first few occurrences. - We should allow the user to request highlighting or not (because it adds overhead). Could be an option in the query like a function: something like RETURN highlight(text, "search terms") as Surreal does with a function returning highlighted text[\[67\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=Querying%20Full). - Implementation can mark up with &lt;em&gt; or some markers and then the UI can style it. Or return start-end indices of matches. - Keep it simple: maybe wrap with &lt;mark&gt; or **some marker** (the question uses phrase "highlighting, scoring controls", expecting a UI concept likely). - **Ranking controls:** - Expose the ability to tune BM25 parameters (k1, b) for advanced users or set globally. For example, BM25's b parameter (field length normalization) could be tuned if needed for certain data. We might not surface that to query level (complicates query syntax), maybe as config or not at all in first iteration. - More likely, allow per-field or per-match boosting. E.g., if a node has fields title and body, user may want matches in title to score higher. If not already, we should allow indexing multiple fields and combining scores (like linear combination). - We might add query syntax like MATCH (n) WHERE TEXT_SEARCH(n.title, 'foo') \* 3 + TEXT_SEARCH(n.body, 'foo') > 0 i.e., manual boosting. Or a simpler variant: allow the search function to take a weight per field. - Possibly an easier approach: if user wants different weighting, they might create separate indexes per field and query them combined. But maybe provide built-in "boost field" param. - At minimum, ensure the developer can retrieve the raw BM25 score if needed (maybe for debugging or advanced sorting). - Also consider adding support for **prefix or wildcard** queries if not present (maybe out-of-scope unless trivial). - **Language analyzers:** Possibly not explicitly asked for, but "language/locale coverage" suggests we should at least consider adding support for non-English text. This could mean: - Stemming for other languages (maybe integrate Snowball stemmers if license allows). - Or at least allow the analyzer to be configured to a certain language (so it uses appropriate stopwords and stemming rules). - If we have infrastructure to define analyzers (like Surreal does[\[68\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=,TOKENIZERS%20class%20FILTERS%20lowercase%2C%20ascii)), we could allow user to define one with particular filters. Possibly too heavy to implement from scratch now, but maybe we can provide 2-3 built-in analyzer modes: "simple" (English), "none" (no stemming, just exact), and maybe "keyword" (no tokenize). - Perhaps plan to integrate something like ICU or Lucene analysis for robust multi-lingual support later, but mention that as next steps.

**Standards & Expectations (SQ3):** - **ISO GQL** might not define full-text explicitly (there might be something in GQL for text search?). Probably not standardized, likely vendor extension. So we have freedom, but we should aim for standard behavior akin to popular search engines. - **User expectations:** are set by systems like Elastic, Solr, Neo4j's full-text (Neo4j does have a full-text index feature in recent versions). - Neo4j's full-text uses Lucene under the hood and supports boosting and phrase queries via Lucene query syntax. - We may not expose that level of query language complexity yet, but perhaps a minimal phrase search by quoting terms. - Perhaps allow double quotes in our search function to indicate phrase, like TEXT_SEARCH(n.content, '"graph database"') could search phrase "graph database". - **Highlighting format:** Many search APIs return highlights in a separate structure or with &lt;em&gt; tags. A common approach is to wrap matches in &lt;em&gt; or some marker in the returned text snippet[\[69\]](https://docs.opensearch.org/latest/search-plugins/searching-data/highlight/#:~:text=Highlight%20query%20matches%20,tag). We should ensure whatever we do is clearly documented and doesn't break JSON (if returning as JSON). - **Relevance scoring**: Provide scores possibly in results so user can see order rationale, or at least allow ordering by score. Could deliver a normalized score if needed. It's okay if we initially just use BM25 and perhaps allow custom boosting multipliers. - **Performance:** The enhancements should maintain search performance near current levels. Phrase queries are a bit heavier because they need to verify positions match, but with good index (positions) it's still fast (just intersect position lists). We should aim that even with phrase queries, search is sub-second for typical text lengths (a few KB doc). - **Accuracy:** Ensure phrase search only returns true positives (should not mistakenly label docs as containing phrase when terms are separate). - Possibly also consider **UTF-8 and case folding**: ensure highlighting works with Unicode (not breaking multibyte), and search by default probably case-insensitive (common expectation). - If supporting multiple languages, ensure we do no harm to current (English) analysis. Perhaps not fully tackling it now, but at least mention that we plan to incorporate standard analyzers.

**Complexity & Risk (SQ4):** - **Index size & build complexity:** Storing positions will increase index size and slightly slow indexing. We need to ensure memory/disk for index is manageable. If text fields are large and many words, storing positions for each can be heavy. But it's a known trade-off. Could consider making it optional (like user can choose to index with positions or not). However, phrase search is considered core enough that we might always do it. - If highlight requires storing original text or large parts in memory, could be heavy. But since data is stored in DB anyway, we can retrieve it from there rather than duplicating in index. - **Complex search queries:** Combining phrase and word queries might be needed. E.g., search for "climate change" and "policy" (so phrase plus a single word). We should support such combos (maybe our search function can parse a query string or we require explicit logic in query). - We might need to implement a mini query parser for search input to handle quotes as phrase delimiters, etc. Or we could force the user to use a different function for phrase search explicitly. But user-friendly would parse quotes. - **Highlight correctness:** Multi-term queries need careful highlighting: we should highlight all terms. If phrase query is used, highlight that phrase in one piece or each word? Probably highlight each matching term or phrase segment as they appear. - If text is very long, constructing highlight snippet of optimal length is non-trivial (like showing enough context but not too long). We could do something simple like take 20 chars before and after each term. If multiple terms far apart, either produce multiple snippets or the first occurrence only. Many search engines give a few snippets separated by "…". That's nice but complexity not to overdo. - **Ranking adjustments risk:** If we allow manual boosting, user might misuse and get weird results. That's on them though. We must ensure boosting doesn't break underlying logic (like negative or zero values handle correctly). - **Testing complexity:** We need to test queries in multiple scenarios (with punctuation, multiple languages possibly). Could be a lot, but manageable with smaller texts. - **Integration with query language:** if we add new syntax or functions for phrase queries or highlights, ensure it doesn't conflict with GQL grammar. Possibly treat search string as a single argument string with internal quotes which might need escaping in GQL. For example: TEXT_SEARCH(n.text, '"foo bar" baz') to find phrase "foo bar" and word baz. That string literal contains double quotes which need to be escaped or single quotes around the whole thing maybe: TEXT_SEARCH(n.text, '"foo bar" baz') might be fine if outer is single quotes and inner double are just part of string. If GQL uses single quotes for strings, this could work. - Also, ensure highlight output formatting doesn't break JSON output if returning as a raw string with HTML tags - but since we are not strictly returning JSON (the client might convert to JSON), maybe fine. If needed, we can JSON-escape it. Or deliver highlights as separate field with offsets. - **Performance risk:** phrase queries can degrade performance if not optimized. But with proper index, intersection of postings with positions is pretty fast. The worst-case is a phrase of common words, which might generate many position comparisons. We can mitigate by skipping results that cannot possibly match early (like if doc doesn't have both words near each other). - **Memory usage:** Possibly minimal extra overhead for positions, should be okay if implemented well.

**Benchmarks & Tests (SQ5):** - **Functional tests:** Create sample documents: - "Alice loves Bob" and search phrase "loves Bob" (should match), - search phrase "Bob loves" (should not match since order differs), - search separate words "loves Bob" (not phrase, should match because both words present in doc). - Multi-occurrence doc like "foo bar ... foo bar" and phrase "foo bar" should highlight both occurrences, etc. - Text with punctuation: ensure phrase spanning punctuation works appropriately or at least is well-defined (maybe punctuation breaks terms). - Case sensitivity: search "New York" vs "new york" expecting case-insensitive match (if our index lowercases tokens). - Non-Latin: if possible, a test with e.g. "mañana" containing accent and searching "manana" if we plan to ignore diacritics (if our analyzer does ascii fold). - Multi-language (if we allow it): maybe not immediate, but at least ensure it handles Unicode without crashing. - **Highlighting tests:** - The returned highlighted string actually contains the query terms wrapped in expected markers (like &lt;em&gt;). - If multiple matches, are they concatenated with ellipses properly? - If highlight function returns as separate field, check it corresponds to the content. - Edge: term at beginning or end of text (snipper logic should handle not having prefix/suffix). - Overlapping terms (e.g., query has "foo" and "foobar" as terms, and text has "foobar", ensure highlight correctly for both? That's tricky, maybe no one will search such simultaneously). - **Ranking tests:** - Ensure a doc with query terms in title vs another doc only in body, if we implement boosting, that one with title gets higher score if configured so. - Without boosting, test that our BM25 scoring roughly aligns with expected: e.g., if a term appears twice in doc A and once in doc B, doc A should score higher (assuming similar lengths). - If we allow global parameter tuning, try adjusting and see effect (maybe not in query time, maybe config and re-index). - **Performance tests:** - Index build time with and without positions (should check it's not drastically slower). - Query latency for phrase vs normal on a dataset of certain size. Eg, index 10k docs (with say average 100 words each). Then measure 1) single term query throughput, 2) two-term phrase vs just two-term AND query. Phrase will be a bit slower, but target under, say, 50ms for typical queries on moderate data (depending on hardware). We should ensure it's at least within an order of magnitude of normal term query performance. - Memory usage: measure index size increase from positions (maybe double, acceptable). - Also measure highlight extraction overhead: how much does returning highlight slow things if text is large. If heavy, maybe we advise limiting snippet length. - **Accuracy & recall tests:** Use known dataset e.g., a set of sentences, and known queries with expected results, to ensure we don't miss any matches or get false matches: - Specifically test phrase vs non-phrase difference (only return exact phrase matches). - If we implement proximity (maybe not explicitly, but phrase is proximity=0 difference), test perhaps an extension: like phrase with a wildcard or so, maybe not for now. - **Stability:** If user queries with weird input like quotes not closed, how do we handle? Possibly just treat as literal quotes if not properly paired or throw error. We should define how robust the mini parser is. - Possibly simplest: if user wants phrase, they should wrap in quotes properly. If they don't, we just do AND logic between terms. If unbalanced quotes, we could either treat quote as normal char or error. Probably error or ignore trailing quote.

Acceptance criteria: - **Functional correctness:** phrase query results exactly match expectations (no false positives, no misses for actual phrase occurrences). - **Highlight utility:** The highlight output contains the needed context with clearly marked terms. It should improve user experience by showing relevant snippet(s). We can define acceptance that highlight contains the query terms and is not longer than, say, 200 characters (to avoid huge output) by default. Possibly allow config for snippet length. - **Ranking consistency:** If two documents differ in term frequency or field presence, the scoring reflects that in sorted order. (We might test by sorting results by score and checking the ranking looks reasonable). - **No regressions to basic search:** Single-term search still works as before, performance not significantly worse by adding positions indexing (maybe slight but in tolerance). - **Localization plan:** Possibly acceptance if we have at least basic support for non-English by allowing custom analyzers. If not implemented fully, mention how we'd approach it. Perhaps minimum: not crashing on Unicode and splitting on whitespace as default (not great for languages without spaces, but that could be a known limitation until we have analyzers). - **User API clarity:** If using TEXT_SEARCH() or similar function, ensure usage documented, including how to specify phrase queries (e.g., inside the string with quotes) and how to get highlights (maybe via a separate function or optional arg). - **Integration test with SurrealDB docs (for inspiration) shows our approach is aligned:** For example, Surreal allows search::highlight and search::score in queries[\[67\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=Querying%20Full). If we implement something similar (maybe HIGHLIGHT(text_field) as a function after doing search, and SCORE() to get the BM25 score), verify they output meaningful data.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** - **Text analysis library:** If we want advanced analyzers, we might incorporate something like the Lucene analyzers or Snowball for stemming. If licensing is okay (Lucene is Apache licensed, so likely fine). Or find a pure Zig implementation of a few stemmers. Alternatively, skip advanced analysis for now and stick to basic lowercase, punctuation removal, maybe English stopwords. But if "language/locale coverage" is an acceptance point, perhaps at least allow user to specify a custom stopword list or minimal configuration. - **Index storage:** If current index is memory-only or stored on disk, ensure adding positions doesn't break format. Need to consider how index persists (maybe it's built at startup from data or persisted? Likely persisted for quick startup). - **If index is persisted, versioning:** if we change format (to add positions), old indexes might need rebuild or upgrade. We should handle an upgrade path (maybe simplest: detect old index version and rebuild it on startup of new version, since index can be derived from base data). - **Query parser adjustments:** for interpreting quotes or special search syntax. Could implement a small parser or simply scan for quotes manually. Surreal possibly just takes quotes literally from the example in docs. Or they have separate function for phrase vs normal. - **Integration with query execution:** TEXT_SEARCH(node, query) likely returns a boolean or score. Currently maybe it returns boolean if match or we do WHERE TEXT_SEARCH(...). If we want to sort by relevance, we might instead have RETURN n, TEXT_SCORE(n) as score ORDER BY score. Perhaps we implement a function TEXT_SCORE accessible after a text search filter to get last computed score. - Or simpler: TEXT_SEARCH could return a score (or 0 if not matching). Then user can do WHERE TEXT_SEARCH(...) > 0 ORDER BY TEXT_SEARCH(...) DESC. That may double compute though, unless we cache result per row. Might need to optimize or provide separate functions for filter vs scoring. - We might also revisit how multi-field search is done. If it's not easy now, maybe out-of-scope but at least allow indexing multiple fields separately. - Possibly need to modify CANARY tests or spec docs if any to reflect these features.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - Introduce these improvements likely in v0.19 (since they are less critical than distributed queries/backups for correctness, but important for usability). The question's "post-review gaps" included FTS improvements as an item, but probably lower priority than earlier items (the list had FTS at #6). - These can be enabled by default as they don't break existing usage (just add new capabilities). If index format changes, document that upgrade will rebuild text indexes (with potential temporary performance cost). - Provide documentation with examples, how to use phrase (with quotes), how to use highlight function, etc. - Possibly label as "beta" if not fully tested in all languages, but I suspect it's fine to call it GA if tests pass. - Encourage feedback from users who have text-heavy use cases - maybe some early adopters can try it and see if relevance is good, or if they need additional features like fuzzy search (not mentioned but something to consider next maybe). - If any known limitations (like no proper Chinese segmentation), list them and suggest using external search for those cases temporarily. - Observability: Not much needed here beyond maybe some stats on index size or query times, which can be seen via profiling if needed. Perhaps just ensure if a text search query is slow or heavy, we can identify it (like by EXPLAIN showing plan with text index usage). - Ensure these changes pass our internal evidence-based checks (the canary system might have requirement for phrase search to meet certain behavior). - If highlight or scoring function is complex, thoroughly unit test them.

### 7\. Concurrency & Throughput (Multi-threaded Execution)

**What & Why:** Geode's current server is largely single-threaded for query processing[\[22\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L14-L15), which limits throughput on multi-core machines and means one slow query can block others (aside from network handling). Adding **multi-threaded execution** means: - Allowing multiple queries to run in parallel on different threads (intra-transaction concurrency), increasing throughput for concurrent clients. - Possibly parallelizing parts of a single query across threads (intra-query parallelism), which could speed up heavy analytic queries by using multiple cores (like how TiDB or Oracle can do parallel query). - Also improved scheduling and fairness - to ensure one long query doesn't monopolize CPU while others wait.

**User Impact (SQ1):** - **Throughput-critical deployments** (many concurrent queries) see linear scale improvements with cores - e.g., on a 8-core machine, multi-threading could nearly 8× the transactions per second if I/O isn't bottleneck. This benefits OLTP scenarios with many short transactions. - **Long-running analytic queries** can complete faster by splitting the work (like scanning different parts of graph or evaluating independent parts of a plan in parallel). This benefits analysts and complex report generation. - For DBAs, it reduces need to scale by adding more machines for CPU if one machine's cores were underutilized - they can now use those cores effectively. - It addresses fairness: currently, if one query loops in execution, others might starve. With multi-threading, we can schedule other queries on other cores (given threads). - Geode's competitor databases (even those that historically were single-thread per query like MySQL) now often have options for parallel execution (Postgres has parallel query since v10, etc.). Not having this would be a performance disadvantage.

**Design Options (SQ2):** - **Multi-threaded Query Scheduling:** Introduce a thread pool for query execution tasks. There are a couple of models: 1. **One thread per session/connection**: allow the server to spawn or allocate one OS thread per active query. This is simpler but can have overhead if there are many connections (not great scaling beyond hundreds of threads). 2. **Task pool with cooperative scheduling**: break queries into smaller tasks that can run on a thread pool, using something like a work-stealing scheduler or an async runtime. This is more complex but scales better (like Go routines or using Zig's async/await could help).

Initially, to keep it manageable, we might do a hybrid: allow each connection to be handled by a thread from a pool (so concurrency on connection level, which is already a big step). That means if 10 clients send queries, up to 10 threads can execute concurrently. For intra-query (like parallelizing a single query's operations), that might come later. However, the question explicitly notes "multi-threaded execution paths in the engine, scheduling, and fairness", which implies both inter- and intra-query concurrency considerations. - **Engine changes:** To allow multi-threaded operation, we must ensure thread safety of shared data: - The storage engine (MVCC) likely can handle concurrent transactions if properly locked or atomic. If not, we need locks around critical structures (like the in-memory graph, indexes, etc.). Already using MVCC suggests multiple transactions could be isolated, but they may still use common structures requiring synchronization. We must audit for any global state (caches, etc.) that could have race conditions. - If going stepwise: first, allow only concurrent read transactions (which should be easier if MVCC snapshot). Then gradually allow writes concurrently with reads (with 2PL or MVCC handling). Possibly writes might still be serialized in current design given single-thread, but aim to allow at least one writer and multiple readers simultaneously (common design like readers don't block readers or maybe even readers don't block writer if snapshot, but writer might block other writers in same data). - **Locking**: Introduce fine-grained locks or atomic operations for structures like the page cache or memory manager. Might use Zig's atomic if available for reference counts, etc. Use appropriate concurrency primitives to avoid big bottlenecks. - **Query scheduling & fairness:** - Implement a basic scheduler that can preempt or yield tasks if needed. Possibly adopt a simple approach where each query runs to completion on a thread, but if we want fairness, maybe break execution into units (like processing a certain number of operations then yield). - Alternatively, if using OS threads, fairness is partly by OS scheduler. But if one thread is CPU-bound and others also CPU-bound, OS will time-slice fairly. If one is I/O-bound and releases CPU, others can use it. - Possibly the fairness issue mentioned might be more about not letting background tasks (like maintenance or heavy analytics) starve interactive queries. We could address that by priority scheduling or query time-slicing. - Perhaps incorporate a concept of query priority or at least ensure that internal tasks (like long graph algorithm) check for interrupts or yield periodically so that other queries can interleave. For example, if doing BFS in a huge graph, break it into steps and allow a check if another request came in that needs CPU. - **Work stealing** or a global pool might come in if we do fine-grained tasks. Many modern systems use that to maximize CPU usage and minimize contention (e.g., one global pool of say #cores threads, they pick up tasks). - **Compatibility with Raft & network:** We must ensure that cluster operations (like Raft heartbeat) run in separate threads too (often already the case, likely v0.17 had a separate thread for Raft events, or we should do that so that heavy query doesn't delay Raft ticks). - **Alternative model: Async I/O**: Zig has async capability. We could theoretically keep single OS thread but use async to handle multiple queries concurrently in an event loop (like Node.js style). But that means rewriting execution in asynchronous style to yield often, which is complex and doesn't utilize multiple cores easily (just interleaves tasks on one core, unless using multiple event loops on threads). - Given hardware trends, using multiple OS threads is the straightforward approach for CPU-bound tasks (which queries often are). - **Intra-query parallel**: Possibly not for first iteration except maybe trivial parallelism like if a query has independent parts (like a UNION of two subqueries, run them in parallel threads then merge). Or scanning multiple partitions concurrently if data is partitioned (ties to federation maybe: each shard scanning concurrently in distributed, which we already plan inherently does multi-thread across nodes). - If within one node for a single query, maybe skip for now, as cost-based planning for parallel ops adds complexity. We can label that as future extension but not immediate. - So likely deliver concurrency in v0.18 as multi-session concurrency (multiple queries at once), and maybe by v0.19 begin exploring splitting certain operations across threads (like parallel graph algorithm steps etc.).

**Standards & Comparators (SQ3):** - No direct ISO requirement on concurrency, but ACID properties mean our concurrency control must maintain isolation (the "I" in ACID). We should ensure that multi-threaded execution still preserves the transaction isolation level (I believe Geode uses snapshot isolation from earlier context[\[70\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L17-L20)). So more threads, but each transaction sees a consistent snapshot, and writes are correctly locked or sequenced. - Other systems: - **TiDB** and others highlight multi-threaded execution as a performance boon[\[13\]](https://tidb-blog.vercel.app/posts/the-most-underrated-tidb-feature#:~:text=1,when%20comparing%20MySQL%20to%20TiDB). TiDB runs each query using many threads (storage engine threads, coprocessor threads, etc.). They overcame the single-thread limit of MySQL. - **Neo4j** in its community edition historically was mostly single-thread per query as well, but enterprise allowed some parallel operations. We should at least match base capabilities where multiple queries can run concurrently (Neo4j definitely can handle multiple queries concurrently, it's multi-threaded, just each single query isn't parallel by default). - **Postgres** uses one process per connection which effectively is multi-threaded concurrency (via multi-process). They introduced parallel query in v10 for certain operations. - So we won't be out of step providing multi-thread concurrency, it's expected. - **Fair scheduling**: We should avoid starvation. Possibly consider a query time quota. At simplest, if each query thread has equal OS priority, OS should handle fairness decently. But in a DB, a CPU-heavy query could still saturate a core and others waiting if core count is limited. If tasks exceed cores, OS time slices anyway, so fairness is given by OS. If we want more control, we might implement our own cooperative scheduler to ensure even distribution. That might be overkill for now. We might rely on OS scheduling but monitor for any threads running too long and perhaps log or allow cancellation. - **Cancellation**: This is related - if a query is long running, user may cancel it. Multi-threading requires we implement safe cancellation points. This is more of a quality-of-life improvement. Possibly out-of-scope, but mention because fairness could include the ability to kill a runaway query. - **Locking correctness**: ensure no data races. Follow standard concurrency control patterns. Use atomic for simple counters, use locks for structures. Possibly use read-write locks for resources where multiple reads are fine concurrently but exclusive for writes (to maximize parallel reads).

**Complexity & Risk (SQ4):** - **Race conditions & Bugs:** Converting a codebase that was single-threaded to multi-threaded is notoriously risky. We must carefully analyze all places where state is mutated. Data structures like caches, index structures must be made thread-safe or confined. The risk is data corruption if not done properly, or crashes due to race. - **Deadlocks:** Introducing locks can lead to deadlocks if not carefully ordered or if multiple locks are acquired in opposite order by different threads. We'll need to define a locking hierarchy or use trylocks or other methods to avoid that. Possibly keep coarse locks at first to be safe (like a global write lock for any write operation, etc.), then refine if performance allows. - **Performance scaling issues:** If we do too coarse locking (like a global interpreter lock), then multiple threads won't actually improve throughput much (they'd be mostly serialized). So need to find the right granularity. E.g., perhaps partition data (like by graph ID or something) with separate locks, so transactions on different parts proceed independently. - **Context switching overhead:** If we spawn thread per query without limit, could overwhelm system with context switching if very many clients. Ideally have a pool and queue extra tasks if threads are all busy, which is typical thread pool design. Also, careful to not oversubscribe CPU heavily beyond core count, or performance might degrade. Usually thread pool size = number of cores or a multiple for I/O heavy tasks. - **Testing concurrency:** We need multi-thread stress tests. It's tricky to catch all race conditions, but use heavy concurrent workload and verify consistency. Possibly use Jepsen-like tests or at least random concurrency tests (like many threads doing transactions that increment counters in the graph, final sum should match expected if no lost update). - **Fairness measurement:** one query not starving others. We can test by running one artificial heavy query (like a long sleep or big loop) and shorter queries concurrently, ensure the short queries still complete reasonably. If heavy query hogs core exclusively, short ones might wait entire time if only one core - but if machine has multiple cores, OS will run short on another core so fine. If heavy query count equals core count and a short query arrives, OS might still preempt one heavy thread to run short for some time slice, but no guarantee it finishes promptly if heavy threads keep consuming. However, OS typically will time slice so short tasks get some CPU eventually. Possibly measure 95th percentile latency in such scenarios to ensure it's not beyond an acceptable factor. - **Transaction correctness:** Multi-threading means multiple transactions in progress, need to ensure snapshot isolation still holds. If our MVCC is well-implemented (each transaction reads a snapshot, writes acquire write locks), it should hold. But we should test for phantom reads or any anomalies carefully. Snapshots should prevent read-read conflicts anyway, and using Raft/2PC ensures commit order is consistent cluster-wide. But multi-threaded local concurrency might need coordination with Raft? Actually, if cluster, each node might have multiple threads doing things, but Raft log is single serial for writes replication, which might become a bottleneck. Possibly not tackling that yet (just ensure local concurrency, Raft can still handle one entry at a time but multiple can pipeline). - **Memory contention:** More threads means more memory usage (stacks, etc.) and possible contention on memory allocators or caches. If Zig's default allocator is not thread-safe or optimized, we might consider using a lock-free allocator or per-thread pools to reduce contention. - **Third-party libs thread safety:** If any libs are used (like OpenSSL for encryption, or file IO libs), ensure they are thread-safe or we use them properly. For file I/O, multiple threads writing can thrash disk if not careful (but OS can queue). - **Debug difficulty:** Concurrency issues might only appear under load, making them hard to debug. We might incorporate runtime checks (like Zig can use Thread Sanitizer if compiled with appropriate flags, perhaps try to test with that).

**Benchmarks & Criteria (SQ5):** - **Throughput scaling test:** With concurrency off vs on: - Set up say 8 client threads each doing some mix of queries (reads, maybe writes). Compare throughput (transactions per second) with the old single-threaded (which probably would handle them sequentially) vs new (should handle concurrently, so roughly 8x throughput if no other bottlenecks). - Also measure CPU utilization: ensure multi-thread mode uses ~8 cores fully, whereas single-thread only uses ~1. - We'll test with a read-heavy workload and a write-heavy workload: - For reads (no write conflicts), we expect near linear scaling until maybe saturating memory bandwidth or some lock in read path if present. - For writes, if using a single global lock, maybe not as good scaling, but we might still allow some interleaving if independent data. Ideally, two writes to different parts can proceed concurrently. - We might adjust workload so minimal conflicts and measure ideal throughput, vs high conflicts scenario to see how locks reduce concurrency (should degrade gracefully). - **Latency under load:** Evaluate how individual query latency is affected by others: - e.g., run one heavy query (simulate heavy by adding a delay or do a large aggregation) and in parallel run a short query (simple lookup). Measure the short query's latency. With single-thread, short query waits until heavy finishes -> very high latency. With multi-thread on a multi-core machine, short query should complete much sooner (if heavy running on one core and short on another). - If cores are saturated, ensure short gets at least some share (maybe slight wait but not entire heavy's runtime). - This is to validate fairness: e.g., ideally short query latency doesn't go beyond say 2-3x its standalone latency even when heavy running (depending on how OS schedules). - **Isolation test:** Design concurrent transactions that would violate isolation if locks/MVCC fails: - e.g., two threads updating the same record concurrently. Use a scenario where if no proper locking, final count would be wrong. Run many times, check consistency (like a lost update test). - Or a phantom read test: one thread does a query scanning a range while another inserts into that range concurrently. Under snapshot isolation, the scanning transaction should not see the new item if its snapshot was taken before insert commit. Verify outcomes align with snapshot semantics (no phantom in snapshot iso). - If Geode supports serializable isolation (probably not yet, maybe snapshot only), test accordingly. - **Deadlock detection:** If we allow multiple writes and used locks that could deadlock if two transactions lock resources in opposite order, test a scenario that would create such a condition (e.g., transaction A updates node1 then node2, transaction B updates node2 then node1 concurrently). We need either to detect and break deadlock or have a policy to avoid it. Under MVCC, usually write-write conflicts are either avoided by ordering or aborted one transaction if conflict.
