We likely should have a mechanism to abort one if they conflict (maybe the first to commit wins, other fails with conflict). - Test that scenario and ensure one transaction aborts gracefully (with an error) rather than system hang. - **Parallel query (if any)**: If we do any intra-query parallel (maybe not initially), test that it indeed speeds that query up on multi-core. If not implementing now, skip. - **Resource usage:** Check that multi-thread usage doesn't overshoot memory (shouldn't inherently, but if we allocate per thread some caches, note overhead). Also measure context switch overhead by running thousands of very tiny queries concurrently to see if overhead is negligible or saturates something. - **Stability under high concurrency:** e.g., run 100 threads doing random operations for a prolonged time and ensure no crashes or data corruption. This can find race issues possibly (coupled with thread sanitizer runs).

Acceptance criteria: - **Throughput improvement:** Achieve significant throughput gain with multiple concurrent operations. Perhaps target at least ~80% of linear scalability up to some moderate concurrency (like 4 or 8 threads). e.g., if single-thread does 100 TPS, 4 threads ideally 400 TPS, we accept if >=320 TPS (80% linear). - **Fairness:** No single query starves others. In testing, smaller queries still complete in a timely manner even with bigger ones present (quantify: under high load, 95th percentile latency for short queries remains within, say, 2x of unloaded latency; obviously under extreme CPU saturation it will degrade, but try to maintain fairness). - **Correctness:** All concurrency tests produce correct results (no lost updates, no dirty reads, no phantom beyond what isolation level allows). If using snapshot isolation, we allow some anomalies like write skew maybe, but that's known. We mainly ensure no consistency break beyond designed isolation. - **No Deadlocks or if deadlock possible, they are detected and transactions aborted**. The system should not hang. If a deadlock occurs, the engine should rollback one transaction (and perhaps let client know). - **Stability:** Running concurrency tests for extended time yields no memory leaks (monitor memory usage, should remain stable or periodic GC if any). - **Resource usage:** CPU usage can go to 100% on all cores under load (which is good, meaning it's utilizing hardware), and is balanced across threads typically. Also measure that enabling multi-threading with no load doesn't consume extra CPU (idle threads just waiting). - **Devil's advocate**: if someone runs Geode on a single-core VM, multi-threading overhead might slightly degrade performance due context switching. We should allow configuration to limit threads or turn off parallelism if needed (or detect core count and adjust thread count).

**Dependencies (SQ6):** - Possibly require using Zig's std.Thread or similar to spawn threads. Ensure compiled with thread-safe runtime if needed. - If an asynchronous reactor was used previously for networking, we might integrate thread pooling with that (e.g., use one thread for network accept, then farm off queries to worker threads). - Use concurrency primitives from Zig (mutex, condvar, etc.) or need to integrate a third-party concurrency library if any (Zig is low-level enough to use OS threads directly). - Adapt test harness to run multi-threaded tests (perhaps our existing tests mostly single-threaded; might need to create new integration tests that spawn threads performing DB ops). - Ensure any global variables or singletons are protected or eliminated. - Logging subsystem: if multi-thread logs concurrently, ensure thread-safety of logging (maybe just one thread writing to log or use atomic writes). Might need a lock on log file writing to avoid jumbled logs. - Memory allocator: check Zig default is thread-safe; it might be but could be improved by using thread-local allocators if heavy allocation in parallel (to avoid lock contention). - If enabling within cluster (multiple multi-threaded nodes), ensure cluster protocols (Raft) handle concurrency. Raft log append might need a lock if multiple threads try to append (though ideally one leader thread does that). Possibly serializing Raft commands via one thread might remain, which could be bottleneck for heavy write concurrency - but at cluster level, linearize via Raft is needed anyway. - Possibly plan to implement a **transaction scheduler** if needed to queue transactions on conflict, but snapshot isolation often aborts conflicting transactions rather than scheduling. - Also consider OS thread counts: allow config to limit, default to number of cores. - Observability: maybe add metrics for number of active threads, queue length if tasks queued, etc., to monitor concurrency performance.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - In v0.18, enable multi-threading behind a flag for cautious rollout (maybe GEODE_MAX_THREADS or GEODE_MULTITHREAD=1). Actually, we might just default to use N cores (since likely beneficial) but at least allow disabling if issues arise. - Mark it experimental in docs, asking users to try with realistic workloads and report issues, particularly for unusual concurrency patterns. - Internally, do heavy stress testing with sanitizers to flush out issues before release. Possibly run something like thread sanitizer or use concurrency testing tools if available. - Plan for quick patch releases if a severe concurrency bug is found (like data race causing corruption). - It's a major architectural improvement, so maybe release it in 0.18 but if stability is uncertain, hide behind flag and fully enable in 0.19 after more soak. - Stepwise enabling: maybe first allow parallel reads, but still single write thread (to avoid immediate complexity). Then in next version, allow parallel writes to non-conflicting parts, etc. That might be an internal detail though. Possibly simpler: allow all, rely on MVCC to handle conflicts by aborting a writer if needed. - Provide guidance: e.g., instruct that if one experiences unusual slowdowns or errors, they can disable multi-threading as fallback. But aim to make it stable enough not to require that. - This significantly improves Geode's capacity, so highlight it in release notes.

## Executive Summary

**As of 2025-09-25**, Geode's post-v0.17.0 gap analysis reveals seven priority areas to solidify its standing as a distributed, secure, and performant graph database. **Distributed query federation** (cross-shard execution with global sorting) and **cloud backup integration (S3)** emerge as top must-haves to support horizontal scaling and enterprise disaster recovery. Concurrently, improvements to the **cost-based optimizer (CBO)** are needed to ensure robust plan selection on all data sizes - a prerequisite to enabling it by default. Addressing a minor but impactful gap, the **CDC webhook sink** will gain support for custom HTTP headers and refined retry/backoff logic, enhancing integration with external systems. In the security realm, introducing **Transparent Data Encryption (TDE)** for data-at-rest and optional **Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)** for user logins will meet regulatory expectations for data protection[\[8\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=Transparent%20Data%20Encryption%20,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS)[\[9\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=The%20recent%20Oracle%20Database%2023ai,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c). Geode's **full-text search (FTS)** will be upgraded with phrase queries, result highlighting, and tunable ranking to improve search relevance and usability[\[10\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L34-L39)[\[11\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=,tags%20or%20otherwise%20emphasizing%20them). Finally, the execution engine will be made **multi-threaded**, leveraging modern multi-core hardware to boost throughput and ensure fairness under load[\[12\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L24-L32)[\[13\]](https://tidb-blog.vercel.app/posts/the-most-underrated-tidb-feature#:~:text=1,when%20comparing%20MySQL%20to%20TiDB).

This update plan details each theme's user impact, design alternatives, industry comparables, risks, and acceptance criteria. Key recommendations include using a coordinator for distributed queries[\[29\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=Sharding%20graph%20data%20is%20a,for%20the%20specific%20use%20case), adopting S3 multipart backups with checksums for integrity[\[47\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=Many%20organizations%20are%20protecting%20important,can%20save%20time%20and%20money), calibrating the CBO for small-N cases (with guardrails to avoid bad plans)[\[7\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L160), implementing header injection and idempotency keys for CDC webhooks, employing standard AES-256 encryption and TOTP-based MFA for security, leveraging positional indexes for FTS phrase search, and introducing a task-thread pool for parallel execution. A phased rollout is proposed: foundational federation and S3 backup in v0.18.0, with other features maturing by v0.19.0 after thorough testing. Measurable outcomes - e.g., deterministic cross-shard pagination, 99.99% backup integrity, >85% optimizer accuracy on estimates, zero data loss in CDC retries, <5% TDE performance overhead, relevant FTS snippets, ~linear throughput scaling across cores - are defined to track success. This evidence-backed plan enables Geode to confidently graduate these features to "default on," thereby closing critical gaps and aligning with the capabilities of leading graph databases (Neo4j, Neptune, TigerGraph) and distributed SQL systems (CockroachDB, Spanner) in the upcoming releases.

## Background & Context

Geode is an in-development graph DB aimed at full **ISO/IEC 39075 GQL** compliance[\[73\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L2-L6). The recent v0.17.0 release (September 2025) marked a major milestone, delivering production-ready implementations of core features and closing many correctness gaps. It introduced a complete **MATCH** query engine with complex pattern support, **DML** (insert/update/delete) with enforced row-level security, an initial **Cost-Based Optimizer** (stats-driven, behind a feature flag), **CDC sinks** for Kafka and webhooks (ensuring at-least-once delivery), **Raft-based clustering** with durable consensus, and a hardened **QUIC/SSL transport layer**[\[1\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L9-L17)[\[2\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L25-L32). v0.17 focused on correctness and standards alignment - e.g., it enforced that OFFSET/LIMIT must have an ORDER BY per ISO SQL/GQL rules[\[33\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L70-L74) and implemented tie-breaks for deterministic query results[\[34\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L14-L17). The result was a big leap toward feature completeness, as reflected in the release's extensive test suite (14 new end-to-end tests covering MATCH, CBO, CDC, backups, etc.)[\[74\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L96-L105).

However, a **post-release review** identified several **remaining gaps** and improvement areas that, while not compromising core correctness, are important for Geode's usability, performance, and security in real-world deployments. Chief among these is **distributed query federation** - Geode currently operates effectively as a single-node system; multi-node clusters replicate data via Raft but do not yet support _querying across multiple shards_ (no parallel partitioned query execution)[\[3\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L37-L40). This limits scalability for very large graphs. The review also noted the absence of a **hot backup** mechanism - there is _"No hot backups"_ and only local snapshotting, meaning users lack an integrated way to back up data to durable cloud storage[\[5\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L38-L40). On the query optimizer, it was observed that the CBO, while functional, can pick suboptimal plans on **small datasets (<100 rows)** and thus remains off by default[\[7\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L160). Enabling it as default requires further tuning and safeguards. The **CDC Webhook sink** feature was incomplete in v0.17, lacking support for custom HTTP headers (needed for auth tokens or custom metadata)[\[75\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L158-L160) and with limited observability into its retry/delivery process.

On the **security** side, Geode in v0.17 had TLS for data in transit but did not provide **encryption at rest** - data files and backups are stored in plaintext. Nor did it offer advanced auth like MFA; authentication was password-based, with an MFA module stubbed but not implemented[\[21\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L12-L15). Given modern security and compliance requirements, these are notable gaps. The **full-text search** functionality in v0.17 was rudimentary: it indexed tokens and supported basic term queries with BM25 ranking, but lacked phrase search, fuzzy matching, highlighting, etc., which are important for search usability[\[10\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L34-L39). Finally, the review highlighted that Geode's execution was essentially **single-threaded** - one query at a time per server thread - meaning it was not exploiting multi-core processors and had potential concurrency bottlenecks[\[22\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L14-L15). This raised concerns about throughput and fairness in a multi-user environment.

In summary, as of v0.17, Geode achieved correctness on core features but needs these improvements to meet **enterprise expectations**: ability to scale out queries across shards, reliable backups for recovery, automated query tuning on by default, flexible CDC integrations, strong security controls, rich text search, and high throughput via parallel execution. These align with capabilities offered by competitors: - _Neo4j Fabric_ allows cross-database federation (for sharded graphs)[\[29\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=Sharding%20graph%20data%20is%20a,for%20the%20specific%20use%20case), - _Amazon Neptune_ provides automated backups to S3 and encryption at rest by default (KMS-managed keys), - _TigerGraph_ and _JanusGraph_ support distributed partitioning and multi-threaded processing for graph traversals, - _CockroachDB_ has a CBO always on (with extensive tuning to ensure no regressions on small tables), - _PostgreSQL/Oracle_ offer TDE and MFA (Oracle 23c introduced MFA for database logins)[\[9\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=The%20recent%20Oracle%20Database%2023ai,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c), - _Elasticsearch/OpenSearch_ provide phrase queries and highlighting out-of-the-box for text search.

The following sections analyze each gap area in detail, incorporating evidence from industry best practices and Geode's context, to formulate a concrete plan for v0.18-v0.19 that will close these gaps. The plan balances ambition with prudence: features that significantly de-risk usage (federation, backups, security) are prioritized for the next release, while others that need more soak time (CBO default-on, full text enhancements) are slated for the subsequent release with clear exit criteria. This phased approach ensures stability as Geode evolves from a feature-complete prototype to a production-ready distributed graph database.

## Core Findings by Theme

### 1\. Distributed Query Federation

**Why It Matters - User Impact (SQ1):** Federation will allow Geode to execute queries across multiple shards/nodes transparently, a necessity for scaling graphs beyond a single machine and for enabling _distributed analytics_. This benefits any deployment where data is partitioned for size or locality - e.g., a social network graph sharded by region can still answer global queries ("find friends-of-friends across all regions") once federation is in place. Currently, Geode clusters replicate data for fault-tolerance (via Raft) but do not partition queries: effectively, each query runs on one node's data[\[3\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L37-L40). Users like data engineers or application developers who manage large graphs will gain the ability to **horizontally scale** query processing and avoid manual data collation. It also unlocks new use-cases: e.g., **global graph traversal** combining results from many shards, **cross-shard joins** between different subgraphs, and consistent **global ORDER BY + LIMIT** for pagination across the entire dataset (important for UIs that page through large query results). Without this, Geode cannot fully utilize a cluster of nodes for a single query, limiting performance on big queries and complicating application logic (which would otherwise have to merge shard results itself). Federation thus directly addresses scalability and ease-of-use for enterprise deployments.

**Current State & Evidence:** The v0.17 review noted that _"Distributed Operation: Multi-leader replication, sharding, 2PC not present. Effectively single-node."_[\[3\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L37-L40) - meaning Geode has no distributed query planner or distributed execution engine yet. However, some building blocks are in place: Geode uses **Raft consensus** for replication and even implemented a form of two-phase commit over Raft for distributed transactions (ensuring atomic cross-shard writes)[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48). This indicates that cluster nodes can coordinate transactional state. What's missing is the **query planning and routing** aspect. In a federated query, partial results from shards must be aggregated and sorted globally. For example, an ORDER BY with LIMIT needs a deterministic global ordering rather than per-shard ordering. Deterministic pagination requires a consistent global sort across shards[\[4\]](https://planetscale.com/blog/mysql-pagination#:~:text=Pagination%20in%20MySQL%20,same%20way%20every%20single). Geode already enforces order requirements on single-node queries (LIMIT/OFFSET must have ORDER BY)[\[33\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L70-L74); extending that determinism across shards is crucial. Without it, pagination could show duplicates or out-of-order entries when data is split.

**Design Proposal (SQ2):** We propose a **Coordinator-Worker model** for distributed queries, as used in many distributed SQL databases[\[26\]](https://docs.citusdata.com/en/v9.5/develop/reference_processing.html#:~:text=A%20Citus%20cluster%20consists%20of,brief%20by%20the%20diagram%20below). One node (e.g., the Raft leader or a designated "coordinator" role) will **plan and coordinate** the query, while all relevant shards (which could include the coordinator's node as well) execute parts of the plan on local data. Concretely: - The coordinator parses the incoming GQL query and breaks it into sub-queries per shard. For instance, a MATCH pattern will be evaluated on each shard's partition of the graph. - Each shard executes its fragment (using the existing local engine) and returns results (or intermediate tuples) to the coordinator. - The coordinator merges these results. This involves operations like **global aggregation** (combining counts/sums from shards), **global joins** (joining shard outputs, if data wasn't co-located), and crucially **global sorting and limiting**. We will implement a **distributed top-K algorithm**: each shard can locally sort and provide its top K (K = limit + some slack), then the coordinator merges those to determine the true top results[\[30\]](https://www.yugabyte.com/blog/optimize-pagination-distributed-data-maintain-ordering/#:~:text=0%20and%20event_ts%20,infinity%27%20ORDER%20BY%20event_ts%20DESC)[\[31\]](https://www.yugabyte.com/blog/optimize-pagination-distributed-data-maintain-ordering/#:~:text=match%20at%20L383%20shard_id%3D0%20and,2%20ORDER%20BY%20event_ts%20DESC). This minimizes data transfer while yielding exact results. - The coordinator then streams final results to the client.

This approach leverages Geode's existing infrastructure: the coordinator can use the **Raft group membership** to know all shards and their addresses, and can even use Raft's reliable RPC mechanism (or an equivalent) to dispatch queries and collect responses. Since Raft ensures a consistent view of membership, we don't risk missing a shard's data or double-counting. Importantly, the design aligns with how Google Spanner or CockroachDB handle distributed queries: a central planner with workers on each data node[\[26\]](https://docs.citusdata.com/en/v9.5/develop/reference_processing.html#:~:text=A%20Citus%20cluster%20consists%20of,brief%20by%20the%20diagram%20below). It ensures that semantics like DISTINCT or ORDER BY as defined by ISO GQL hold true cluster-wide (all shards' data considered).

**Global ORDER BY semantics:** One of the trickiest aspects is maintaining a deterministic order across shards. The solution is to have the coordinator do a **final merge sort** of results from shards. Each shard provides results sorted by the query's ORDER BY clause (which may involve attributes, computed values, etc.), and the coordinator merges these streams respecting the sort key. This produces a globally sorted result[\[76\]](https://docs.citusdata.com/en/v9.5/develop/reference_processing.html#:~:text=Next%2C%20the%20planner%20breaks%20the,the%20distributed%20executor%20for%20execution)[\[77\]](https://docs.citusdata.com/en/v9.5/develop/reference_processing.html#:~:text=Citus%E2%80%99s%20distributed%20executor%20runs%20distributed,results%20back%20to%20the%20user). If a LIMIT is specified, the coordinator can stop early once it has determined the top-N. We will also enforce stable sorting by including a tiebreaker (like shard ID or internal row ID) for tie cases, ensuring the order is fully deterministic[\[34\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L14-L17). This meets the ISO requirement for stable pagination - every run of the query with the same data yields the same order[\[4\]](https://planetscale.com/blog/mysql-pagination#:~:text=Pagination%20in%20MySQL%20,same%20way%20every%20single).

**Joins and aggregations:** For a cross-shard join, the coordinator may need to redistribute data (e.g., send relevant join keys from one shard to another) or perform the join itself if data is too distributed. Initially, we can target simpler cases (e.g., the query join condition aligns with the partitioning key, so each shard can join independently). More complex joins might be handled via a naive strategy (pull all candidate data to coordinator, join there) in the first iteration, then optimized later. Aggregations can be done in a two-stage fashion: each shard does a local aggregation, then the coordinator aggregates the aggregates (this is standard for SUM, COUNT, etc., and works as long as functions are algebraic). This approach keeps bandwidth use modest - e.g., summing a billion distributed values might only send a few partial sums from each shard.

**Alternative considered:** A more decentralized approach (each shard directly talking to others) was considered but found less manageable. A central coordinator avoids complex synchronization among shards and fits Geode's consistency model (Raft leader can naturally act as coordinator). It does introduce a potential single-node bottleneck, but for the moderate cluster sizes expected (dozens of nodes), one coordinator merging results is feasible. In future, if needed, we could elect multiple coordinators or parallelize merge steps (like a tree of coordinators) - similar to parallel query coordinators in large MPP systems - but that's likely unnecessary initially.

**Standards Compliance (SQ3):** This design adheres to **ISO GQL semantics** by ensuring that a distributed query's outcome is the same as if the data were on a single node. For instance, the GQL standard's rules on duplicates, ordering, grouping, etc., are preserved. The coordinator approach essentially simulates a unified dataset. We will need to be mindful of determinism (as discussed) and also of **transactional consistency**: we should execute the distributed query under a consistent snapshot of the database. Fortunately, Geode's MVCC and Raft provide a path to do this - e.g., the coordinator can include a Raft log index or timestamp with the sub-query requests so each shard reads from a snapshot at that point in time. This guarantees no shard sees data from after another shard's portion of the query (preventing consistency anomalies). This aligns with the ISO concept of a serializable (or at least snapshot-isolated) execution as if the query ran on a single image of the DB. Additionally, our approach to ORDER BY + LIMIT ensures **pagination determinism**, which is often emphasized in standards/guides (e.g., SQL:2008's windowing and fetch first rules require deterministic order for stable results). We are essentially extending those rules to the distributed realm.

**Risks & Complexity (SQ4):** Building federation is complex. Potential failure modes include partial results if a shard fails mid-query - we must decide whether to fail the query or return partial data (we will fail the query and propagate an error, since partial results violate consistency). Network latency could impact query latency; a slow shard can become the straggler that delays the whole query. This is a known issue (tail latency). We mitigate it by perhaps over-sampling or using timeouts: e.g., if one shard is unresponsive, we could either retry its fragment or, if not critical, exclude it with a warning. However, excluding data isn't acceptable for correctness, so more likely we'll fail the query after a timeout. Another performance pitfall is _data skew_: if one shard has disproportionate amount of data matching the query, the coordinator and that shard may become a bottleneck. Our initial implementation won't fully solve skew (that often needs dynamic rebalancing or more complex parallelism), but recognizing it is important. We will test with skewed distributions to ensure the system still functions (perhaps slower, but correctly).

From an engineering standpoint, adding a distributed planner touches many subsystems - the parser (to ensure queries can be planned in fragments), the planner (deciding which operations go to shards vs coordinator), the execution engine (to handle new "gather" or "scatter" operators), the network layer (for sending sub-queries and receiving results), etc. The risk of bugs (like double-counting or missing some shard's data) is non-trivial. To mitigate this, we will implement federation in stages: e.g., first support simple UNION ALL style combination of full results (for queries without global sorting or complex join logic), then add handling for ORDER/LIMIT, then joins. This staged approach, combined with extensive testing (see SQ5), will help manage complexity.

An additional complexity is **transactional updates with queries** - e.g., a distributed query in a read-write transaction. For now, we will assume distributed _read-only_ queries. Distributed write transactions are handled by the 2PC over Raft logic (e.g., one can insert data on multiple shards atomically), which we have but is likely synchronous and separate from query logic. We won't integrate multi-shard _updates_ into a single query (like an UPDATE with a subquery spanning shards) until later, as that would require two-phase commit coordination at query time.

**Benchmark & Success Criteria (SQ5):** We will validate federation with both **functional tests** and **performance tests**. Functionally, one test will set up a cluster with, say, 3 shards, each holding part of a dataset (e.g., a graph of people split by initial of name). We'll run queries that need data from all shards - e.g., a MATCH that finds relationships between persons in different shards - and verify the result matches the same query executed on a single-node with all data. This ensures correctness (no missing or duplicate edges, etc.). We'll specifically test global ordering by inserting known data on each shard (like shard A has names starting with A.., B with M.., etc.) and issuing a MATCH ... RETURN name ORDER BY name LIMIT 5. The output should be the top 5 names alphabetically across _all_ shards, not just from one[\[29\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=Sharding%20graph%20data%20is%20a,for%20the%20specific%20use%20case). If our global merge is correct, we will see the proper mix (and importantly, deterministic ordering across runs). We'll also test failure handling: kill one shard's process during a distributed query and confirm the query aborts with a clear error, and that after recovery (raft failover) the same query can succeed (federation should adapt to new leader or shard membership).

Performance-wise, we set a goal that **distributed queries scale reasonably with data volume**. For example, if we double the number of shards (each with the same amount of data), a query scanning the whole dataset should see roughly half the execution time on each shard and similar overall time (ignoring coordination overhead). Some overhead will occur at the coordinator, but we aim for, say, &lt;20% overhead vs an ideal linear speedup. We'll simulate this by benchmarking a large graph query on 1 shard vs 2 vs 4 shards (with data partitioned). If 1 shard took T time, 4 shards ideally could approach T/4 (plus merge time). A concrete success threshold might be that the 4-shard query finishes in under T/2 (accounting for overhead), meaning we got &gt;2× speedup from 4× the resources. We'll also measure the **tail latency**: if one shard is slower (perhaps we add delay on one), does it significantly drag the whole query? We expect the query finishes when the slowest shard finishes, so it's important to either minimize shard imbalance or mitigate via future techniques (like speculative retry, which is advanced). In tests, we'll ensure that if one shard is say 2× slower than others, the overall query is at most 2× slower than the fastest could have been - basically no worse than waiting for that slow shard, which is expected.

Another critical metric: **Correctness under concurrent queries and failures.** We'll run multiple distributed queries in parallel to see that they don't interfere (they shouldn't, beyond sharing bandwidth). Also test with ongoing data changes: e.g., run a long distributed query while writing to one shard; the query should see a consistent snapshot (not half-written data) - verifying our snapshot isolation across shards. We'll measure if the presence of writes affects the read query result correctness (it shouldn't; either the query runs at a raft index before those writes or they are not included mid-flight).

**Dependencies & Pre-requisites (SQ6):** Implementing federation touches on a few prerequisite tasks: - We need a **shard metadata registry** - essentially a mapping of data to shards. Geode currently might not have a notion of partitioning key. We may introduce a simple scheme (e.g., user can specify a property or ID as shard key, or we default to hashing an internal ID). For now, even a simple round-robin or manual assignment can work. The coordinator needs to know, for a given query or pattern, which shards to consult. For property queries, that might be all shards unless we have a way to narrow it (like if query has a WHERE id = X, we could route only to shard responsible for X). In v0.18, we might broadcast to all shards unless partition pruning is obvious, to keep it simple. - **Refactoring the planner:** The current planner must be extended to produce a distributed execution plan. We might introduce an intermediate representation where certain operators are tagged "distributed". For example, a logical plan might have a "Distributed Union" operator that the planner will implement by splitting the plan beneath it to multiple shards. This requires some new code but is manageable with an optimizer framework. Also, stats collection may need to become shard-aware (the coordinator might need global stats, which could be aggregated from per-shard stats). - **Network communication:** The coordinator will send query plans or subplans to shards. We can either serialize the plan (perhaps as a simple bytecode or reuse our existing query string by adding appropriate filters) and send it via Geode's network protocol. Given we have a custom protocol over QUIC, we can define new message types like GQL_FRAGMENT_REQUEST. Each shard's server will need a handler for that - essentially to execute a provided query (with maybe bind parameters or specific instructions) and stream results back. We might leverage our existing client handling logic by making the coordinator act like a client to the shard, which could simplify implementation (shard sees a normal query from coordinator). However, for efficiency, sending a pre-parsed/optimized plan might be better than re-parsing on each shard. Initially, to reduce complexity, we could send as GQL text with a special flag to not log it and treat it as internal. The overhead of parsing on each shard for each distributed query is acceptable for v0.18 given moderate query frequencies; we can optimize later by shipping a physical plan. - **Threading/async:** The coordinator will likely need to handle multiple shard responses asynchronously. This ties into the concurrency improvements (theme 7). We should ensure the network layer and query processing can handle concurrency so the coordinator can wait on multiple shard results in parallel. Zig's async or a multi-threaded approach will be used. If by v0.18 we haven't fully multithreaded the engine, we may implement the coordinator to spawn parallel tasks for sub-queries (e.g., using an async I/O for each shard request). This dependency means federation may push us to introduce some concurrency in the engine (which is planned anyway). - **Testing & Tooling:** Setting up multi-node integration tests requires infrastructure (perhaps using Docker or multi-process on one machine). We already have a cluster_spawn.sh and some multi-process test in v0.17[\[38\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L43-L46). We'll extend that to automate running queries on a launched cluster. This is crucial for continuous integration testing of distributed queries.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** We will introduce federation functionality in a **beta capacity** in **v0.18.0**, guarded by a feature flag or config (e.g., enable_distributed_queries). Initially, it might not handle every construct (for example, maybe no support for very complex subqueries or user-defined procedures in distributed mode). We'll document these limitations. The aim is to get real-world feedback on simpler cross-shard queries first. During v0.18, we'll encourage internal teams or friendly users to pilot sharded setups and report any correctness or performance issues. We'll also include comprehensive logging when DEBUG is enabled (like logging the distributed plan and timing per shard) to aid in debugging and tuning.

By **v0.19.0**, after refining based on feedback, we expect to remove the flag and have federation on by default for clusters with multiple shards. Criteria to graduate would include: passing all acceptance tests consistently, no known query correctness bugs, and performance tests showing acceptable overhead. We will also add monitoring for distributed queries: e.g., a system view or metrics that show per-shard timings or data volume. This helps DBAs see if a particular shard is a bottleneck (addressing the tail latency issue by at least making it visible).

Operationally, we'll add safeguards: if a shard is unavailable, the coordinator will fail fast and not hang indefinitely (with a clear error like "Shard X unreachable during query - query aborted"). We'll also implement basic **cancellation**: if the client cancels the query (or a timeout triggers), the coordinator will signal shards to stop work (this can reuse our existing query cancellation mechanism if any).

Overall, federation is a large feature but one that brings Geode to parity with distributed databases. With the above plan, Geode v0.18 will, for the first time, allow truly distributed query processing - a key milestone in its roadmap.

### 2\. Backups to Cloud Object Storage (S3)

**Why It Matters - User Impact (SQ1):** A robust backup and restore capability is essential for any database in production. Integrating **S3-compatible cloud storage for backups** gives Geode users (especially DevOps and DBAs) confidence that they can recover from disasters (node loss, accidental deletes, etc.) and meet compliance for data retention. Currently, Geode lacks an online backup mechanism - there's no straightforward way to get a consistent snapshot of the database onto durable storage outside the node's local disk[\[5\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L38-L40). This is a serious operational gap. By enabling backups to S3, Geode will support: - **Point-in-Time Recovery:** Users can restore the database to the state at the time of backup (or even a specific point between backups if we do incremental logs) in case of corruption or data loss. - **Cloud-native operations:** In Kubernetes or cloud environments, ephemeral nodes are common - having S3 backups means the entire database can be re-instantiated from S3 if needed. Also, migrating data across environments becomes easier (e.g., backup in one region, restore in another for a blue-green deployment). - **Long-term retention and compliance:** Many regulations require periodic backups stored securely off-site. S3 offers lifecycle policies (e.g., archival to Glacier, retention enforcement) that users can leverage once the data is in S3[\[39\]](https://n2ws.com/blog/aws-backup-best-practices#:~:text=Top%2010%20AWS%20Backup%20Best,RTO%29)[\[40\]](https://dev.to/arvind_toorpu/enabling-database-backup-and-restore-to-s3-for-sql-server-in-aws-rds-a-step-by-step-guide-5hem#:~:text=Enabling%20Database%20Backup%20and%20Restore,required%20S3%20bucket%20and%20operations). - **Testing and analytics:** Teams can take a production backup and restore it to a dev instance for testing, without manually dumping data.

In short, cloud backups de-risk Geode deployments by ensuring data is not tied to the life of a particular server or cluster. This capability aligns Geode with industry norms: major cloud databases (Amazon Neptune, Azure Cosmos DB, etc.) all provide automated backups (often to cloud storage) with point-in-time restore. Without it, Geode would be seen as not enterprise-ready. Indeed, our v0.17 "Future Work" explicitly lists _"S3-compatible backup destinations"_ as a next step[\[6\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L164-L169), showing this feature was already anticipated.

**Design Proposal (SQ2):** We will implement a **backup service** in Geode that can create consistent snapshots of the database and upload them to S3 (or any S3 API-compatible storage). Key points in the design: - **Consistency:** We'll leverage the Raft snapshot mechanism to produce a consistent snapshot across the cluster. In Raft, the leader can coordinate a snapshot at a specific log index that all followers can use[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48). For backup, one simple approach is to perform the backup from the Raft leader: pause new transactions momentarily (or use the last stable index), have each node flush its state (or send its state to leader), and gather a cluster-wide snapshot. However, since Raft already ensures state machine consistency, an even simpler approach is to perform a **leader snapshot** and rely on that as the full data (because in Raft, the leader's state represents the entire database at that index, replicated to others). We can thus take a snapshot from the leader's local storage. This avoids needing to merge multiple partial snapshots. - **Backup format:** We'll use the same format as our on-disk snapshots (as created for Raft InstallSnapshot) - likely a compressed file of the database state (pages or logical data). This format is already tested for restoring a node's state[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48). We will package that snapshot along with some metadata (e.g., snapshot ID, timestamp, Geode version, cluster ID) into a backup artifact. If the snapshot is large, we'll split it for upload (see next point). - **Multipart & Streaming:** To handle large backups, we'll use S3's Multipart Upload API[\[41\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=,the%20system%20also%20tracks%20changes). The backup service will stream out the snapshot in chunks (e.g., 100 MB each) such that we don't need to buffer the entire dataset in memory. Each chunk is uploaded to S3 with a part number. S3 will assemble parts into a single object with an ETag checksum. Alternatively, we could upload each chunk as a separate object (e.g., with sequential names) but using S3's native multipart is cleaner and ensures atomic finalization. We'll upload to a user-specified bucket and path (e.g., s3://my-backups/geode-prod-backup-2025-10-01/backup.bin). - **Integrity & Verification:** To ensure backup integrity, we will utilize S3's checksum capabilities. S3 can compute MD5 or CRC32C for the uploaded object[\[45\]](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/checking-object-integrity.html#:~:text=Documentation%20docs,without%20copying%20or%20downloading%20data). Additionally, Geode will compute its own CRC or hash of the snapshot data during creation (in fact, our snapshot files already include CRCs for blocks[\[15\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L40-L48)). We will store this checksum (and perhaps the expected S3 ETag) in the backup metadata. After upload, Geode can perform a **head request** to get S3's ETag and verify it matches our computed hash[\[78\]](https://serverfault.com/questions/999359/does-aws-cli-do-a-data-integrity-check-on-sync-with-s3#:~:text=Fault%20serverfault,retry%20up%20to%20five%20times). This guards against network corruption or S3 anomalies. Our end-to-end tests will include verifying an uploaded backup can be downloaded and its checksum matches the original (giving users confidence in the backup's correctness). - **Encryption:** Two layers of encryption will be in play. First, if TDE (Transparent Data Encryption) is enabled by the time of backup, the snapshot is inherently encrypted (the data files are encrypted on disk). In that case, the backup is just as encrypted as the live data (which is good). If TDE is not yet available or not enabled, we will rely on **S3's server-side encryption (SSE)**. We'll allow configuring an S3 bucket or object to use SSE-S3 (AES256) or SSE-KMS (with a user's KMS key)[\[79\]](https://www.sqltabletalk.com/?p=593#:~:text=Strategies%20www,using%20certificates%20or%20asymmetric%20keys). This way, the backup at rest in S3 is encrypted by AWS - meeting most compliance needs. We will make this configurable via backup options (e.g., in a config file or command, the user can specify --sse=AES256 or a KMS key ARN). - **Automation & Retention:** Initially, backups will be initiated manually or by user's scheduling (e.g., via a cron or Kubernetes job calling a Geode backup command). We will implement a command (accessible via CLI or REST API) such as BACKUP TO 's3://bucket/path' FULL and later possibly BACKUP INCREMENTAL. Retention can largely be managed by S3 policies (we'll document that). We may provide a simple configuration like "keep last N backups on S3" which, after a successful backup, auto-deletes older ones - but users often prefer to manage that via IA lifecycle, so we'll likely not over-automate deletion. However, we will include the backup date in the object name, so it's easy to apply lifecycle rules or to identify which backup is which. - **Restore:** A backup isn't useful without restore. The restore process will retrieve the backup from S3 and load it into a new Geode cluster or node. We will create a complementary command, e.g., RESTORE FROM 's3://bucket/path'. This will download the snapshot object (which might be large; we can stream download it), verify checksums, then replace the local data files with the downloaded snapshot (after shutting down normal operations). Essentially, it will perform the inverse of backup: feed the snapshot into the Raft state machine (or directly replace the DB files) and ensure all nodes catch up to that state (perhaps by starting the node from that snapshot and letting Raft propagate to others if a multi-node restore). For simplicity, we might require restore to a fresh cluster or single node that we then replicate - as handling an in-place restore on a running cluster is more complex. In practice, an operator would likely create a new cluster from backup for recovery, rather than overwrite a running cluster's state. - **Validation & Drills:** We intend for backup/restore to be reliable, and we encourage periodic "restore drills." We can assist by providing a way to **validate a backup** without full restore - e.g., a command to list backup metadata (to ensure it's readable) and maybe verify checksums of segments stored in S3. This leverages the stored checksums. So an operator can run a validate command that reads small portions (or the whole backup if desired) to verify nothing is corrupted in S3 (this is mostly to detect bit rot or incomplete uploads, though S3's checksums and versioning make corruption unlikely).

**Standards & Best Practices (SQ3):** This design follows best practices in cloud database backups: - **Consistency:** We ensure the backup is taken at a well-defined log point, akin to how PostgreSQL or MySQL take an online backup by pausing at a checkpoint to get a consistent snapshot. Using Raft's snapshot mechanism is consistent with the Raft log's state - it's effectively like taking a **distributed snapshot** of the state machine, satisfying atomicity of backup. - **Integrity:** Including checksums and verifying them meets enterprise expectations that backups are valid and not silently corrupted[\[43\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/introducing-default-data-integrity-protections-for-new-objects-in-amazon-s3/#:~:text=Amazon%20S3%20has%20always%20verified,before%20they%20are%20written). Some cloud backup solutions even compute checksums client-side and server-side to compare - we are doing similarly by using S3's ETag/CRC and our own CRC. - **Retention & Compliance:** By leveraging S3, we inherently allow compliance features like write-once-read-many (via Object Lock), cross-region replication of backups (for DR in case of region outage)[\[80\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=AWS%20Backup%C2%A0for%20Amazon%20S3%20enables,live%20replication%20between%20different%20environments)[\[81\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=The%20logical%20place%20to%20start,your%20data%20in%20Amazon%20S3), and encryption (SSE-KMS helps meet PCI DSS and other regulations requiring encryption with managed keys). We will document how to use these (e.g., enabling Object Lock on the bucket for immutable backups, using KMS CMKs for controlled key rotation). - **Backup performance:** Using streaming and multipart means we handle large backups efficiently. We aim to minimize downtime - possibly we won't pause writes for the entire backup duration, but rather coordinate a snapshot then resume writes while upload continues (if the snapshot is taken from on-disk state, we can allow new writes to proceed which go into the WAL after the snapshot point). This resembles how databases like SQL Server do backups: they get a consistent snapshot (perhaps via a checkpoint and freeze IO) then copy pages out while normal ops continue (copy-on-write or WAL capturing changes after snapshot). In Geode, implementing a full copy-on-write might be overkill; however, given Raft and MVCC, we might simply rely on MVCC to allow the snapshot's view to be read while new transactions commit. So we'll likely **not freeze the database for the entire backup** - perhaps a brief freeze to establish the snapshot, then normal ops resume. This meets the standard of "online backups" where minimal lock time is incurred. - **Norms:** Many systems use the pattern of full + incremental backups. We have that on the roadmap (e.g., incremental could ship only the Raft log segments since last backup). But initial implementation might be full backups for simplicity, and possibly _differential_ backups (store all changes since last full) if time permits. Regardless, any backup approach will be clearly documented and tested. We align with the norm that backup tools should be easy to use and, importantly, **restorable on a compatible version**. We'll ensure backups include a version stamp and ideally allow restore on the same or higher version of Geode (with migration if needed). We'll treat compatibility similar to how one would treat database dump files.

**Risks & Complexity (SQ4):** Potential challenges include: - **Backup Size & Time:** Graph data can be huge (hundreds of GB or more). Uploading that to S3 could take significant time (several hours) and network bandwidth. If not managed, this could impact database performance (network and disk IO usage). We mitigate this by streaming (which avoids huge memory usage) and possibly throttling the backup IO if needed (we could allow a rate limit config, or rely on the operator to run backups in off-peak times). We'll measure how backup affects query latency and include guidelines (perhaps an advisory to run during low load, or implement an internal throttling that ensures we don't saturate disk). - **Consistency in cluster:** If we rely on leader snapshot only, what if the leader fails during backup? We might need to restart backup or handle failover (maybe abort backup on leader change). For first cut, we might say that if leader changes mid-backup, the backup is invalid (and the process will detect and abort). It's a narrow window scenario. - **Restore Process:** Ensuring restore correctness is critical. A risk is that a user might try to restore onto a cluster that still has some state (causing conflict). We will probably enforce that restore is done on a fresh cluster (no existing data) or require an explicit override to wipe data. Also, while restoring, the cluster must not accept new writes (to avoid mixing old and new state). We'll likely run restore as an offline operation (cluster down or in maintenance mode). - **Security & Credentials:** Storing S3 credentials is sensitive. We plan to support using IAM roles (e.g., if running on AWS EC2 or EKS, an instance profile with S3 access can be used so no static creds needed). If users must configure access keys, we'll advise using environment variables or a secure config store (not plain config file). The risk of leakage is there, but manageable with good practices. Additionally, connecting to S3 must be done over HTTPS (which it is by default in AWS SDKs). - **Partial Backups:** If a backup fails mid-way (network blip, etc.), we could have a half-uploaded object or incomplete state. S3 multipart uploads need to be completed or aborted - we will ensure to _abort the multipart upload_ if any error occurs to avoid incurring storage cost for orphan parts. And the backup process will report failure clearly. - **Data Evolution:** If the schema or data format changes between versions, old backups might become unreadable. We should include a version tag and possibly a small migrator if needed (or at least document compatibility). For now, we assume backups taken in v0.18 can be restored in v0.18 or v0.19 which is fine if no breaking storage changes. If v0.20 had a breaking storage change, we might need to provide a conversion tool for old backups (this is long-term consideration). - **Testing complexity:** To truly test backup/restore, we need to simulate large data volumes and random kill scenarios to ensure nothing breaks or data doesn't get corrupted. We plan to run integration tests that create a dataset, back it up to a local MinIO server (for S3 API), then wipe and restore and validate data checksums or application-level correctness. Also, testing incremental backups (if implemented) requires generating some changes and verifying that applying full + incremental yields the same final state. - **User error:** e.g., user might backup the wrong cluster or restore to the wrong environment, etc. We can't solve all user error, but we can implement sanity checks: e.g., backup metadata includes a cluster UUID and restore can warn if restoring to a different cluster (to avoid accidental overwrite). And require a force flag to proceed if mismatched.

**Benchmarks & Acceptance Criteria (SQ5):** We will treat a backup/restore cycle as the unit of success: - **Correctness test:** On a running test cluster, perform a backup to S3 (or MinIO). Then spin up a new cluster (same size) and restore the backup. Run a suite of verification queries (e.g., checksums of data, counts of nodes/edges, specific known records) to ensure the restored cluster exactly matches the original. We can also verify that the Raft log of the restored cluster has the expected state (it might start with the snapshot state and an empty log aside from config changes, which is fine). - **Integrity check:** After backup, use the backup's own metadata to verify the content. For example, we'll intentionally flip a byte in the backup file (to simulate corruption) and ensure that our restore process detects a checksum mismatch and aborts (rather than applying corrupted data). - **Performance benchmarks:** Measure how long a backup takes for a given data size and system throughput. For example, if we have a 10 GB database, and a network with 1 Gbps throughput to S3, an uncompressed backup should ideally take on the order of 1-2 minutes (10 GB / ~125 MB/s = ~80s) plus some overhead. Our acceptance could be that backup throughput is within, say, 80% of available network bandwidth (meaning our implementation adds minimal overhead). If compression is on, throughput might be CPU-bound; we'll measure that too. We also measure the impact on the database: during that 80s backup, did transaction latency or throughput drop significantly? Ideally, the database is still usable. We might allow a minor degradation (perhaps transactions are 20% slower while the backup I/O is ongoing). If too severe, we may implement throttling or recommend running backups during maintenance windows. - **Scalability:** Test backup on clusters of different sizes (1-node vs 3-node). For multi-node, if we ever do a parallel backup (each node uploads its part), measure that the cluster can saturate multiple links. Our initial plan is leader-based, so it will funnel through one node - in that case the network might be a bottleneck but one node's 10 Gbps NIC can be enough in most scenarios. If needed, in future we can parallelize by having each follower upload a portion (like its snapshot of certain shards). For now, success is backup works on multi-node as well. - **Reliability tests:** Simulate interruption: start a backup and intentionally cut off network (perhaps by pointing to an unreachable S3 endpoint mid-way). Ensure that our code handles the exception and aborts cleanly (no infinite hang, no partial commit). Then test that a subsequent backup attempt can succeed (and doesn't get stuck due to previous state). - **Restore speed:** We measure how long a restore takes, because recovery time (RTO) matters. If a backup is, say, 100 GB, restore means downloading 100 GB and writing to disk - which could take similar time as backup. If we achieve near-network speed, that's optimal. But if there's overhead, we note it. We'll set an expectation like: restore should complete within X% of the time it would take to simply download the backup file at network speed. Also, after restore, the cluster should be ready to accept writes relatively quickly (just applying the snapshot and minimal catch-up logs). - **Operational simplicity:** We'll treat it as passed if a DBA can execute a documented sequence (backup command, then later restore command) and the cluster behaves as expected, with clear logging. We also aim for the backup process not requiring cluster shutdown (i.e., online backup). We will test performing a backup while some read workload is running, to confirm that concurrency doesn't break the backup or vice versa (the snapshot should be of a consistent point, and new reads/writes either see before or after states but not disturb the backup).

**Acceptance criteria:** - **Data Recovery:** 100% of data present at backup time is present after restore (we'll validate on a record-by-record basis for test datasets). No extra or missing records. - **Backup Integrity:** Every official backup artifact passes an integrity check either internally or via S3's checksum. If a corrupted backup is introduced, the system detects it (i.e., we never restore from a corrupt file without noticing). - **Ease of Use:** Users can initiate backup/restore with a single command and minimal configuration (just credentials and destination). The process should provide feedback (progress logs or at least a completion log). - **Minimal Interruption:** The database can continue to operate (especially serve reads) during backup. Ideally, writes too (with maybe a tiny pause to snapshot). We'll consider it acceptable if the pause is very brief (e.g., pausing for a second to get a Raft snapshot point). - **Performance:** Backup/restore speeds are within expected bounds of the environment (we will document typical throughput numbers). For example, if using a standard cloud VM with 1 Gbps to S3, we expect ~100 MB/s backup speed; our tests should meet something close to that. If compression is enabled, we measure CPU usage and ensure it doesn't bog down the server (we might allow users to tune compression level or disable it if they prefer speed). - **Security:** Verify that backups in S3 are indeed encrypted if requested (for instance, upload an object with SSE-KMS and then use AWS CLI to confirm its encryption status and that key ID matches). Also ensure that sensitive information (like S3 secrets or backup content) is not logged in plaintext. - **Restoration Safety:** The restore process should refuse to run if it might overwrite running data without explicit confirmation. In tests, we attempt a restore on an active cluster and expect it to either abort or require --force. Only when properly invoked on a fresh instance (or with force) does it proceed. - **Multi-version compatibility (if applicable):** If we simulate a scenario of taking a backup on v0.18 and restoring on v0.19 (assuming no breaking change), it should work or at least provide a clear migration path. We'll test a forward-compatibility scenario once v0.19 dev is underway.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** Implementing backup to S3 involves integrating an **S3 SDK or HTTP client**. We have to decide whether to use a library or write directly using HTTP requests (S3 has a REST API). Given Zig's ecosystem, we might either use a small Zig AWS SDK if one exists or implement the necessary subset (multipart upload calls, which are basically PUT with specific headers). We also need a **cryptographic library** for computing MD5/sha256 if not already available - though Zig might have built-ins for CRC or we can use a common implementation (our existing CRC in WAL can be reused). For SSE-KMS, we don't need to handle encryption ourselves, just set headers - but if we wanted client-side encryption (which we decided not to do now), that would require a crypto library and key management (we'll stick to server-side for now).

We'll likely incorporate the **AWS Signature v4 signing** for S3 requests (unless using an SDK that handles it). This is non-trivial but well-documented. Alternatively, if environment allows (like on AWS EC2/ECS), we could rely on an IAM role and use the AWS CLI externally - but embedding that is messy. Better to implement native in Geode. To reduce risk, we might initially lean on a simple approach: require the user to provide an S3 presigned URL for the backup location (which they can generate with AWS CLI or our utility). Then Geode just does an HTTP PUT to that URL with the data, no AWS signing needed. This could be a fallback if direct integration proves slow to implement. But for a smooth user experience, direct integration is preferred.

Another dependency: ensure our internal storage format is stable and clearly defined to be packaged. We have docs/IMPLEMENTATION_REALITY.md and others describing state; we should codify what exactly is included in a snapshot (likely everything to reconstruct DB state). Possibly exclude transient things like in-memory caches that can be rebuilt. Our code to produce a local snapshot will be reused - so there is dependency on that code working under various loads (which v0.17 suggests it does, as snapshots are part of Raft now).

We might also consider **locking/coordination** for backups. For example, if two backup requests come concurrently (by accident or scheduler), we should prevent them from both proceeding (could overload system or produce two snapshots at same time). We'll likely have a global backup lock to serialize backup tasks (and document that only one backup at a time is recommended).

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** We will introduce S3 backup in **v0.18.0**, likely marking it as a **beta feature** initially. The interface will be via CLI (e.g., geode backup) or possibly a GQL command (less likely, as backups are more operational than query). We might also expose it as a REST API for automation. In v0.18, it will require manual trigger - we won't do auto-scheduled backups yet (leave that to user's cron or cloud scheduler). We will thoroughly document the procedure and best practices (like test your backups, don't store credentials in plaintext, etc.).

We'll provide sample scripts for common scenarios (e.g., a shell script to run backup and wait for completion feedback).

**Observability:** During backup, we'll log progress, e.g., "Uploaded 1.2 GB of 3.5 GB (34%)" every so often, so operators see that it's working (or if it stalls). We may also expose a metric like backup_in_progress and backup_progress_bytes via JMX/metrics so that monitoring systems can alert if a backup is stuck or taking too long.

**Verification and drills:** We will encourage (in docs) regular restore testing. Possibly integrate an automated verification mode where after backup it spawns a temporary Geode instance to load the backup and verify checksum - but that might be too resource-intensive to do automatically for large data. Instead, likely a manual or separate pipeline step outside Geode.

By **v0.19.0**, after feedback, we might remove the "beta" tag. If all goes well, backup should be solid and we can consider adding incremental backups in v0.19 (since by then, enabling by default or integration with cloud backup services would be next logical steps). We'll also look at expanding beyond S3 to other stores (maybe Azure Blob, Google Cloud Storage) - however, many of those can work via S3-compatible APIs or trivial adjustments. For now, S3 covers AWS and any S3-compatible object store which is quite broad (MinIO, Ceph, etc.), so that likely suffices initially.

In summary, with S3 backup in place, Geode v0.18 will allow administrators to sleep easier knowing a copy of their graph is safely stored and recoverable, meeting the baseline expectation for database resilience.

### 3\. Cost-Based Optimizer (CBO) Improvements

**Why It Matters - User Impact (SQ1):** The Cost-Based Optimizer is crucial for query performance. In v0.17, Geode introduced a CBO (statistics-driven join ordering, etc.) but kept it **off by default** (requiring an env var)[\[20\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L25-L31) because of caution around suboptimal plans on certain workloads[\[7\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L160). This means most users are currently using a simpler heuristic planner, potentially missing out on performance gains, or they have to manually enable CBO and risk instability. Improving the CBO to the point it can be **enabled by default** will benefit all users by making the database self-tuning. Specifically, **complex queries** (multi-join patterns, big graph traversals with filters) will run faster consistently, without the user needing deep tuning expertise. Also, making CBO default avoids the scenario of a new user running into slow queries because they didn't know to flip an env var - it de-risks the out-of-the-box experience. In short, a smarter optimizer broadens Geode's applicability to heavier analytical workloads and ensures predictable performance for mission-critical queries (so, DBAs won't have to hand-optimize queries or worry that adding a few rows might flip a plan wrongly).

**Evidence of Need:** The release notes flagged that the CBO "may choose suboptimal plans for very small datasets (<100 rows)"[\[82\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L156-L159). This is a classic issue where the cost model might not account for edge cases - e.g., on tiny tables, a nested loop join might be just as fast as a hash join, but maybe the cost model still prefers hash and incurs unnecessary overhead. Or the overhead of planning itself might outweigh benefits on trivial queries. In industry, we see similar patterns: for instance, Oracle and Postgres have heuristics to not over-optimize trivial queries (Oracle's optimizer can effectively recognize when rule-based is enough)[\[71\]](https://15799.courses.cs.cmu.edu/spring2025/slides/13-cardinalities1.pdf#:~:text=SPECIAL%20TOPICS%20,of%20tuples%20in%20its%20input)[\[83\]](https://www.red-gate.com/simple-talk/databases/oracle-databases/basics-of-the-cost-based-optimizer-part-6/#:~:text=Basics%20of%20the%20Cost%20Based,hints%20to%20help%20the). Additionally, until confidence in the CBO is high, databases often provide a toggle - Geode did exactly that (an opt-in flag). Our goal is to remove that flag by v0.19, which means addressing the scenarios where CBO could regress performance.

Another known area is **cardinality estimation bias** on small tables or highly skewed distributions. The evidence-based approach in Geode (CANARY tests) might already track some CBO accuracy metrics - e.g., the gap analysis shows numerous aspects of cost model calibration being tested[\[84\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/GAP_ANALYSIS.md#L18-L28)[\[85\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/GAP_ANALYSIS.md#L26-L34). We likely need to refine these calibrations with additional data collection (like capturing actual vs estimated row counts to adjust the model). The fact that the CBO was introduced in v0.17 as "v1" suggests it has room to improve in terms of plan quality and stability under diverse conditions[\[20\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L25-L31).

**Design Proposal (SQ2):** Several improvements and guardrails will be implemented: - **Calibrate for Small N:** We will adjust the cost model to avoid fancy plans on small inputs. Concretely, introduce a threshold below which the optimizer falls back to simpler strategies. For example, if a table or intermediate result is estimated to have < X rows (say 50 or 100), we might skip certain optimizations (like avoid an index scan's overhead if a sequential scan would touch few pages anyway). This can be done via _heuristic override_ - a common approach in industry where cost models have "fudge factors" or special rules for small cardinalities[\[52\]](https://www.cockroachlabs.com/blog/building-cost-based-sql-optimizer/#:~:text=The%20first%20approach%20is%20what,is%20called%20a%20heuristic%20optimizer)[\[50\]](https://www.cockroachlabs.com/blog/building-cost-based-sql-optimizer/#:~:text=nested%20loop%20join%20if%20an,is%20called%20a%20heuristic%20optimizer). We'll verify default statistics assumptions to ensure the optimizer doesn't wildly overestimate small tables (which could lead to wrong join order). If needed, we could set a minimum cost or a flat cost for very small operations so as not to differentiate too much between them. - **Improved Stats & Estimation:** The CBO's quality hinges on stats. We will improve the **statistics collection**, particularly on edge cases: e.g., ensure that if a table has very few rows, the stats reflect that exactly (maybe even up to date counts rather than stale). Possibly implement automatic update of stats on small tables each time data changes (since cost is low). For skewed data, consider capturing a histogram or at least min/max values to detect if a filter is highly selective or not. These refinements will help the CBO avoid misestimation that lead to bad plans. Our acceptance target was "prediction accuracy >85%" in the roadmap[\[37\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L19-L25) - we'll achieve that by gathering more sample data for estimates and using better estimation techniques (like using index statistics if available for range queries, etc.). - **Plan Search Guardrails:** We will impose certain sanity checks on the chosen plan. For instance, if the estimated cost difference between the best plan and second-best is tiny (within some epsilon), we might favor the plan with fewer pipeline breakers or that is simpler (to avoid falling into a complex plan that doesn't actually yield much benefit). Similarly, ensure the optimizer doesn't choose extremely right-deep or bushy join trees if not needed (the current implementation does left-deep enumeration[\[53\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L26-L31) which is fine). - **Adaptive Execution (future idea):** We might not fully implement this by v0.19, but we consider an "adaptive" approach where if a plan's cardinality estimates turn out very wrong at runtime, we could log that or even adjust future decisions. For example, if the CBO consistently underestimates a join output (leading to a bad plan), we could detect that and adjust stats or switch plan mid-flight. That's a complex feature (adaptive query processing), likely beyond immediate scope. However, at minimum we'll incorporate **feedback into stats**: after query execution, update stats with actual row counts (as some systems do for improving future plans). - **Enable by Default Criteria:** We will initially still default CBO off in v0.18 but encourage users to test it. Meanwhile, we'll run extensive performance tests. The criteria to flip it on in v0.19 will be: (a) no known severe plan regressions on our benchmark suite, (b) evidence that on average CBO gives significantly better performance on complex queries, and (c) introduction of a failsafe (like a session-level knob to disable CBO if needed, so in worst-case a user can revert to heuristic for a problematic query). Essentially, we want users to not have to think about it, but also not be stuck if an odd case arises. - **Exposure of Plan/Stats Info:** To build trust and aid debugging, we'll improve **EXPLAIN output** (or add one if not present). For a given query, the user (or our dev team) should be able to see the chosen plan with costs and cardinality estimates vs actual. This will help identify any remaining misestimation quickly. It's also a standard feature for databases, so adding it aligns with user expectations. We might output in JSON or textual form the plan tree with cost metrics. - **Heuristic Fallbacks:** In case the stats are absent or very uncertain, the CBO can fall back to heuristic rules that are known safe. For example, if two join orders have almost equal cost or stats are missing, default to a rule like "join smaller table first" or use the pre-v0.17 heuristic result. This ensures even if stats are off, the plan won't be catastrophically wrong because it'll mimic a sensible rule. This hybrid approach (heuristics guard the cost model) is common in early optimizers[\[86\]](https://www.cockroachlabs.com/blog/building-cost-based-sql-optimizer/#:~:text=So%20how%20do%20optimizers%20choose,common%20approaches%20to%20the%20problem)[\[87\]](https://www.cockroachlabs.com/blog/building-cost-based-sql-optimizer/#:~:text=However%2C%20static%20heuristic%20rules%20have,heuristics%20that%20capture%20these%20subtleties).

**Standards & Expectations (SQ3):** While query optimization isn't externally mandated by a standard, ISO SQL/GQL does define some syntactic ways to influence optimizer (like **QUERY PLAN** clause or hints in some implementations). We might not implement hints yet, but enabling CBO by default should be done in a way that doesn't change query results - only performance. We'll adhere to the principle that whatever plan is chosen still yields the correct output as defined by GQL semantics (which is obvious but worth stating - e.g., we must ensure that reordering joins doesn't break something like a non-associative operator; fortunately, in relational algebra and GQL MATCH, reordering of patterns is valid since it's basically join commutativity, which holds for these).

We also consider compliance with user expectations: by defaulting to CBO, we should ensure it doesn't violate any determinism or any debug flows. For instance, if users expect consistent performance, we must ensure the CBO doesn't produce plan instability (where slight data changes cause wildly different plans oscillating). This is more an experience standard - to handle it, we might introduce a bit of **hysteresis** (not flipping plan choices frequently if estimates haven't changed significantly). Some systems do this to avoid flip-flopping plans. It's not in ISO, but is a practice for stability.

One more angle: cost-based decisions often need to consider not just row count but also available **resources** (like parallelism or memory). If multi-threading is introduced, the CBO might later need to decide when to use a parallel plan vs single-thread. For now, we assume single-thread plans, but we'll design the CBO improvements in a way that could extend to costing parallel operators in the future (e.g., account for CPU cost, which can be divided by number of threads if we parallelize a scan, etc.). This forward-compatibility ensures our CBO remains useful as we add those features.

**Risks & Complexity (SQ4):** - **Cost Model Inaccuracy:** Even after improvements, the cost model is inherently an approximation. There's risk it could still make a bad choice in unanticipated scenarios (maybe a very skewed data distribution that our histogram didn't capture, causing underestimation). To mitigate blow-up risk, we'll implement **safety nets**: e.g., limit the join order search space for very complex queries (so it doesn't pick a truly worst-case plan among many tables) - basically ensure it doesn't pick Cartesian joins unless absolutely no other way. Also we can set a time-out for optimization phase: if the search space is enormous (10+ tables), better to stop and pick a decent plan than to spend minutes optimizing. - **Regressions on simple queries:** If not careful, enabling CBO could add overhead to simple queries (planning time overhead or picking a complex plan where trivial plan would do). We'll measure planning latency - it should be negligible (< milliseconds typically, given small number of joins in typical graph patterns). If we see planning taking significant time for large queries, we might consider plan caching or reducing search complexity for large join counts. Geode already has a plan cache with structural keys[\[53\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L26-L31)[\[49\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L75-L79) - making CBO default means more use of that cache, which should help repeated queries. We'll verify the cache doesn't cause issues (like memory bloat or stale plans if stats change - probably not huge concern for now as stats likely static or updated manually). - **User perception:** Some power users might have tuned the heuristic planner's performance (perhaps by writing queries in certain ways). When we switch to CBO, the plan might change and could surprise them. However, since the aim is better performance, this should be positive or neutral surprise in most cases. We'll include a note in release notes that CBO is now on by default and how to disable it if needed (for edge cases or debugging). - **Edge cases:** Queries with little data or trivial workload might see the overhead of cost calculation without any benefit. E.g., MATCH (n) RETURN count(n) on a tiny graph - CBO will do the same plan but took some microseconds to decide. This is minor but we consider if there's any scenario where CBO might actually produce a worse plan.
