One might be if stats are outdated after data changes - e.g., if a table grew big but stats still small, the CBO might choose a plan as if it's small (bad). Our mitigation is to encourage updating stats (or implement auto-stats collection when threshold crossed). We may also include a fail-safe: if during execution it's clear estimates were far off (like join produced 1000× more rows than estimated), log a warning or update stats for next time. This doesn't save current query but helps future ones. - **Testing complexity:** We need a broad set of test queries (including random graph patterns) to ensure CBO picks the expected good plan in each. Possibly use known graph benchmark queries (LDBC SNB has complex ones) to test. Since performance is the criterion, tests must verify not just correctness but that the chosen plan is within a factor of optimal runtime. Setting up that optimal baseline is tricky - we may do comparative runs with CBO on vs off on various data sizes. The risk is some cases only show up at scale (like a certain join order becomes bad only on certain data distributions). We mitigate by including varied distributions in test (uniform vs skew, small vs large). Still, it's hard to catch everything - that's why keeping a way to toggle CBO off provides an emergency bypass if an unforeseen regression occurs in production. - **Integration with Federation & Concurrency:** If these come in parallel (federation in v0.18, concurrency in v0.19), the CBO might eventually need to be aware of them. For now, the CBO can assume single-node execution. But by v0.19, if distributed queries are common, we might need the CBO to cost remote data access (network IO) vs local, etc. That's advanced and likely for a later version. In v0.18/0.19 timeframe, we might treat distributed execution plans separately (maybe using simpler heuristics for which parts to push down). So there's a risk our CBO is initially only focusing on local planning, and distributed queries might not be fully cost-optimized. That's acceptable initially (functional correctness first), but on our radar to incorporate network cost later.

**Benchmarks & Acceptance Criteria (SQ5):** We will measure improvement by constructing a suite of **challenging queries** and data scenarios, including: - **Join Order Test:** Create a scenario with, say, 4-5 vertex types with various connection patterns (like a chain A-B-C-D-E), and vary the size of each (A: 1k nodes, B: 10k, C: 100, D: 5k, E: 100k, etc.). Query connecting all (e.g., a pattern through A-B-C-D-E). The worst plan would join largest first, best plan joins smallest first, etc. We'll see how the CBO orders them. Success is CBO chooses a near-optimal ordering (we know the sizes so we can predict best order). We can run the query and confirm performance is much better with CBO vs without. For example, if heuristic did a poor join order that takes 10s, and optimal is 0.1s, we want CBO to achieve ~0.1s. We'll design multiple such scenarios. - **Small vs Large CBO behavior:** We'll test the same query on small dataset vs large dataset. For a trivial dataset, ensure the overhead of CBO doesn't make it slower than if we just did it (maybe measure CPU time difference when only a few rows - it should be negligible, but we'll verify the threshold logic that maybe stops considering too many plans for trivial cases). - **Plan Stability:** Using a given dataset, run a set of queries before and after enabling CBO and record their runtimes. We expect no significant regressions. We define acceptance as "no query in our benchmark is more than 2× slower with CBO on compared to off" (and ideally none are slower at all - possibly equal or faster). Meanwhile, many should be faster with CBO. We specifically include queries known to benefit (like multi-join queries, or queries with selective filters where index usage is important). If any query does slow down with CBO, we analyze why - it might highlight a misestimation that we can fix or decide to implement a special case for. - **Estimation Accuracy:** We'll instrument the CBO to output estimated vs actual rows for each operator for test queries (likely through EXPLAIN ANALYZE). We'll collect stats on estimation error (perhaps mean error or % of operators with error > factor of 2). Our goal is that in most cases estimates are within a small factor so that the chosen plan is right. The acceptance target from Next.md was _prediction accuracy >85%_[\[37\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L19-L25) - meaning perhaps 85% of plan nodes have accurate cardinality within some tolerance. We'll use that as a guideline. In tests, if we find systematic biases (e.g., always underestimating for certain pattern or always overestimating for small tables), we'll adjust the model or add bias correction terms. - **Complex Query Performance:** Use something like LDBC SNB complex analytical query (which has multiple hops and filters). Run it on a reasonably sized synthetic graph. Check that with CBO, the query finishes in feasible time, whereas maybe without CBO it times out or is much slower. Essentially, demonstrate that CBO enables scaling to more complex queries gracefully. For acceptance, if a known benchmark query can't run efficiently without CBO but runs well with it, that's a strong signal we succeeded. - **Default-On Soak:** To further build confidence, we will run our full regression test suite with CBO forced on and ensure all queries still produce correct results (which they should - optimizer changes shouldn't affect correctness). We'll also monitor test runtime; it should ideally shrink or remain similar for test queries. If a particular test query gets slower, investigate that case. A soak test on a snapshot of user-like workload or random query generation with CBO on, running for hours, could be done to see if any extreme plan appears (maybe hooking into our benchmark tests which already cover core operations with performance targets[\[19\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L75-L83)). We have performance benchmarks from v0.17 (like join selection up to 10× improvement)[\[49\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L75-L79) - those presumably used CBO in ideal scenarios. We ensure those improvements are consistently realized with CBO default (no regressions from v0.17 results).

**Acceptance criteria:** - **Performance Uplift:** On our benchmark suite, enabling CBO by default yields overall better performance. Specifically, complex query runtime is reduced significantly (e.g., by >50% in cases with multiple joins or selective filters) compared to the heuristic planner. And no major outliers where performance is worse beyond an acceptable margin. - **Plan Quality:** The join orders and index choices made by the CBO should align with intuitive expectations given the data distribution (when checked via EXPLAIN, we see it picking the smaller or more selective first, etc.). Any glaringly wrong choices must be resolved. - **No Crashes/Correctness Issues:** Obviously, turning on CBO should not cause any query to fail or return wrong data. We will test with tricky queries (including ones with subqueries, OPTIONAL matches, etc., which the CBO should handle). - **Statistics Use:** The CBO now uses a broader set of stats. We consider it a pass if, for example, adding stats on a property or having histogram data actually changes a plan in a beneficial way (we could test by forcing different stats and seeing plan changes, demonstrating the CBO is responsive to stats). - **Default Behavior:** We will flip the default in a staging config and run all integration tests. The expectation is all tests still pass in roughly the same time or faster. Once we do that, we know we can safely have it on for users. The final check is documentation and messaging: ensure users know they can still disable it with a flag if needed (for edge scenarios or debugging), but likely they won't have to. - **Feature Parity:** Make sure that features previously supported under heuristic planning are still supported under CBO. E.g., if certain MATCH constructs or subqueries were only handled in the heuristic planner but not integrated into cost logic, ensure they are now (or at least default to a safe plan if not cost-optimized). We don't want any query failing because the CBO path didn't cover it. If needed, certain query patterns can bypass CBO (the optimizer can detect "I don't handle this well, use fallback plan"). Over time that should diminish as we cover all cases.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** Refining CBO may require: - **Enhanced Stats Collection:** We may implement an ANALYZE command or automatic stat update mechanism. This might involve scanning data or using background collection. We need the storage engine to provide table sizes, distinct counts, etc. Possibly extending our existing Statistics Registry[\[20\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L25-L31) to keep more info (like multi-column stats if needed, though not likely for v0.19). - **Catalog changes:** If we change how stats are stored or need to persist them (so they survive restarts), we'll adjust system catalogs or meta files. - The CANARY tests for CBO (if any) might need updating as we change algorithms (ensuring tests expect improved biases). - **Plan Cache adjustments:** If enabling CBO, the plan cache might need to incorporate the fact that with different stats, the plan could change. Currently, plan cache keys are structural; that's fine. But we should consider invalidating cached plans if stats are updated (so that a new optimal plan can be computed). We might implement a simple mechanism: bump a cache epoch when stats on a table updated. Not strictly necessary for v0.19 if auto-stats aren't frequent, but good to have to avoid using a stale plan. - **Integration with Federation:** As mentioned, initially the CBO might not consider distributing a plan; however, if by v0.19 we want to cost remote data, we might treat each shard's data as source with additional cost. This is complex (like what Citus or Spanner's optimizer does). Possibly out of scope for now, but need to keep it in mind. For now, maybe just do simple rule: if a query is distributed, do local optimization on each shard and then a known merge step - not fully costed but fixed strategy. That is acceptable until we gather more data. The dependency is minimal because we can separate concerns (the federation code can decide distribution and call local CBO for fragments). - **Testing Infrastructure:** Possibly we need to enhance our performance test harness to measure query times and verify plans. We might integrate something like a micro-benchmark suite for common query patterns (maybe repurpose the existing performance tests which track variance[\[88\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L50-L58)). Ensuring variance targets still hold with CBO on is important (those tests likely assert that no regression beyond tolerance occurs).

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - In **v0.18.0**, we will deliver most of the CBO improvements behind the scenes but likely keep it **opt-in** (perhaps make the opt-in easier or more recommended). We will encourage advanced users to try enabling CBO and maybe gather feedback (especially if they find a query where CBO did something bad). This period acts as a quasi-beta for default-on. We will also use v0.18's own new features (like federation, concurrency) as an opportunity to test CBO in more scenarios. - We'll add more logging under debug for the optimizer decisions (like log estimates and chosen plans if a trace flag is set). This will help both us and power users understand what the CBO is doing. - By **v0.19.0**, if all acceptance criteria are met, we will make **CBO enabled by default** (flip the default of GEODE_ENABLE_CBO to true or remove that env var entirely). We will highlight this in release notes as a major improvement and note that significant testing has been done to ensure it's beneficial. We'll also provide guidance: e.g., if a user suspects a query plan issue, how to diagnose (use EXPLAIN, possibly how to disable CBO for that query if needed, though ideally not). - We plan a **feature flag fallback**: For safety in v0.19, we might not immediately remove the ability to disable CBO (in case an unforeseen regression is discovered by a user, they can set an env var to use the simpler planner as a workaround until a fix). But our aim is that such cases are rare or none. - Observability improvement: We might add a metric like "optimizer mode used" or "number of queries optimized by CBO vs heuristic" for debugging usage in the field. Probably not needed beyond development telemetry. - The roll-out will include documentation updates: explain how the CBO works at a high level, how to update stats for it, etc., so users trust it. Perhaps publish a small blog or whitepaper referencing our evidence (similar to Cockroach's blog explaining their optimizer journey[\[89\]](https://www.cockroachlabs.com/blog/building-cost-based-sql-optimizer/#:~:text=Here%20at%20Cockroach%20Labs%2C%20we%E2%80%99ve,optimizer%20and%20prioritize%20future%20work)) to instill confidence.

By making the CBO robust and default, Geode takes a step into maturity where the system itself finds efficient query plans without manual tuning, which is expected from modern databases. The improvements planned will ensure that enabling the CBO will not cause surprises, only performance gains, thereby fulfilling the promise mentioned in v0.17 notes (join selection yielding up to 10× improvement)[\[49\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L75-L79) uniformly across workloads.

### 4\. CDC Webhook Sink Hardening (Custom Headers & Reliability)

**Why It Matters - User Impact (SQ1):** Geode's **Change Data Capture (CDC)** feature allows streaming updates to external systems via "sinks". The **Webhook sink** in v0.17 sends HTTP POSTs of change events to user-defined endpoints[\[54\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L32-L40). However, it currently lacks the ability to set **custom HTTP headers** on those requests[\[75\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L158-L160). Many real-world webhooks require headers - for example, an Authorization: Bearer &lt;token&gt; header for authentication, or custom headers for routing (like X-Project-ID) or message signatures. Without header support, Geode's webhook sink cannot directly integrate with secured APIs (which typically reject requests without proper auth headers). This severely limits the usefulness of the CDC webhook, as production endpoints are usually protected. By adding custom header support, we enable a range of use-cases: - Streaming changes into SaaS services or microservices that expect an API key or JWT in the header. - Including metadata in each event (like an event type, version, or idempotency key) that the consumer needs, beyond the JSON payload. For instance, certain systems use a header as an idempotency token to ensure exactly-once processing[\[56\]](https://hookdeck.com/webhooks/guides/implement-webhook-idempotency#:~:text=How%20to%20Implement%20Webhook%20Idempotency,multiple%20times%2C%20ensuring%20data%20integrity)[\[57\]](https://www.moderntreasury.com/journal/webhook-idempotency#:~:text=Webhook%20Idempotency%20,which%20webhooks%20have%20already). - Potentially signing requests (some webhooks include an HMAC signature in a header; while Geode might not generate that automatically without a secret, at least we could allow a static signature if needed).

Additionally, we want to harden the CDC sink's **reliability and observability**. v0.17 implemented retries with exponential backoff and a dead-letter queue (DLQ) for failures[\[58\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L34-L38), which is good. But we should ensure those mechanisms are configurable and transparent. For example, an operator should be able to see if the webhook is failing often (through metrics) and perhaps tune the retry policy (how many retries, the backoff schedule) to match their endpoint's needs. Also, currently if an endpoint requires custom headers for idempotency (like Idempotency-Key)[\[57\]](https://www.moderntreasury.com/journal/webhook-idempotency#:~:text=Webhook%20Idempotency%20,which%20webhooks%20have%20already), we can now support that to further ensure exactly-once processing on the receiver side.

In summary, these enhancements benefit **integrators and DevOps**: They can connect Geode CDC to secured endpoints reliably, have confidence events won't be lost or duplicated excessively, and monitor the delivery pipeline. Without these, one would have to put an unsecured intermediary or forgo CDC, which is not acceptable for production (security and reliability are top concerns in change data capture pipelines).

**Design Proposal (SQ2):** - **Custom Headers Configuration:** We will extend the CDC sink configuration to accept arbitrary headers. This might be via the config file (e.g., cdc.webhook.headers = "Authorization: Bearer abc123, X-Client: Geode" in a properties file) or via an admin command/API. The headers will be stored securely in memory. If a header value contains secrets (like an API token), we should avoid logging it in plain text. The implementation will simply add these headers to every HTTP request the webhook sink sends. We should support multiple headers and ensure the formatting is correct (HTTP standard). - **Templating Headers (potential):** Possibly, allow placeholder values like including the event's partition or some metadata in a header. This is advanced and maybe unnecessary now, but could consider a simple substitution (not in scope unless user asks). - **Idempotency Key:** We can generate an Idempotency-Key header for each event automatically, using the event's unique ID (maybe a combination of transaction id and sequence). This helps downstream consumers avoid processing duplicates on retries[\[57\]](https://www.moderntreasury.com/journal/webhook-idempotency#:~:text=Webhook%20Idempotency%20,which%20webhooks%20have%20already). Many payment or stateful APIs use this header. We will include this by default for each request unless disabled. Since Geode already assigns some sort of CDC event identifier (it could use the Raft log index or an internal sequence), we can reuse that. So on each POST, set Idempotency-Key: &lt;eventId&gt;. This is safe even if the endpoint doesn't use it (it'll just ignore it), and beneficial if it does. We'll document that we do this, and possibly allow overriding it or disabling if needed. - **Enhanced Retry/Backoff Controls:** Currently, the sink uses exponential backoff with fixed limits[\[58\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L34-L38). We will expose configuration for: max retry attempts, initial retry delay, max delay, and maybe a linear backoff option. For example, user might want a shorter retry interval if their endpoint is quick to recover, or fewer retries if they prefer failing fast. We'll implement this in the sink logic, defaulting to sensible values (e.g., initial 1s, exponential factor 2x, max attempts 5, max delay 30s). Also consider **non-retryable status codes**: Many webhook systems do not retry on client errors (400-499) because those indicate a bad request (except maybe 429 Too Many Requests, which often should be retried after a delay). We should implement logic: if response status is 2xx -> success (ack), 5xx or network error -> retry (transient), 4xx (except 429) -> do not retry, treat as permanent failure and perhaps log to DLQ immediately[\[90\]](https://stackoverflow.com/questions/79612180/jira-webhook-idempotency-key#:~:text=Jira%20Webhook%20idempotency%20key%20,not%20hit%20my%20webhook%20endpoint). This prevents flooding an endpoint with retries on a bad request (like auth failure 401 - retrying won't fix until configuration is fixed). We'll move such events to DLQ after one attempt for non-retriables. If we want to be configurable, allow listing status codes to retry or not. - **Observability & Metrics:** We will add metrics for the webhook sink, such as: - Number of events sent, - Number of failures/retries, - Current backlog (events waiting to be sent or in DLQ), - Perhaps latency distribution of webhook calls. These will be exposed via our metrics system (maybe JMX or an HTTP stats endpoint). This allows ops to create alerts like "webhook failures >0 in last 5 min" to detect integration issues. We'll also include more detailed logging at appropriate levels: e.g., an info log if a batch of events fails and will be retried, a warning if events are moved to DLQ after final retries with some context of why (HTTP status). - **Threading and Throughput:** Currently, it's likely single-threaded (processing CDC events sequentially). This ensures in-order delivery which is good. But if one endpoint is slow, events queue up. We will keep single-thread by default to preserve ordering. However, we might allow parallelization if the user doesn't care about order or has independent streams (maybe in future, partition CDC by table, etc.). For now, stick to one worker thread to maintain ordering guarantees: events are delivered in the commit order they occurred (which is usually desired). - **Backpressure & DLQ Handling:** If the endpoint is down for an extended period, our queue of events in memory could grow. We already have a DLQ concept (likely an internal file or memory store where events go after retries are exhausted)[\[58\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L34-L38). We will ensure that queue is bounded; if it grows beyond a threshold, we might drop oldest events or pause ingestion (this scenario indicates a serious issue - we should alert rather than silently drop). Possibly we set a high limit like thousands of events can buffer; beyond that, either apply backpressure (slow down transactions - not ideal) or start discarding to DLQ with an error. We'll refine this logic and document it. - **Manual DLQ replay**: Not necessarily implementing now, but plan: an operator should be able to inspect the DLQ (the saved failed events) and maybe retry them once the endpoint is fixed (or route them elsewhere). We will make the DLQ a visible thing (maybe stored as a file or accessible via an admin command). In this iteration, we at least make sure failed events are logged (including key info like their ID) so one can manually reprocess them if needed (pull from logs or archived file).

**Standards & Security (SQ3):** - **Security compliance:** By enabling auth headers, we are aligning with standard API security practices (tokens in Authorization header, etc.). We will advise using secure transport (which we already do: the webhook uses HTTPS by default if the URL is https). We should ensure that if user provides a header like "Authorization: Bearer X", we treat that data carefully (do not log the full value). Possibly we'll mask sensitive header values in logs (e.g., log "Authorization: Bearer **_\* (masked)" if logging request headers for debug). -_ \*Reliability best practices:** We are implementing the common pattern for webhooks: at-least-once delivery with **idempotency tokens** to achieve effectively-once on consumer[\[57\]](https://www.moderntreasury.com/journal/webhook-idempotency#:~:text=Webhook%20Idempotency%20,which%20webhooks%20have%20already), **exponential backoff with DLQ** to avoid overwhelming endpoints and to not block indefinitely, and **non-retry on client errors** to prevent pointless spam. These match recommendations from sources like Stripe or AWS blogs on webhooks (e.g., Stripe sends each event with an Idempotency-Key and doesn't retry 400s). - **HTTP compliance:** We'll adhere to HTTP standards in our implementation: properly format headers (no illegal characters), follow redirect if needed (maybe up to a limit), and set a sensible User-Agent (perhaps "GeodeCDC/0.18"). For highlights, we likely won't do chunked encoding or anything fancy; simple one-shot POST with JSON body is fine. - **ISO/Regulatory:** Not directly applicable, but reliability can tie into SLAs. We might document that our CDC mechanism aims for at-least-once, and if exactly-once is needed, the consumer must deduplicate using provided IDs (which we facilitate via idempotency keys). Also, note that enabling headers might include PII or sensitive data (like an auth token) - we store in memory only and not expose, which is fine under security guidelines. If multi-tenancy in future, we'll ensure each tenant's webhook config is isolated (not a concern now). - **Compatibility:** The changes are backward-compatible (except maybe if someone was using the webhook without headers, nothing changes except now they can add). If someone expected it to retry on 400, our change might alter that (but that's a correct fix; 400 is non-transient). We'll note this in release notes ("Webhook sink now does not retry on client errors except 429 by default").

**Risks & Complexity (SQ4):** - **Misconfiguration:** If the user configures headers incorrectly (typos in header names or values), the endpoint might reject the requests. We can't fully prevent that, but we can validate header format on startup and log an error if it looks wrong (e.g., missing ":" separator). Also, if an auth fails (401), we'll now not retry (with our new logic), so if they put wrong token, events go to DLQ immediately - which is actually appropriate. They'll have to fix config and possibly replay from DLQ. We'll document to test connectivity (maybe we can provide a "test webhook" function that sends a ping to the endpoint with headers to verify 200 OK). - **Performance Overhead:** Adding headers is trivial overhead. The main performance aspect is if enabling idempotency key (computing a unique key per event). We can likely use the Raft log index or a simple atomic counter or UUID per event, which is negligible overhead. So no big performance risk. If anything, heavy logging or metrics in the loop could slow things if events are extremely high volume (like thousands per second). But CDC typically is not extremely high throughput (and if it is, user likely uses Kafka sink not webhook). - **Thread Safety & Concurrency:** If we implement this in the same single-thread model as now, thread safety isn't a new issue. If we decide in future to parallelize webhooks, we have to ensure ordering or allow out-of-order semantics optionally. For now, remain single-threaded in delivery order, which is simpler and safe. The code is likely running in a background thread separate from Raft or main threads, which is fine. - **Memory Growth:** If the endpoint is down, events buffer. We need to ensure memory is bounded. We likely already have some limit or the DLQ concept effectively bounds it by offloading to disk. If not, we'll add a reasonable limit (maybe configurable). Risk is if too many events accumulate, could OOM or cause high memory usage. We'll test scenario of endpoint down with high update rate to see how our system reacts (should eventually put events in DLQ or drop them after retries). We will be cautious to avoid memory bloat. - **Complex failure logic:** With new conditions (not retrying on 4xx, etc.), we must carefully implement to avoid, say, accidentally not retrying a 429 that should be retried. Also, we must consider if the user's endpoint might throw some specific codes (like 418 or something unusual); we default those as client errors not to retry. That's fine typically. We'll follow common practice: 500-599 and networking timeouts are retried, 408 Request Timeout maybe retried, 429 retried (with perhaps respect to Retry-After header if provided - as a nice touch, if 429 has a header telling how long to wait, we could use that instead of exponential). This complexity is manageable and adds robustness if done. - **Testing complexity:** We need to simulate various conditions: the endpoint returns 200 (success), 500 (should retry), 404 (should not retry), slow responses, network timeouts, etc., to confirm our logic. Also test that custom headers indeed arrive at the server (we can use a dummy HTTP server in tests to inspect requests). We'll test the idempotency key presence and uniqueness. We'll test DLQ by forcing permanent failures and see that events land there. - **Stalled pipeline handling:** If the endpoint is slow but not failing, our single-thread sender could become a bottleneck. E.g., endpoint takes 2 seconds per event, and events come in faster. We'd queue up and eventually backlog. In such cases, at-least-once means we might fall behind real-time significantly. We might want to detect if backlog is growing and warn the user (some metric or log "CDC webhook falling behind, X events queued"). We could consider multiple threads to catch up backlog faster once endpoint is healthy, but out-of-order would break strict ordering. We might accept that if they get that far behind, they have a problem to solve (scale endpoint or accept lag). It's a design trade-off. For now, keep it simple (one thread). - **Idempotency semantics:** We assign an idempotency key to ensure receiver dedups. However, if the receiver doesn't implement idempotency and processes duplicates anyway, that's on them. We can't guarantee exactly-once delivery because if our process crashes after sending but before acknowledging internally, we might resend on restart. Idempotency keys mitigate this. We should document that exactly-once requires consumer support via these keys (which we now provide). So risk of duplicate processing remains if consumer isn't built for it. That's inherent; we just make it easier to handle.

**Benchmarks & Acceptance Criteria (SQ5):** Functional tests will drive acceptance: - **Header injection test:** Configure a dummy HTTP server (could be an Echo server in our test) and set custom headers in Geode config. Perform some DB change that triggers CDC. Verify that the HTTP request the server received contains exactly the headers configured (and no unexpected ones aside from defaults). For a security header (auth), ensure it's present. This confirms functionality. - **Auth integration test:** Perhaps simulate a scenario: server requires a header X-Token: secret. If not present, it responds 401. We configure Geode with that header and send an event. Without header, event should move to DLQ due to 401 (no retry); with header, it should succeed (200). This tests both header and the non-retry logic on 401 and that when corrected, it goes through. - **Retry logic test:** Have the server respond with 500 for the first N attempts and 200 on the (N+1)th. Ensure Geode retries N times then succeeds on (N+1)th. Check the delays between attempts are roughly exponential (we can timestamp in server). If server returns 400, ensure Geode doesn't retry (only one attempt then DLQ). - **429 Too Many Requests test:** Server returns 429 with Retry-After: 5 header. Expect Geode to treat it as retryable. Ideally, we implement reading that header - we can test if we did. If not implemented, it will just exponential backoff like normal (which is okay, but better if we handle it). If implemented, we expect next attempt ~5 seconds later as instructed. - **DLQ & idempotency test:** Simulate persistent failures to ensure events go to DLQ after final retry. Then fix the endpoint and attempt to manually replay (perhaps by re-inserting events or by a tool). If we implement a replay command, test that; if not, just ensure events are logged or stored where we expect. For idempotency, we will have the test server record the Idempotency-Key of each request. If we deliberately cause a duplicate (like simulate an app crash and resend same event), the server logic can check if it's a repeat key and not double-process. We verify that the keys are indeed identical for the same event on retry, and unique across different events. That confirms our idempotency key mechanism. - **Throughput/Performance test:** Fire a large number of small transactions into the system and measure the CDC throughput. If the endpoint is a fast local dummy, see how many events per second we can push through. It should handle a reasonably high rate (hundreds per sec) depending on network. If too slow, see if overhead is our code or just HTTP overhead. It should mostly be network-bound or endpoint-bound. We set maybe a baseline that adding CDC with an available endpoint doesn't degrade transaction throughput beyond X%. (Likely minor impact since CDC is async). We can also simulate endpoint slowness and see that we backpressure appropriately (e.g., measure queue length metric if exposed). - **Concurrency correctness:** If two transactions occur close together, ensure their events maintain order in delivery. We can test ordering by tagging events with an incrementing counter in payload and seeing that the server receives them in that order. - **Resource usage:** Monitor memory when endpoint is down and many events occur. Ensure memory doesn't grow unbounded (it might for a while until DLQ kicks in). We might artificially limit in test (like allow 100 events then drop). We should see that after hitting limit, events get dropped or queued to DLQ and memory usage stabilizes. Also CPU usage of retry logic is minimal (just sleeps between tries). - **Metrics observability:** If possible in test, query the metrics (if in memory, maybe via an API or direct call) to ensure that counters increment (like "failed_count" goes up by number of failures, etc.). Or simpler, rely on logs: e.g., when final fail goes to DLQ, a warning log is emitted with event ID. We can assert that was logged. Likewise, when retries are happening, maybe debug logs show it. If metrics are not easily queryable in test, manual verification in a staging environment could be done.

**Acceptance criteria:** - **Header support works seamlessly:** Users can specify headers and they are present in outbound requests correctly. This will be confirmed via testing multiple header variations (auth, content-type override if needed, etc.). - **No regression in delivery:** The addition of headers does not break the core delivery logic - events still eventually get delivered or DLQ'd, exactly as before or better. - **Enhanced reliability:** The system no longer wastes time retrying non-transient errors - we treat that as achieved if our tests show 4xx lead to one attempt then DLQ. - **No event loss without DLQ:** All events that cannot be delivered are accounted for in DLQ or logs; none are silently dropped. At-least-once semantics hold. We will inspect that if an event was supposed to be sent and the endpoint was down, it either is pending, in retry, or in DLQ, but never just disappears. (We might simulate a crash after adding to DLQ to ensure DLQ is persistent or at least logged - likely DLQ is in-memory in v0.17? Actually, "Dead Letter Queue" implies they store them, maybe in a file or a separate log. If not, we might need to implement storing to disk or at least memory structure surviving until manual retrieval.) - **Idempotency keys on duplicates:** On retries of the same event, the Idempotency-Key header remains identical. And different events have different keys. We verify this reliability. This means a consumer can safely de-dup if needed. - **Observability:** If we provide metrics or clear logs, an operator can tell if the webhook is failing. We treat as pass if, for instance, our logs clearly show "Webhook delivery failed for Event X (HTTP 404) - moved to dead letter queue" and a metric cdc_webhook_failures_total increments. This way, nothing fails silently. Ideally, we also provide a way to monitor backlog (maybe cdc_webhook_queue_length). - **Performance overhead minimal:** With CDC enabled (and endpoint up), the overhead on commit latency is minimal since we do async. We'll assert that enabling CDC doesn't slow down writes significantly in our perf test (should be the case as it's decoupled). If our test sees any major slowdown, that's a problem to fix (maybe synchronization issues). - **Documentation & Usage**: The feature is considered done when documentation is updated to show how to configure custom headers (with examples for common use like Authorization), how the retry/backoff works now (with mention of idempotency and non-retry of 4xx), and how to monitor CDC. This ensures users can fully leverage it.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** Implementing this will mainly touch the CDC module: - We may need an HTTP client library if not already in use (the sink likely uses Zig's HTTP or some minimal implementation). We can likely craft raw HTTP with TLS since it's just one endpoint - maybe the code already does that via a simple approach. Adding headers is straightforward string addition. - For metrics, if we have a metrics library integrated (maybe using Zig's metrics or exporting to Prometheus), we'll add counters/gauges as needed. If not, we might just rely on logs which is less ideal. Possibly incorporate a simple metrics collector or piggyback on existing ones (maybe the database already tracks some metrics - e.g., we saw mention of metrics export in tests[\[91\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L108-L110)). - Possibly a lightweight JSON library if needed to parse and manipulate some things (shouldn't be needed, we mainly forward the event JSON as given). - We should ensure thread-safety of any global counters if we have multi-thread context. But if one thread doing all, not an issue. - If implementing persistent DLQ store, that introduces dependency on either writing to a file or storing in an internal table. A quick route: write DLQ events to a local file in append mode (each line maybe JSON of the event and some metadata). This is durable and can be read later. It's not complex; we just have to format lines safely. This ensures if Geode restarts, those failed events are not lost (so user can manually re-ingest them if needed). - If we go further, perhaps integrate a small part of an HTTP library that can do e.g., reading Retry-After header. But that's trivial to parse if needed. - A way to get config input: presumably, Geode has config for CDC sinks (maybe in a config file or passed via command-line). We'll extend that. If a user can dynamically add a header at runtime, that might be not needed; static config is fine as these are usually static tokens. We will parse that config at startup and apply.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** This feature is relatively self-contained and low-risk, so we plan to include it in **v0.18.0** as a minor enhancement (not behind any flag, just always available). Since it doesn't affect core DB logic, we can safely enable it by default (it only has effect if user configures the webhook sink). For existing users using CDC webhook, after upgrade nothing changes except now they can add headers and they might notice less retries on client errors (which is typically beneficial). We will highlight in release notes the new capabilities and changed behavior: - "CDC Webhook sink now supports custom HTTP headers (e.g., for auth) and uses improved retry logic: it won't retry non-transient errors (4xx) and includes an Idempotency-Key header on each delivery for deduplication. Configure via ..."

We'll also update documentation to cover: - How to specify headers in config. - Explanation of the retry policy and how to adjust it if necessary. - How the dead letter queue works and how to retrieve events from it (this part might still be somewhat manual unless we provide a CLI command).

We might not need a phased rollout or beta period because this is additive and can be tested thoroughly. But we will test in a staging environment with a real scenario (e.g., set up a dummy service requiring auth and see that Geode can deliver events to it reliably). If any issues (like a header not being properly formatted or a status code we mis-handle), those can be fixed quickly since it's localized code.

If, for some reason, a user preferred the old behavior (like always retrying even on 404), they might have to adjust (though that scenario is unlikely; retrying 404 is not useful). We will mention the changes in behavior clearly so it's not a surprise.

Finally, we'll monitor if after release any feedback comes about this (maybe a user suggests dynamic header values or multi-tenant header injection - we can consider that for future).

All in all, this hardening of CDC webhook should make Geode's CDC much more viable in production environments where security and reliability are non-negotiable. The success of it will be reflected in users being able to plug Geode into their event-driven architectures without writing custom middleware to add tokens or handle duplicates - Geode will handle that out-of-the-box.

### 5\. Security Hardening (TDE & MFA)

**Why It Matters - User Impact (SQ1):** As Geode moves into enterprise use, robust security features are critical. **Encryption at Rest (Transparent Data Encryption, TDE)** ensures that if someone gains access to the database files or backups, they cannot read sensitive data (addresses data breaches concerns). Many industries (finance, healthcare) mandate encryption of data at rest - lacking TDE would be a show-stopper for adoption in those environments[\[92\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=protecting%20data%20at%20rest,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS)[\[23\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=transaction%20records%2C%20and%20personal%20identifiers,standards%2C%20including%20the%20Payment%20Card). Likewise, **Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)** for database access adds a layer of defense for user accounts, mitigating the risk of credential compromise leading to unauthorized access. Particularly for administrative or direct database logins, MFA can prevent attackers from logging in with just a stolen password[\[62\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=Database%20owners%20can%20now%20opt,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c).

Who benefits: - **Security/Compliance officers** can approve Geode usage if it meets encryption standards (e.g., using strong ciphers, managing keys properly) and access control best practices (like MFA for privileged accounts). - **DBAs/DevOps** benefit by being able to integrate Geode into environments that require these controls (cloud environments with strict compliance, etc.) without adding external solutions (like full-disk encryption which is coarser and doesn't cover backups explicitly). - **End-users and customers** have higher assurance their data in Geode is safe even if physical media or backups are stolen. - **Developers** may see no direct effect (TDE is transparent), but they may need to handle MFA for interactive sessions (like connecting with MFA tokens, which they likely already do for other systems).

Absence of these features currently: Geode data files are plaintext (an attacker obtaining a copy could read all data). Also, any user who gets DB credentials could log in without a second factor - a single point of failure in identity. The review noted the presence of stub modules for security ("authentication, roles, MFA exist (stubs)"[\[21\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L12-L15)), indicating these were planned but not done. Now is the time to complete them to not "undermine correctness" (they don't affect correctness of answers, but they are correctness of security).

**Design Proposal (SQ2):** - **Transparent Data Encryption (TDE):** Implement encryption of data files (and backups) using industry-standard algorithms (AES-256). We will generate a **master key** (or use a user-supplied key) which is used to encrypt a **data encryption key (DEK)** that actually encrypts the data. The DEK could be randomly generated and stored encrypted by the master key (envelope encryption model)[\[64\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=match%20at%20L277%20Furthermore%2C%20TDE,access%20to%20critical%20encrypted%20data)[\[63\]](https://www.mysql.com/products/enterprise/tde.html#:~:text=MySQL%20Enterprise%20Transparent%20Data%20Encryption,rotation%20and%20storing%20historical%20keys). The master key itself could be stored in a keystore or derived from a passphrase. To integrate with cloud KMS, perhaps allow specifying a command to retrieve the master key (not for v0.18, but perhaps by v0.19 we integrate AWS KMS support so that Geode can fetch the key at startup using a KMS CMK). Initially, a simpler approach: a config option encryption.master_key which can point to a file containing the master key encrypted with a passphrase. The DBA then provides the passphrase at server startup to unlock (similar to Oracle's wallet approach)[\[60\]](https://repost.aws/questions/QUjZrarwG_TOOoPZeGmIeVzQ/aws-kms-for-database-tde#:~:text=KMS%20cannot%20be%20used%20directly,be%20stored%20in%20AWS%20CloudHSM). This prevents storing the master key on disk in plaintext. Another approach is to accept the master key via environment variable or command-line (though that might be less secure if history is recorded). - We will encrypt the storage layers: page files, write-ahead log (WAL), Raft log snapshots - essentially anything on disk containing user data. We likely implement this at the page manager level: when writing a page to disk, encrypt it; when reading, decrypt. For WAL, each log record or buffer can be encrypted (or the entire WAL file as a stream). A minor overhead is adding an initialization vector (IV) for encryption - we can use page number or offset as part of IV for variance, or a random IV stored alongside the data. - We should also encrypt backups written to S3 if possible. If using KMS SSE, that covers it, but if user downloads the backup file, it is encrypted at rest in S3 but they could read it if they get it from S3. However, if the backup is just the encrypted pages, then even retrieving it yields encrypted content, which is secure. So actually, if the database itself is TDE, the snapshot we take will be in encrypted form (we might choose to keep it encrypted or not). E.g., if our backup exports the data logically it would need encryption. But if backup is a physical snapshot, it might already be encrypted bytes - which is fine and probably simplest (just copy the encrypted files). Then restoring requires the same master key to decrypt as the DB uses. - We will adopt AES in GCM or CBC mode (GCM gives authenticity check too). We must handle key rotation eventually, but in initial implementation, we may allow rotating the master key that encrypts DEK (re-encrypt DEK with new master, which is quick), but rotating DEK (re-encrypt all data) is heavy - might not implement automated rotation yet, rather advice to do a full re-encrypt backup/restore cycle if needed. - Overhead: use CPU AES acceleration (Zig might allow calling OS crypto libs or we can integrate a fast AES lib). On modern CPUs, AES-256 GCM can be very fast (a few GB/s per core). That overhead is usually acceptable (many DBs report <5% performance impact for TDE)[\[65\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=Due%20to%20the%20encryption%20and,tuning%20database%20settings%20and%20regularly). We will measure to ensure overhead is within, say, 5-10%. - Implementation complexity: moderate, but since we have clearly defined I/O paths, it's contained. We should ensure proper error handling - e.g., if wrong key provided, DB won't start (and gives clear error). Also need to ensure not to leave plaintext traces (like not writing unencrypted temp files or core dumps). - **Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA):** Provide an option to require MFA for certain user accounts (likely on a per-user basis). Implementation approach: - Use **TOTP (Time-based One-Time Password)**, the common standard for 2FA codes (used by Google Authenticator, etc.)[\[62\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=Database%20owners%20can%20now%20opt,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c). For each user that enables MFA, generate a secret (16-32 byte base32) and share it (the DBA can retrieve it to give to the user to load into their authenticator app). We can implement the TOTP generation (it's just HMAC-SHA1 with time steps). On login, after password verification, if the user has MFA enabled, the server expects a 6-digit code. We'll extend the authentication protocol to accommodate this: - If using Geode's native auth, after sending username/password, if MFA is required, respond with an error or special response indicating "MFA token required". The client (e.g., CLI or UI) then prompts the user for the token and resubmits (or maybe we design a way to include it in initial handshake if possible). It might require a new command or extension in the client protocol. - We might implement this first in our CLI (geodec) for administrative access. For programmatic access via drivers, those typically don't support interactive MFA easily; however, for app connections, many use service accounts (where MFA might not be practical). We may allow MFA to be optional per user - so only humans use it. - Optionally, support **Duo/WebAuthn**: Oracle's approach included integration with an identity domain and push notifications[\[62\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=Database%20owners%20can%20now%20opt,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c). That's out-of-scope for now. Our simpler TOTP approach covers most needs without external dependencies. - For MFA secrets storage, we'll store them hashed or encrypted in the system catalog (to avoid someone reading the secret and generating codes). Actually, TOTP secret is like a password equivalent; if someone steals it, they can generate codes. So it should be protected. We can encrypt it with the database master key (if we have TDE, we have a key; but if TDE off, we might still have some internal key or at least treat it as sensitive and not expose). Possibly we simply avoid storing it in plaintext - maybe not worry too much if DB files themselves are encrypted by TDE anyway. - Provide user management commands: e.g., ALTER USER alice ENABLE MFA which generates a secret, which the admin can retrieve (maybe printed once, or accessible via a function) to give to Alice. We should have a safe way to share it (one-time secret reveal ideally). - Provide a fallback: If user loses device, an admin or procedure can disable MFA or provide a recovery code. We might plan to allow storing a few one-time backup codes (like typical systems do). Simpler, an admin can remove MFA requirement for that user if needed. We should plan for that scenario operationally (so DBAs aren't locked out if they lose MFA device). - Logging: All login attempts with MFA should be auditable (success/failure), which ties to general access logging. - Likely scope to implement: local user auth with TOTP. If Geode supports LDAP/OIDC auth, maybe leave MFA to those systems (e.g., if using SSO with MFA, you don't need DB-level MFA). We can clarify that in docs. - Performance: verifying TOTP is negligible overhead (HMAC-SHA1 on 10 bytes). The user experience: it will require an extra step to log in, which is expected. - **Key Management & Integration:** For TDE, we'll also integrate with external KMS in the future. Possibly by v0.19, allow storing the master key in AWS KMS or Azure Key Vault. That way, even DB admins cannot see the key - separation of duties. For now, maybe just provide hooks or at least design such that it's addable (e.g., maybe have an interface for master key retrieval so someone could implement an integration). - Additionally, we'll incorporate a procedure for key rotation (maybe generating a new DEK, re-encrypting data gradually or by rewrite of files). Might not do in initial release due to complexity, but plan it. - **Encryption of Backups:** We discussed that if TDE is on, backups are essentially encrypted. If TDE is off but user still wants encrypted backups, they could rely on S3's SSE. We should note that. Maybe allow an option to encrypt backup with a user-supplied password (like a separate encryption just for backup file). If TDE is widespread, that covers it though. - **Optional MFA enforcement:** Perhaps allow configuration: e.g., an option "require MFA for all users with admin role" or similar. If there are multiple user roles, maybe only admins need MFA. Or organization might say all direct DB logins require MFA. We could let them enforce that via policy or by enabling MFA on each user individually. - **Roles and Permissions:** The review mentions roles. Implementing MFA might coincide with finishing role-based access control (RBAC). TDE and MFA don't directly depend on RBAC, but RBAC is part of broader security. If not fully done, we consider at least making sure enabling/disabling MFA or handling keys is limited to administrators. We might have a built-in admin user who can do these operations. - **Integration with other security features:** e.g., if an environment already uses Vault or Key Management, possibly allow Geode to use an external source for the encryption key and even for user auth (some DBs allow PAM or LDAP plus TOTP). - For v0.19, maybe beyond scope; we focus on internal solution.

**Standards & Compliance (SQ3):** - **Encryption algorithms:** We'll use NIST-approved ciphers (AES256 in a mode like GCM or CBC with HMAC for integrity). This meets PCI DSS and other regulatory requirements for strong encryption[\[92\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=protecting%20data%20at%20rest,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS)[\[23\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=transaction%20records%2C%20and%20personal%20identifiers,standards%2C%20including%20the%20Payment%20Card). We will document cipher and key length for compliance checklists. - **Key Management:** We will generate keys with high entropy and store them securely. If using a passphrase, we encourage a strong passphrase. If integrated with KMS, that aligns with best practice of central key management. A likely compliance need: the ability to rotate keys and to have separation of duties (DBA can't read keys). By using a passphrase not stored on disk, we ensure that an attacker who steals the DB files cannot decrypt without that separate secret (this is how Oracle's wallet works - it requires opening with a password). - **MFA standards:** TOTP is based on RFC 6238, widely accepted. We will also align with guidelines: e.g., codes typically expire after 30 seconds, allow a small window of previous code (±1 step) to account for slight time drift, etc. The default 6-digit codes are standard, meaning it works with all authenticator apps. We will ensure the secret key is provided in a standard format (base32) and possibly provide a URI or QR code that can be scanned (maybe output an otpauth:// URI that the admin can turn into a QR code for user convenience). - **User Experience:** For MFA, we should ensure that brute forcing codes is mitigated (most systems lock out after some attempts). We might implement a simple rule: if 5 consecutive MFA code failures for a user, maybe lock the account for a minute or require re-login. We should log such events. This prevents online guessing of the code (though 6-digit, 1e6 possibilities, but if unlimited tries it's a risk albeit small with a 30s window). - **RBAC & Least Privilege:** If we have roles, ensure that key management operations (like rotating keys, viewing the backup secret) are restricted to a high privilege role. - **Auditing:** Ideally, we log security-relevant events: login success/failure, MFA failures, key changes, etc. That helps meet compliance auditing requirements. For now, at least ensure login attempts and key changes are logged. - **FIPS compliance:** If some want FIPS 140-2 compliance, we might need to use a FIPS-certified crypto library. Not sure if required now, but using common algorithms is a start. If needed, we can allow plugging in an external crypto provider. - **Backwards compatibility:** Enabling TDE on an existing database might require conversion (which we plan via backup/restore or similar). If not, we might only support enabling TDE at database creation for now (thus existing data remains unencrypted unless reloaded). We should clarify that. For MFA, existing users can continue without MFA unless explicitly enabled, so no breakage. - **No performance impact on compliance:** The design ensures performance impact is minimal (the overhead is known and we've targeted it to be small). This aligns with how TDE in other DBs is accepted (they consider the slight performance trade-off worth the security). - **Key retention and recovery:** Need to ensure if user loses the master key or passphrase, data is irretrievable (that's by design - a necessary risk to accept because otherwise encryption is pointless). We will caution users to safely store keys/passphrases (like in a password manager or KMS). This meets the security principle that there's no backdoor.

**Risks & Complexity (SQ4):** - **Potential Performance Penalty:** If encryption is not implemented efficiently (e.g., not using hardware acceleration or encrypting too often unnecessarily), it could slow down I/O significantly. We mitigate by encrypting in blocks (like page sized) and using AES-NI. We'll test on bulk operations (like large data import) to see overhead. Also, multi-threading on queries can hide some encryption cost if CPU is free on other cores, but worst-case a single thread doing heavy I/O will be slightly slower. We'll ensure it's within acceptable range (target <10% overhead as said). - **Implementation Bugs:** A bug in encryption could lead to data corruption or in worst case, data loss if we mess up decrypting. We need to be extremely careful with encryption/decryption routines and key handling (e.g., if we use wrong IV or corrupt the cipher text inadvertently, we could make data unreadable). We'll rely on well-known algorithms and maybe existing libraries to reduce bug risk. Also implement tests: e.g., write some pages encrypted, then try to read them back to verify correctness. Possibly integrate known vectors for AES to test correctness. - **Key Mismanagement:** If keys are not stored correctly, e.g., writing them to log or leaving them in memory where they can be swapped to disk, that's a risk. We'll avoid logging keys, and if possible, lock memory for key storage (some systems allocate keys in secure memory to avoid swapping - maybe an enhancement we can do using OS calls). - **Operational Complexity:** TDE requiring a passphrase at startup means automated deployments need a mechanism to provide that (like via a secret management system). If not planned, it could cause pain (e.g., auto-scaling cluster nodes that can't start without manual key entry). One solution is to use KMS integration to auto-unlock (which transfers trust to KMS). We should at least plan for that soon, and in interim, document how to use environment variables or a secure config injection for headless startup. Risk is if not addressed, some might find TDE hard to use in automated env. - **Data Migration for TDE:** If enabling encryption on an existing dataset, we need to rewrite it encrypted. This could be done by backup (plaintext) and restore (encrypted), which requires double storage and downtime. Or implement in-place but that's tricky (would involve re-encrypting each page online - possible but complex and risk of crash mid-process leaving partial encrypted DB). Given complexity, we might say TDE can only be enabled at creation time in v0.18. That's a limitation but one we might accept initially. - **MFA user friction:** If not implemented well, MFA could lock out users or be seen as a nuisance. We mitigate by making it optional and per-user. Also ensuring backup codes or admin override exist to avoid lockouts. There's also a risk if time drift between client and server - we should use NTP on server, and allow ±30s in code check. - **Backup/Restore interplay:** If a database is TDE, how do we handle backup? Possibly backup stays encrypted requiring same key at restore. That means one must supply the key when restoring (which is fine, as if building a new cluster, they need to supply the same master key). We should ensure our backup tooling can accept the key (or if the cluster is started with the key, it will already handle it). - **RBAC not fully implemented:** Possibly roles are incomplete, but MFA doesn't strictly need roles. We can attach MFA to any user definition. However, if user management itself is minimal (maybe just a single admin user currently), implementing MFA for just that admin is still valuable. Role support is a separate improvement (e.g., row-level security was mentioned, roles might exist). We can probably piggyback on the existing user model to store MFA secrets. If that model is not fully fleshed out, part of our work might be to refine the user management system slightly. - **Testing difficulty:** Testing security features thoroughly requires not just unit tests but maybe pen-testing mindset. For TDE, ensure no plaintext escapes (we might want to search the data files for known plaintext after enabling TDE; our test could insert known patterns and verify they are not visible in file). For MFA, simulate multiple login attempts, wrong code, etc., and ensure it locks as expected. Also test concurrency (maybe two codes in parallel - not relevant usually; each login attempt handled individually).

**Benchmarks & Acceptance Criteria (SQ5):** We will primarily test for correctness and acceptable overhead: - **TDE Functional test:** Enable encryption with a known test key on a test database. Insert a variety of data (including edge cases like very small values, very large values, different data types). Ensure that all read/write operations still function correctly (no corruption or errors). Then _inspect the on-disk files_ (the data file, WAL, any snapshot) to verify content is encrypted (the known data patterns do not appear in plaintext). We might use tools or just attempt to find a plaintext substring in the file - it shouldn't be there. This confirms encryption is working. Another test: backup the database, then attempt to open the backup without the key (it should fail to decrypt - basically showing it's secure). - **TDE Performance test:** measure throughput of a write-heavy workload with encryption on vs off. We expect some drop, e.g., without TDE maybe 10k ops/sec, with TDE maybe 9k ops/sec (just an example). If the drop is much larger, investigate optimizations (maybe we'd need to adjust block sizes or ensure we're not double-encrypting or something silly). Similarly measure read query latency slight overhead. We will consider it acceptable if throughput drop <15% and latency impact <10% in our tests. If it's borderline, we might document performance overhead but it's likely fine given modern hardware. - **Key handling test:** Change the master key (simulate rotation). If we design that, test that data remains readable after proper re-encryption. If we only implement re-encrypting the DEK with new master (which is easy), test that scenario: have new master, decrypt old DEK with old master, encrypt with new master, ensure can still read data. That is trivial but should test e.g., by doing that in memory or via a command. - **MFA Functional tests:** - Create a user with MFA, ensure that logging in _without_ token now fails (with a clear message). Logging in _with_ correct token succeeds. Logging in with wrong token fails (and ideally after multiple tries perhaps lock or at least require re-auth). - Test time drift: generate a token for the previous 30s interval and ensure server accepts it (within allowed window). Use an out-of-window token to ensure it fails. - Usability: Possibly create a test that retrieves the MFA secret QR code or URI and feed it into a TOTP library to generate the token - verifying our secret is correctly interpreted by standard algorithms. - Recovery: If we implement backup codes or admin disable, test that scenario. - Multi-user: if multiple users have MFA, test that each has separate secrets and one user's token doesn't work for another (it shouldn't, but just covering base). - **Security scanning:** We might do static analysis to ensure keys aren't logged or memory handling around keys is secure (maybe beyond what tests can do, but manual code review). - **Compatibility tests:** Ensure enabling TDE doesn't break other features (like the CDC still operates, presumably unaffected). Or that backups taken from encrypted DB can be restored to a new encrypted DB (we need to supply key). We'll simulate that: backup the encrypted DB, then try to restore on a new instance with the same key. It should work and the new instance should produce same data on queries. - **No regression in correctness/performance for non-users:** If TDE and MFA are not used (default off), ensure the DB behaves exactly as before (no overhead, no new failures). That means our changes to incorporate these features don't impact normal code paths when disabled. E.g., encryption off might mean just write plaintext as before - test that still works. MFA off means login as usual - test that not every login expects a code, only those configured.

**Acceptance criteria:** - **Data encrypted at rest:** Verified by inspection that no sensitive data is stored in plaintext on disk or in backup when TDE is enabled. Also, trying to start or access data without providing the key fails securely. - **Negligible performance impact:** Under typical workloads, TDE overhead is within expected range (target ~5-10%). No noticeable overhead if features are disabled. - **MFA effectively secures login:** Without the second factor, login is impossible (tested), thus mitigating stolen password scenario. Also, after a few failed token attempts, appropriate action is taken (maybe requiring re-auth - even if we don't implement auto-lock, at least each attempt requires correct password+token, so an attacker can't brute force easily because they'd also need the password each time ideally; depending on how we implement the flow). - **Usability considerations addressed:** For example, we have a documented procedure for rotating keys, and a procedure for when an admin loses the MFA device. Those don't need to be fully automated, but at least documented (like "if lost, contact another admin to disable MFA on your user"). We should ensure there's at least an emergency override (like a special startup flag to bypass auth for emergency - though that itself is a risk, maybe requiring physical access). - **Integration with ecosystem:** It's acceptable if some advanced integration (like KMS, SSO-based MFA) is not done yet, as long as the basics are there and planned. But we might list that as future work (e.g., "In future, integrate with AWS KMS for master key storage, and with external IdPs for MFA via SSO/OAuth"). - **No breaking changes:** Existing databases can be used without turning on TDE/MFA. Those who want it can opt in via config. New databases can be initialized with encryption from the get-go. - **Docs and messaging:** The feature is considered accepted when we have thorough documentation on how to use it (ex: how to enable TDE, store keys, the fact that if key is lost data is irrecoverable, etc.; how to enable MFA for a user, how the login flow works, etc.). Also, internal testing should confirm all our acceptance tests above and no major issues remain.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** - **Crypto library:** likely needed for AES. We could use Zig's std if it has AES (not sure if Zig std has crypto ready; if not, perhaps a small library or code from known source). HMAC-SHA1 for TOTP and a base32 encoding/decoding for secret keys (to present to user). These are straightforward or could use existing code. - **Persistence of keys/secrets:** We might add fields in existing system catalogs or config files. For example, storing an encrypted form of DEK or TOTP secrets in a system table or a separate file. We likely already have some system storage (maybe docs/REQUIREMENTS mention internal catalogs). We must ensure these are encrypted with something if not already protected by TDE. Possibly we keep them in memory only after reading once from operator input (meaning, if the DB can't store them persistently, the operator must supply them each time, which is an option but not user-friendly for TOTP secret - so we probably do store MFA secret hashed or encrypted by DB master key). - **Time synchronization for TOTP:** ideally the DB server's clock is correct. We may suggest enabling NTP on server to avoid drift. Not a code dependency but an operational one. - **UI/Client changes:** If we have a CLI or GUI, they need to handle MFA challenge. For CLI, we can prompt for MFA code if server indicates it's needed. We should implement that in our Geode CLI tool. For other clients (like if someone uses a generic SQL client), they might not know how to handle a custom challenge. We could decide to simply treat MFA code as part of password (like require user to append the code to password - though that's less secure, but some systems did that historically). But better to have a protocol: e.g., a specific exception or result code that means "MFA required". If the user is using code we control (like our CLI), great. If using third-party, they probably can't do MFA unless they have custom support. That might be okay (for those, they might use service accounts without MFA). - **Memory management for keys:** Maybe integrate with OS to lock memory pages for keys (to avoid swapping). Zig may allow using mlock. Not mandatory but good practice. - **Testing environment:** Possibly need a safe way to test these without exposing keys in logs. We'll run tests mostly offline so it's fine; just be careful to scrub logs or secrets after use in tests. - **Future integration:** KMS - maybe beyond current timeline but good to design keys such that integration is possible (like a KMS plugin to fetch the master key). Not a direct dependency now, but keep in mind when designing key storage abstraction.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - **v0.18.0:** Introduce TDE and MFA as **optional features** turned off by default (so as not to surprise anyone upgrading). Mark them as **"Tech Preview" or beta** perhaps, depending on confidence. We would encourage early adopters to try them in non-prod and give feedback. - Provide documentation and possibly a small reference implementation or demo (like a how-to guide: enabling TDE on a new database, enabling MFA for admin). - Possibly require setting flags to enable these (like --enable-tde at init, or a config file entry) to ensure people don't accidentally turn it on without understanding. But likely it will be explicit anyway (since they must provide a key or password). - We'll perform a security audit internally on the code (maybe have a security-minded dev review it). - If any concerns (like performance), note them in docs. - If there's not enough time to fully test rotation or some edge aspect, label accordingly (e.g., "MFA currently supports TOTP; push-based MFA integration not yet; TDE does not support online key rotation yet - for new key, re-init DB or restore from backup with new key"). - Engage with one of our enterprise users (if any in early access) to test these features in their environment and report any issues. - **v0.19.0:** Remove "beta" tag, possibly make TDE easier to use (maybe integrate a KMS option by this time), and MFA more integrated (maybe with client tools). - We might consider in v0.19 or later making encryption on by default for new DBs (some systems do that now, e.g., in cloud all databases are encrypted at rest by default). But for on-prem, it might remain opt-in. We likely will not force encryption by default yet, but maybe in cloud scenarios we will. - Provide more automation by v0.19: e.g., an easier key management CLI, backup code generation for MFA, etc., based on feedback. - Throughout, ensure that enabling these features does not produce any unpredicted behavior in other areas (which our tests cover).

We'll monitor after release for any issues, especially performance and any bug reports about inability to decrypt etc. Because security features need high reliability (a bug could lock out data or users). If any showstoppers appear, we might release a minor patch quickly, or advise turning the feature off until fixed. But given thorough testing, we plan to avoid that.

Implementing TDE and MFA significantly elevates Geode's security posture to align with enterprise expectations, making it suitable for sensitive data and usage in strict IT environments. These changes, while largely transparent to normal operation, provide crucial protections and likely will be required by many customers evaluating Geode in 2025 and beyond.

### 6\. Full-Text Search (FTS) Improvements (Phrase Queries, Highlighting, Ranking Controls)

**Why It Matters - User Impact (SQ1):** Geode's full-text search capability allows indexing and querying text content stored in the graph. However, as of v0.17 it supports only basic keyword search with BM25 ranking and no advanced features[\[10\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L34-L39). For users building search-driven applications (like knowledge graph exploration, product catalogs, social posts search, etc.), **phrase search** and **result highlighting** are crucial for relevance and usability. - **Phrase queries:** allow searching for exact sequences of words (e.g., "machine learning") which dramatically improves precision by filtering out documents where those words appear separately. Without phrase support, a search for a multi-word concept returns many false positives (any document containing the words anywhere) and the user may have to sift through irrelevant results. - **Highlighting:** when showing search results, highlighting the matched terms in context (a snippet of text with the terms emphasized) helps the user quickly see why a result was matched and how relevant it is[\[11\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=,tags%20or%20otherwise%20emphasizing%20them). Without this, a user sees a raw text and might not easily find the matched term, reducing search UX quality. - **Ranking controls:** BM25 is a good default ranking algorithm, but being able to adjust ranking (boost certain fields or tune BM25 parameters) or offer alternative scoring (like TF-IDF or cosine similarity for certain use cases) gives developers flexibility to fine-tune search results to their domain. Also, controlling ranking is needed if some fields are more important than others (e.g., hits in a title should count more than hits in body text). - Additionally, extended FTS features like wildcard/prefix search, fuzzy matching (typo tolerance), and proximity queries (words within N words) are common expectations in search. While not explicitly in the gap list, phrase search covers a form of proximity (distance=0). Possibly proximity with small N could be considered as well.

Overall, improving FTS will benefit end-users performing searches (by increasing result accuracy and readability), and developers by enabling more advanced query capabilities so they don't need to bolt on an external search engine (like Lucene/Elasticsearch) just to get phrase or highlight functionality.

**Evidence of Need:** The code review explicitly noted that **Full-Text Search is missing highlighting, phrase queries, and proximity**[\[10\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L34-L39), indicating these were known gaps. SurrealDB, a newer multi-model DB, advertises integrated FTS with phrase search and highlighting[\[11\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=,tags%20or%20otherwise%20emphasizing%20them), which suggests the industry expects these features even in a database context (not just dedicated search engines). Neo4j's full-text search (introduced in Neo4j 3.5+) supports Lucene query syntax including phrases and prefix queries, and it's a frequently used feature for text-heavy graph scenarios. If Geode's FTS remains limited, users might have to export data to an external search service to fulfill requirements - complicating the architecture. Instead, by offering phrase search and highlighting, Geode can cover a majority of text query needs itself.

**Design Proposal (SQ2):** - **Phrase Query Support:** To support phrases, we need to index term positions. We will extend the FTS index to store the position (token offset) of each term within the document[\[93\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=%28inverted%20indexes%2C%20suffix%20arrays%2C%20etc,details%20that%20enable%20relevance%20scoring). Likely, the current index maps each term -> list of docs (with term frequency). We'll change it to map term -> list of (doc, positions...). This increases index size somewhat but is necessary. Then, for a phrase query (sequence of terms), the query engine will retrieve the posting lists for each term and then perform a positional merge: find documents where the terms appear with consecutive positions. This is similar to how Lucene processes phrase queries (it intersects postings with an offset). If phrase search is requested (e.g., user input is quoted "machine learning"), we will perform this positional intersection. Complexity is linear in sum of postings lengths - fine for moderately common terms given we presumably have relatively small text or not web-scale volumes. We should ensure our implementation is efficient (maybe break out early if one term isn't present). - We'll add a query syntax or function to indicate phrase search. Possibly simplest for users is: if the search string contains quotes, treat that portion as a phrase. E.g., TEXT_SEARCH('content', '"machine learning" data') meaning find documents containing phrase "machine learning" and the word "data". We can parse the search string to identify phrases (quoted segments). Alternatively, provide a separate function or parameter like TEXT_SEARCH_PHRASE('content', 'machine learning'). But supporting quotes in the search string is user-friendly and akin to search engine syntax. We just have to parse it carefully (account for escape quotes etc.). - We'll also handle multi-term queries combining phrases and single terms. A simple approach: interpret the input as a mini query language: terms separated by spaces = AND logic by default (like all terms must appear).
