That seems to match typical default (or we might consider OR by default - but for database search, AND is safer default to avoid too broad results). If needed, allow OR with OR keyword. But maybe not go too deep - supporting phrase and implicit AND may suffice. - **Highlighting:** Once a query is executed, we know which parts matched. To highlight, we will retrieve the original text of the matched field and then mark up the terms. Specifically: - When returning results, we can include an extra field like snippet that contains a snippet of text around the first (or all) occurrences of the search term, with them wrapped in e.g. &lt;em&gt; tags (or some marker that the UI can bold). SurrealDB's approach is functions like search::highlight("&lt;b&gt;", "&lt;/b&gt;", field) to produce text with &lt;b&gt;...&lt;/b&gt; around terms[\[67\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=Querying%20Full). We could emulate that: perhaps provide a built-in function HIGHLIGHT(field, query_string, pre_tag, post_tag) that returns highlighted HTML (or text) for the matches of that query in that field. But to highlight, we need to know what query was (the terms). We do have the terms and possibly positions from the search result. - Simpler approach: after finding a doc match, we can scan its text for the query terms (case-insensitively) and insert tags. For phrase queries, highlight the whole phrase occurrence as one (maybe by highlighting each term or the entire phrase span). - We should return a snippet of limited length, not full text if text is large (like highlight ~30 characters before and after the first occurrence, etc., and use "..." for omitted parts). Or multiple snippets if multiple terms far apart. Many search engines do multiple snippets. We could do at least the first snippet. We might not implement multi-snippet extraction initially for simplicity; one snippet might be okay, or allow user to specify snippet length. - We will ensure we escape HTML in text except for our tags (so we don't introduce XSS if the text itself contains e.g., user input that looks like HTML). - If text is very large, computing snippet for every result might be heavy - but presumably, FTS queries often limit results (like top 10). It's okay. If needed, we can optimize by using the positions from the index to directly locate terms without scanning entire text (since we have term positions in doc). - Outputting highlight: Could either be an extra pseudo-field in the query results or a separate API call. The simplest for usage is a function in the RETURN clause: e.g., RETURN title, HIGHLIGHT(content). Surreal does search::highlight using likely the last executed search context. We might simplify: if TEXT_SEARCH is used in WHERE, then HIGHLIGHT(field) could implicitly highlight terms from that last search. Implementation-wise, store the query terms from the TEXT_SEARCH evaluation and use them. Alternatively, require user to pass the same query string to highlight: HIGHLIGHT(content, '"machine learning"'). That ensures consistency. - We'll choose a design balancing ease and clarity - maybe highlight uses the same query string as search, to avoid confusion. - **Ranking Controls:** - For boosting fields: if the user wants to search multiple fields with different weights (title vs body), one approach is allow specifying a field boost in the query string. E.g., a query "foo bar" that by default searches content, but if we have separate indexes, maybe they have to query them separately and combine. Another way: We implement a TEXT_SEARCH function that returns a relevance score (rather than just boolean), then the user can combine or weight them. For example: WHERE TEXT_SEARCH(content, 'foo') \* 1.0 + TEXT_SEARCH(title, 'foo') \* 2.0 > 0. That suggests altering TEXT_SEARCH to output a numeric score (the BM25 score), which can be used in expressions and sorting. Actually, in v0.17, how do we use BM25? Possibly TEXT_SEARCH returns something and maybe they had to sort by it manually. It's not clear if in v0.17 they exposed score (maybe not). If not, we should expose it. Possibly via a function TEXT_SCORE() that yields the last query's score for that document. Surreal uses search::score(n) to get score of nth index used[\[67\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=Querying%20Full). - Simpler: Make TEXT_SEARCH(field, query) return a numeric score, or 0 if not matched (we could still treat non-matches as 0). Then a user can do WHERE TEXT_SEARCH(content,'foo') > 0 to filter, and also ORDER BY TEXT_SEARCH(content,'foo') DESC to sort by relevance. This would recompute the score for each doc though (we'd have to search the index for each doc, inefficient). Alternatively, maybe integrate it so that when used in WHERE we also get scores. It's a bit tricky to integrate elegantly. - Perhaps easier: keep TEXT_SEARCH as boolean (for filtering), and introduce a TEXT_SCORE() that can only be called after a TEXT_SEARCH filter was used (like contextual). This is complex to implement in engine. Might require storing scores in a hidden column during query execution. Surreal's approach with specific search functions hints they store some context. - As a simpler initial compromise, we may skip exposing fine-grained boosting control in v0.18 beyond possibly letting user adjust a global BM25 parameter if needed. But at least we should allow retrieval of the score for ordering or display. So implementing TEXT_SCORE() function that returns the score of the document from the last executed text search in the query might be acceptable. Or, more hacky but easy: after running the search, sort the results by score internally then return sorted (the user asked for search results, likely they want them ranked anyway). If we implicitly sort by score when using TEXT_SEARCH in WHERE without explicit ORDER BY, that might be nice but could surprise (databases usually don't implicitly sort). Instead, we'll encourage using ORDER BY TEXT_SCORE() explicitly, which we will support. - We'll implement something like: if a query uses TEXT_SEARCH in WHERE, the execution collects matching docs with their scores. We attach the score in memory. If user calls TEXT_SCORE() in RETURN or ORDER BY, we output or sort by that attached score. This requires a bit of plumbing but doable. - Boosting fields: if needed, user can do separate TEXT_SEARCH on each field and weight them (once we have numeric output). Or we might later implement multi-field index support. - Additionally, consider configurable BM25 parameters (k1, b). We might add config for the FTS index creation (like if user defines an index, they could specify custom parameters). This might be a later refinement (not strictly needed for initial improvements). - **Other features (if capacity):** - _Prefix/Wildcard search:_ Support trailing wildcard like "micro_" to match "micrograph", "microbiome". This requires storing a prefix index or scanning postings of terms with that prefix. Possibly out-of-scope for now (complex and not asked explicitly). -_ Fuzzy search: _find similar words (typos). This would require storing phonetic codes or using Levenshtein on query. Likely out-of-scope now. -_ Stop-word handling: _Already likely done (common words might be either indexed or could be skipped if there's a stopword list). If not, we could integrate a small default stopword list to not index extremely common words (the trade-off: phrase search requiring "to be" might fail if we drop "to" and "be"). We might leave stopwords included by default for correctness, or allow customizing analyzers later. -_ Language analyzers:\* Possibly not now beyond basic case-folding and punctuation removal (which we have). But keep in mind if text in languages like Chinese, segmentation is needed. That might be advanced for this timeline, but maybe support quoting phrase in Chinese which requires indexing character bigrams etc. Probably not now.

**Standards & Expectations (SQ3):** - **ISO GQL** doesn't define full-text search (it's typically extension). So we have freedom in design. We should align with common de-facto standards from search engines and maybe Cypher's FTS. For example, Neo4j uses Lucene syntax if you use their index query string. We don't need full Lucene syntax (like wildcards, NOT, etc.) at once, but phrase queries with quotes is a common convention in user interfaces (Google, etc.). We adopt that (with possible limitations). - **Output format:** Highlighting often is HTML with &lt;em&gt; or such, as that's easy for clients to render. We should ensure to choose tags or markers that are unlikely to conflict with the actual text content. &lt;em&gt; is standard and highlight styling can be applied easily. Surreal uses user-specified markers in their function call[\[94\]](https://surrealdb.com/docs/surrealdb/models/full-text-search#:~:text=,highlight%20matched%20terms%20in%20queries). We might not allow dynamic markers at first (just default to &lt;em&gt; and &lt;/em&gt; or &lt;mark&gt; tag). Possibly provide a config if needed. - **Ranking transparency:** Many systems allow retrieving a score. We should too. And ideally, the scoring algorithm (BM25) is documented so users know what it means, and that it's consistent with typical BM25 usage. If we let them configure k1, b, we mention default values (e.g., k1=1.2, b=0.75 typical). - **Case and diacritics:** Typically, search is case-insensitive and accent-insensitive by default. We likely already lowercase tokens. We should confirm if we handle accents (maybe not now, but could optionally remove diacritics so "résumé" matches "resume" - that might require an additional filter or library for Unicode normalization). Might not do in v0.18 unless trivial. - **Precision vs recall trade-offs:** Our enhancements should not break the ability to do broad searches. We are adding features, not removing any. If anything, phrase search improves precision, highlighting improves user interpretation, ranking control improves result ordering. - **Performance considerations:** Users expect sub-second response for moderate text search queries. If our index is efficient, phrase queries should still be fast (especially if not extremely common terms). We need to check that we do efficient merging and not, say, scan entire documents. We will test performance to ensure search queries scale appropriately (maybe search time vs number of docs). - **Index build consistency:** If we modify index format, need to ensure we either auto rebuild indexes on upgrade or bump an index version to trigger rebuild. We can programmatically detect if an FTS index lacks positions and then re-index it if needed. That might be needed for upgrade from v0.17 to v0.18 so that phrase search works (we will do that or instruct user to rebuild FTS indexes). - **Integration with Graph Patterns:** Perhaps allow FTS in match queries (like find nodes where content contains words). We already do that via a WHERE clause with TEXT_SEARCH presumably. That will continue to work, now with more capability. - **Testing queries determinism:** Search results ordering by relevance might not be deterministic if scores equal; but that's accepted in search (we might have tie-break by doc id if needed to have stable order for ties). - **Security:** If any HTML is returned in highlight, ensure it's properly encoded to avoid cross-site scripting if someone displays it raw in a web UI. We likely will document that our highlight uses &lt;em&gt; and that calling code should mark it as safe HTML or escape accordingly (except the tags).

**Risks & Complexity (SQ4):** - **Index size & update cost:** Storing positions will enlarge the FTS index. If a document has a lot of text, its posting entries get bigger. This could slow indexing slightly and use more memory/disk. The trade-off is necessary. We should measure how much bigger: likely proportional to total term count (each term occurrence now stores an int position, maybe compressible somewhat). Possibly fine given typical usage (if storing large articles, it could be significant but still manageable as search systems do it). - **Query performance:** Phrase search might degrade if the query terms are very common. E.g., search for phrase "of the" (common words) - posting lists are huge, and intersection may check a lot. The result likely no docs have "of the" consecutively except basically everywhere, so it might actually match many docs or process heavy. We can test and perhaps choose to skip phrase if terms are stopwords. Or just document that extremely common phrases can degrade performance. Many engines handle that by e.g., not indexing very common words. We could integrate a basic stopword removal (maybe optional). - **Highlight generation overhead:** For large texts, scanning to produce snippet might be heavy. We can optimize by only scanning until we find the first occurrence or the top few occurrences. If text is huge (MBs), that could be slow in CPU. But usually textual data in graphs (like descriptions, etc.) are not extremely huge per entry. If some are, we could limit snippet length artificially (like if nothing found in first 10k chars, maybe skip highlight or show start-of-text). - **Complex parsing:** Implementing a mini query language for search (handling quotes, maybe boolean ops) is a bit of work. If we keep it minimal (support quotes for phrases, and maybe treat unquoted terms as AND by default), it's manageable. We need to carefully parse to not mis-handle quotes or escape sequences. We can create a small parser or use regex to split by quotes vs spaces. Should be fine if done systematically. - **Backward compatibility:** If someone used TEXT_SEARCH in v0.17, they might assume it returned boolean and no ability to sort by it. If we change it to return numeric, it could break queries that did WHERE TEXT_SEARCH(...) because numeric > 0 is truthy but if we treat any nonzero as true maybe fine. If numeric 0/ some positive, used in boolean context perhaps it's fine (0 is false, nonzero true in many languages). Zig's query eval might not treat numeric as boolean though. We might just keep TEXT_SEARCH as pseudo-boolean to avoid that complication. Possibly we introduce a new function for score or allow cast. We must be cautious not to break queries - probably okay because most likely usage was just filtering in WHERE or ordering by it if they tried. If it was boolean and they tried to ORDER BY, might not have been allowed. We'll design such that any query that used it continues to work (maybe by still returning a numeric but ensuring filter semantics still work as intended). We can test one of their example tests for FTS if present. - **Multi-language not addressed:** If dataset contains multilingual text, our default analyzer might not tokenize correctly for some (like Chinese, we would index each character or nothing separated). That's a known limitation; we can mention that supporting additional analyzers (like language-specific tokenization or stemming) is future work. It's not a regression, just not implemented yet. - **Stemming and synonyms not addressed:** Means searching "run" won't find "running" currently unless both words present. Many search solutions provide stemming. Without it, some recall might suffer. Given timeline, we may skip stemming now or implement a very basic one for English (maybe use Porter stemmer code). If skip, we note it as future improvement, and caution user to index all forms of words or use wildcard if needed (if we implement prefix that partially covers some morphological variance). - **Testing complexity:** We must test across variety of cases (with punctuation in query, with case differences, overlapping highlights if query has repeated term, etc.). It's manageable but lots of test strings needed. Also ensure that phrase search results are correct - we might cross-check on a small dataset manually or against an external reference (like feed same dataset to a Lucene query to verify we get same phrase hits). That could validate our logic. - **Memory use:** Storing positions means memory overhead, but presumably our index might be on disk or memory mapped. If entirely in memory, big text corpus could use a lot of memory. Might be acceptable for moderate data sets. We should mention that if adding FTS with positions, index memory usage will increase, plan accordingly.

**Benchmarks & Acceptance Criteria (SQ5):** - **Phrase correctness test:** Create a set of documents (strings) where some contain a specific phrase and others contain the same words but apart. For example, text1: "the quick brown fox", text2: "the fox is quick and brown". Search for "quick brown" phrase. Expect it matches text1 and not text2 (because in text2 "quick" and "brown" are separated by other words). Also test phrase at boundaries (start or end of text). Possibly test multi-word phrase with punctuation in between (like "New York" in "New, York" - depending on how tokenizer handles comma, probably it's like "New" and "York" separated by punctuation, but we likely treat punctuation as whitespace so phrase might not match if punctuation consumed an empty token or so - we have to handle punctuation by either ignoring it or treating as separator; typically it's removed, so "New, York" becomes \["New","York"\] with positions maybe skipping one? Actually if removed, positions might treat them adjacent anyway). We'll ensure our tokenization yields correct adjacency for phrase queries even with punctuation (maybe by treating punctuation as space). - **Highlight correctness test:** Using a sample text and query, generate highlight and verify it contains the query terms wrapped in tags, and includes some context (and no extremely long context or missing it). E.g., text: "Alice went to the market to buy apples and oranges.", query: "buy apples". Expect highlight like "... to **buy apples** and ..." (with tags around buy and apples). Also test that case differences still highlight (like query "Alice", text "alice" - if we fold case, it should highlight "alice" with capital 'A' in highlight or do we preserve original text? We should preserve original text and just wrap it - so 'alice' becomes '**alice**'). - If multiple occurrences: ensure at least the first occurrence is highlighted. Possibly highlight all occurrences if easily done, but snippet likely focusing on first. We can test scenario with multiple matches and see what we do, then decide if that meets acceptable output (e.g., highlight only first occurrence plus maybe indicate if more occurrences exist by an ellipsis or count). - **Score availability test:** Run a search query and ensure that ordering by score works. For instance, have two docs: one with query term once, another with query term twice. Query for that term, sort by score descending, verify the one with two occurrences comes first (since BM25 would give higher score). Or if the lengths differ, ensure the ranking seems plausible (shorter doc with term once vs longer doc with term once - shorter likely ranks higher because term is more concentrated). - **Field boosting test (if implemented):** If we allow combining scores, test that boosting yields expected outcome. E.g., docA has term in title, docB has it only in body many times, with a certain boost maybe docA can outrank docB if configured. If our interface is manual (like user adds weighted sums), test that arithmetic works. - **Performance test:** - Indexing: measure time to index a sample of, say, 10k documents with and without positions. It will be slower with positions but should still be fast (maybe within 1.2x or so). If too slow, maybe optimize by using efficient data structures or multi-threading indexing if possible. But likely okay. - Query: phrase query vs equivalent broad query. E.g., search "machine learning" as phrase vs searching "machine AND learning" separately. The phrase query should be equal or faster than the broad query in terms of candidate results because it filters more. It might be slower in processing though because it has to do positional checks on postings. We'll measure time on e.g., 100k docs average length, phrase vs two-term query. It should be in the milliseconds ideally. If we find any query taking unacceptable time (like >100ms for moderate data, or scaling badly with doc count), we might need to optimize the index or search algorithm (like use skip lists in postings). - Concurrency: ensure FTS queries can run in parallel to other queries without interfering (should, as index likely read-only in query, and updates handle locks appropriately). Possibly fire a search while updates happening to see if any issues (should be fine with proper locks). - **Resource usage test:** If possible, measure memory overhead of index with positions vs without. If it's, say, 20% more, that's fine. If 100% more, still maybe fine if absolute size is okay, but we'll note it. It's an implicit cost for phrase support but expected. Also test that enabling highlights (computing them) doesn't cause memory bloat or leak (process many search requests and see memory stable). - **Edge cases:** Query with no terms (like empty string or just quotes with nothing - we should handle gracefully maybe treat as match nothing or everything, likely match nothing and perhaps warn), query that is only stopwords if we implement stopping - how do we handle (maybe treat them like any term if we didn't implement stops, or if stops, phrase "of the" would not find anything but should it? Possibly if removed stops, we can't find phrase composed solely of stops. That is acceptable trade-off if we use stops). - **International test:** If possible, a quick check: search for a phrase in a non-space delimited language if we treat each character as token, phrase search might require characters adjacent. If we did nothing special, searching "你好" might require that exact two-character token if we tokenized that way. Possibly works by default (if we consider each Han character a token, phrase "你 好" might not match "你好" if we put space - if user writes "你好" as phrase, maybe we treat that as two characters phrase but if our tokenization didn't separate them, they'd be one token and phrase search wouldn't find it since we look for '你' followed by '好'. Alternatively, if we treat every character as a token and they are adjacent, phrase would find them. Depending on approach, might be a nuance. We might not fully solve Chinese segmentation now, so we note that multi-character words in languages like Chinese won't be recognized as phrases properly unless user explicitly separates characters. That's an advanced use-case and can be documented as future work (perhaps using bigrams as tokens).

**Acceptance criteria:** - **Functional completeness:** Users can perform phrase queries and get correct results; can retrieve highlighted text snippets showing query matches; and can influence ranking or retrieve scores for result ordering. Specifically, known issues from v0.17 ("missing phrase, highlighting") are resolved[\[10\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L34-L39), and tests confirm these features working. - **Improved search experience:** In a sample scenario, doing a phrase search yields far fewer, more relevant results than the equivalent multi-term AND search, demonstrating improved precision (e.g., earlier example with "quick brown"). - **Highlight accuracy:** The returned highlights clearly mark all or key occurrences of query terms/phrases in results. They should make sense contextually (like not cutting off mid-word or such). - **Minimal overhead:** The presence of these features doesn't drastically slow down search queries. Ideally, non-phrase queries remain as fast as before (we should ensure that adding positions doesn't slow normal single-term search by much; it usually doesn't since you just ignore positions). Phrase queries are a bit slower than single term queries but still efficient. Highlight generation adds some processing but acceptable for typical result counts. We'll consider it acceptable if, for example, searching across 100k docs of average 100 words takes under, say, 200 ms for a phrase query and highlighting top 10 results, which is fine for many applications. Our smaller-scale tests should show much lower times, but just conceptually set a threshold. - **Ease of use:** The syntax to invoke phrase search and get highlights is straightforward and documented. If using quotes in search string, it should feel natural to users familiar with search engines. - **No negative impact on normal usage:** If someone doesn't use these features, the index and search still work as before (except maybe index size a bit bigger, which they won't notice unless memory constrained). Possibly mention in release notes that FTS index will be rebuilt to add positions, but that should happen automatically or be a one-time overhead on upgrade. - **Extensibility for future:** We should design with future improvements in mind (like analyzers). Possibly we allow in index definition to specify an analyzer (English by default, etc.). Even if we don't fully implement multiple languages now, our design (like an indexing pipeline that could include lowercasing, stopword removal, etc.) should be modular enough to extend. If our code is structured to easily add stemming later, that's good. This is more an internal acceptance that we aren't painting ourselves into a corner.

**Dependencies (SQ6):** - **Text analysis library:** If we choose to implement or include a Porter stemmer for English, that's a small algorithm we can code or copy (Porter stemmer is not large and is license free). If adding that, we should test it doesn't degrade results undesirably. Possibly not now, but keep as optional. - **Stopword list:** Could include a built-in list of common English stopwords to ignore in indexing (which reduces index size and speeds phrase queries by not indexing 'the','of', etc.). If we do, include ~30-100 common words. It's easy, but need to ensure not breaking phrase logic (e.g., if phrase contains a stopword we dropped, the phrase might never match). Many search engines handle that by indexing stopwords but giving them special treatment. We might avoid the complexity and index everything for correctness, given our scale is likely moderate. So maybe skip stopword removal for now (as v0.17 likely didn't remove them either). - **Data structures:** We might want to compress postings with positions. We can delta-encode positions to save space. If index is memory-resident, compressing might help memory but cost CPU. If on disk, compression helps IO. We might do a simple delta compression for positions (like store difference between consecutive positions, which are mostly positive small ints except between sentences maybe). Implementation can be simple (like varint encoding). - **Test harness:** Might need to incorporate some open-source text corpus or generate random text to test performance and correctness. Could use something like an excerpt of Wikipedia for tests. - **Collation issues:** Maybe not needed. We'll just consider everything as lower-case for indexing, which deals with case insensitivity. - **Integration with query engine:** We'll modify how TEXT_SEARCH works internally. It may need new code in query planner or executor to handle returning scores and positions or so. We should ensure that fits. Possibly we might implement FTS as a specialized index lookup function that returns an iterator of docIDs and scores. Might already be such given we had some C code bridging. We'll adapt accordingly. - **Documentation & examples:** We need to update docs on how to use phrase queries (with examples), highlight usage, what scoring means, etc. Possibly provide a small demo with sample data (like searching a small article collection in the docs). - **Upgrading index:** If users have existing FTS indexes from v0.17, our code should detect they lack position info and trigger re-index. We might implement a version number in index metadata and if outdated, rebuild on startup or first query. Alternatively, we mention in release notes that to use phrase search, re-create the index (less ideal if manual, better if auto). We'll likely handle it under the hood.

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - **v0.18.0:** Release these FTS improvements fully integrated (not behind a flag, just as enhancements to the existing FTS feature). We would note in release notes that "Full-text search now supports exact phrase queries using quotes, and highlight snippets via the HIGHLIGHT() function" etc. Also mention any index rebuild that happens automatically. - Mark them as stable (no reason to hide behind beta, as they don't affect critical DB operations, just an added feature). - We will verify with at least one quasi-real example (like indexing some documentation or known text and performing queries that show phrase vs non-phrase difference, and see highlight). - If any aspects are not fully complete (e.g., if we could not implement score ordering easily due to time), we might mention that as a limitation (but try to get it in, as ordering by relevance is pretty key). - After release, gather user feedback if they want more (like wildcard search). Could plan those for v0.19+ along with analyzers for different languages. - Ensure internal QA covers edge cases (which we will with tests). - There's a slight risk that rebuild of index might cause heavy CPU on upgrade. If large data, initial startup might be slow reindexing. We'll mention that in upgrade notes (so they aren't surprised if first startup is doing index work - possibly we log "Reindexing full-text index X for new version with phrase support").

By accomplishing these enhancements, Geode's FTS becomes competitive with basic search engine capabilities and greatly improves user satisfaction in text query scenarios. Users will no longer have to implement complex workarounds for phrase search or manually process highlights outside the DB, thus streamlining application development on Geode.

### 7\. Concurrency & Throughput (Multi-Threaded Execution)

**Why It Matters - User Impact (SQ1):** Geode's current execution model is essentially single-threaded, processing one query at a time per server process[\[22\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L14-L15). This limits the database's ability to handle concurrent requests and fully utilize multi-core CPUs. As usage grows (especially with multiple clients or heavy analytic queries), lack of concurrency becomes a bottleneck for throughput and can cause unfair query latency (one long query blocking others). By introducing **multi-threaded execution**, Geode can: - Serve many simultaneous queries or transactions in parallel, improving throughput (more transactions per second, better CPU utilization across cores). - Potentially speed up individual expensive queries by splitting parts of the work across threads (intra-query parallelism), though our initial focus might be on inter-query concurrency and simple intra-query parallelism for scan-heavy operations. - Ensure responsiveness: A slow query or operation won't freeze all other operations; this improves fairness and tail latency. For example, short OLTP transactions can complete quickly even if a big analytical query is running, because they can execute on different threads (and cores) concurrently.

This directly benefits **application developers and end-users** by providing more consistent performance under load. Also, **operations teams** can scale workload on a single node more effectively (not forced to shard data solely to use multiple cores; they can instead utilize a multi-core machine fully). In summary, multi-threading is crucial for Geode to be competitive in performance on modern hardware.

**Evidence of Need:** The code review flagged concurrency as a concern, noting "the server is single-threaded and primarily runs in a single-process context; no evidence yet of multi-core parallel query execution or rigorous thread safety beyond basic locks"[\[22\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L14-L15). It explicitly warns that as workload increases, there could be latent synchronization issues or performance bottlenecks that haven't been revealed with single-thread tests[\[22\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L14-L15). Additionally, competitor databases routinely exploit multi-threading: e.g., TiDB's performance advantage on analytical queries partly came from multi-threaded execution where MySQL was single-threaded[\[13\]](https://tidb-blog.vercel.app/posts/the-most-underrated-tidb-feature#:~:text=1,when%20comparing%20MySQL%20to%20TiDB). The review mentions limited testing of multi-threaded behavior, implying this is an area to develop and de-risk with high priority. Indeed, with features like distributed queries and FTS now in place, concurrency becomes even more important to handle those tasks in parallel and not sequentially.

**Design Proposal (SQ2):** We will implement a **multi-threaded scheduling system** for query and transaction execution: - **Thread Pool & Task Queue:** Introduce a pool of worker threads (size configurable, default maybe equals number of CPU cores) that can execute tasks. We will treat each query or transaction as a set of tasks that can be executed concurrently. At minimum, each query itself is a task that can run in its own thread, allowing multiple queries to run at once (inter-query parallelism). - **Inter-Query Concurrency:** The simplest step is to allow multiple client sessions/transactions to be processed in parallel. We'll remove the global lock that currently serializes execution (if one exists) and ensure the transactional subsystem can handle multiple simultaneous transactions. Geode's MVCC should allow concurrent reads easily (snapshots for each transaction) and concurrent writes with proper conflict checking. We will add necessary locks around data structures (e.g., the page cache, the memory allocator, index inserts) to ensure thread safety. Or, where feasible, adopt lock-free structures for certain components like read-mostly data (maybe indexes can be handled with atomic operations for reads). - **Intra-Query Parallelism:** We likely won't fully parallelize single queries in v0.18, except possibly in obvious places (e.g., if we implement some parallel graph algorithm or parallel index scan if data is partitioned). But we will lay groundwork: e.g., our thread pool can be used by certain operators. For instance, a heavy full graph scan could be split by range and processed by multiple threads, merging results. Or a join between two large sets could be done with a parallel sort-merge if data is partitioned. We might not implement these in initial rollout but design scheduling such that it's feasible later (like break a query plan into subplans that can be executed concurrently). - **Fair Scheduling:** Use a simple queue for tasks that distributes tasks in a roughly FIFO manner to worker threads. This ensures no starvation (assuming tasks eventually complete). For fairness, we might implement yielding: if a task runs too long, perhaps it can yield control and go back in queue. However, with multiple threads, OS time slicing might suffice for fairness on CPU. If one query hogs CPU on one core, others on different cores still proceed. If tasks outnumber cores heavily, OS will schedule them in and out, generally fairly. We may incorporate priorities if needed: e.g., short transactions could be prioritized to reduce latency (one approach is to classify tasks by expected duration). But initially, a uniform scheduling is fine. - **Transaction Coordination:** Ensure that concurrency doesn't violate isolation. Under MVCC, each read transaction sees a snapshot (so fine with concurrency). For writes, if two transactions write the same data concurrently, we rely on the 2PL or MVCC conflict detection. Likely Geode currently might allow one writer at a time to avoid conflicts. We should implement proper conflict detection: e.g., if two write transactions commit, one might have to abort if a conflict (or use a commit timestamp check). We should test this scenario. Possibly, Geode's serializable snapshot isolation might abort one on conflict or use index locking. We'll confirm the mechanism and ensure it works with multiple threads. If not, we might restrict concurrency of writes initially (still better than fully single-threaded: multiple reads can run and a write can run concurrently with reads, but maybe only one write at a time if needed). That would still be improvement (like readers don't block each other). - **Thread Safety:** Audit all parts of the code for thread safety: - The storage engine: ensure that operations on in-memory structures are locked or made atomic. E.g., the buffer pool, index structures might need fine-grained locks. We might implement coarse locks at first (like a global write lock for any write operation, and shared lock for reads) to guarantee correctness, then refine. Coarse locking would still allow read-read concurrency and one write concurrently with reads (if using something like readers-writer lock). That already improves throughput for read-heavy workloads. Over time, we can refine to allow multiple writes concurrently if they don't conflict logically, but that's complex (since a global write lock is safe but limiting). Perhaps use partitioned locking: e.g., lock per graph or per page range to allow disjoint writes concurrently. - The query engine: ensure intermediate data structures (like operator state, caches) are either local to a query (so no conflict between queries) or properly synchronized if global. For example, the plan cache is global - we must make that thread-safe (use a mutex when reading/updating plan cache to avoid race). - The network I/O: likely currently single-thread event loop. We might want to offload query execution from the network thread to the worker threads. So network thread reads a request, then pushes a task to execute the query on worker threads, then when done, network thread sends the response. Or we can use multiple threads for handling sockets as well (like acceptor thread and multiple handlers). Possibly simpler: we use an async model or one thread per connection. But one per connection can be heavy if many connections. Maybe best: use one thread for all network but it only handles I/O and delegates heavy work to workers. That is a common pattern (duty separation). - If using Zig's async/await, maybe easier to manage concurrency inside one OS thread, but that won't use multiple cores unless we have multi-threaded event loop. So likely use actual OS threads (Zig can spawn threads). - The Raft consensus logic might assume single-thread - we need to ensure Raft operations (like applying log entries) can be done concurrently with query tasks in a safe manner. Possibly dedicate one thread for Raft tasks or use locks around Raft state if accessed by multiple threads. It's critical to not break Raft correctness. Typically, Raft state machine apply is serialized in order anyway. We might continue to apply log entries sequentially (maybe in a dedicated thread), while queries (which are reading state) run in parallel. That works if queries read from stable committed state, which they do. Writes will flow through Raft sequentially but can be processed concurrently up until the commit point maybe. Possibly, we won't parallelize the Raft commit itself (it can't by nature; order must be preserved). But we can concurrently prepare multiple transaction writes while the log commit for a prior one is in flight, if our design allows it. - **Thread management:** We'll implement graceful thread lifecycle (start a pool at DB startup; join threads on shutdown, etc.). Provide config for number of threads. - **Monitoring:** Add some basic stats like number of active tasks, queue length, etc., for debugging if needed. Possibly as part of metrics.

**Standards & Comparators (SQ3):** - No standard mandates how the DB uses threads, but performance benchmarks (like TPC-C, LDBC) will expect multi-thread usage. We want to meet general expectation that a DB can saturate a multi-core machine. - We can draw from well-known multi-thread scheduling approaches: - Postgres: process-per-connection, which effectively uses OS threads (processes) in parallel; we can do thread-per-connection but that doesn't scale to thousands of connections well. Our design is more like a fixed thread pool, which is more akin to modern architecture (like a typical thread pool in C++ servers). - We should ensure ACID properties in concurrency: likely providing snapshot isolation means we have to guard against phenomena (like phantom if not using predicate locking - though we have RLS, not relevant here, RLS is Row-Level Security from earlier context; not to confuse). - Concurrency control: if we maintain serializability or snapshot, we must ensure our conflict detection is robust. Possibly incorporate a write-write conflict check and abort policy as needed (like if two write transactions conflict, one abort). - The system should appear to users as fully transactional with isolation as before, just faster with concurrency. No difference in semantics except maybe more interleaving of operations (which is fine as long as isolation holds). - Multi-threading might expose latent concurrency bugs; heavy testing under concurrent load (with e.g., Jepsen tests or at least random concurrency tests) is needed to ensure we don't introduce isolation violations or crashes. We will simulate worst-case patterns for that. - Another expectation: eventually to take advantage of parallelism for single queries, but even having multi-connection concurrency is a big first step. Perhaps note that intra-query parallelism for, say, graph algorithms or certain operators is planned (which aligns with e.g., upcoming GPU execution mention in future work[\[6\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L164-L169) - though GPU is separate, multi-thread CPU is immediate). - We should also consider fairness: many DBs have a scheduler to avoid starvation (maybe not an issue if tasks are short or OS handles fair scheduling). But if one query keeps using CPU, OS will give others some time too - so likely fine. - Some DBs implement a yield in execution loops to allow cooperative multi-tasking in user space if using fibers. We might just rely on preemptive OS scheduling with threads which is simpler albeit with overhead of context switches (which is fine given heavy tasks). - We should maintain determinism in results where needed (like if two queries run concurrently, each sees a consistent snapshot). We follow snapshot semantics to ensure that.

**Complexity & Risk (SQ4):** - **Data Races & Deadlocks:** Introducing threads always risks data races if any shared state isn't properly protected, or deadlocks if locks are not carefully ordered. We will do a thorough audit. Use thread sanitizer in tests if possible to catch some races. For deadlocks, we'll define lock ordering rules (e.g., always acquire locks in a fixed global order to avoid circular wait). Possibly we won't have many nested locks - we might try to keep locking localized to each structure, which reduces cross-lock deadlock possibilities. - **One client hogging pool threads:** If we have limited threads and one client opens many parallel queries, they could occupy all threads and potentially starve others. Solutions: maybe limit number of threads per client or allow pool to expand temporarily. Or simply trust that cores will handle them concurrently and OS will time-slice fairly among tasks if more tasks than cores. We likely will not implement per-client quotas initially, assuming normal usage distribution. - **Context switching overhead:** If tasks are very short (like very simple transactions), using many threads may add overhead from context switching. However, thread per request model is common and overhead is usually minor if thread count ~ core count. If tasks far outnumber threads, they queue, which is fine as long as queue wait doesn't degrade latency badly in moderate load. We might measure if short transactions throughput improves linearly or if overhead hits at certain thread counts. Possibly consider lightweight threads or async if needed, but probably not needed at our likely scale of concurrency (which might be tens or low hundreds of concurrent queries, not tens of thousands). - **Memory contention:** Many threads concurrently may contend on memory allocation, possibly causing locking in malloc. We might consider using per-thread allocators or thread caching to mitigate, or use a lock-free allocator. Zig's default might be thread-safe but could have overhead. If we see a lot of time in allocator, might tune by using thread local caching (some memory pools). - **I/O concurrency:** If multiple threads write to disk or network concurrently, we should ensure the underlying system can handle it (most OS can, disk can have multiple outstanding writes, network can handle multiple sends). Actually, concurrent disk writes may result in random I/O - but in high concurrency workloads that's expected. Possibly our storage uses sequential log writing which is single-stream; multiple threads writing will funnel into the log, which might become a bottleneck. But since commit log must be sequential anyway, that could throttle commit rate. We might accept that or consider group commit (batch commits to log) to improve throughput under contention (maybe a future improvement). - **Transactional anomalies:** We must double-check that our transaction semantics hold when actions interleave. For instance, phantom read: if one transaction scans a range and another inserts in that range concurrently, does the scanning transaction see it or not? Under snapshot isolation, it should not see it (since it took a snapshot at start). Our MVCC should handle that: the scanning transaction reads an earlier snapshot and new insert has a newer commit timestamp so it's not visible - as long as our MVCC is correctly implemented. We'll test that scenario with concurrency to ensure the scanning transaction either sees consistent snapshot (no phantom entry appears midway). - **Testing complexity:** We will need to simulate and test many concurrent operations to catch any subtle bugs. E.g., run 4 threads each doing random inserts/updates on overlapping data and then verifying final consistency or at least that no constraints violated or no crashes. Use row count invariants, etc. Possibly incorporate known concurrency test sets (like TPC-C or a simpler bank transfer simulation for consistency). - **Debugging concurrency issues:** Data races or deadlocks discovered in production are hard to debug. We plan to catch them in testing, but as usage grows, more patterns might emerge. We should instrument logging for when waiting on locks (maybe not in production normally, but debug logs if needed) to diagnose if a deadlock occurs. Also possibly implement a timeout on lock waits (to detect deadlocks by breaking out with an error rather than hang). That might help avoid full hangs, though erroring a transaction is not great but better than freeze. - **Complex interplay with other new features:** e.g., multi-threading plus distributed queries - we have to ensure we don't saturate too many resources: e.g., a distributed query might spawn tasks on multiple nodes. If both are multi-threaded, how do we avoid overload? Possibly fine since each node handles portion, but if each node spawn tasks across all cores, that's expected. Should coordinate if needed, but not an immediate concern for moderate cluster sizes.

**Benchmarks & Acceptance Criteria (SQ5):** - **Throughput test:** Simulate a multi-client workload like: - Short read transactions: measure transactions per second with 1 thread vs N threads. We expect near-linear scaling until CPU saturates. For example, with 4 cores, maybe we get ~3.5-4x throughput vs single core (some overhead). We set a target of e.g., >3x improvement on a CPU-bound workload going from 1 to 4 threads. If we achieve that, concurrency is working well. - Mixed read/write: e.g., simple updates, measure how many per second with concurrency. Even if writes are serialized via log, multiple threads can prepare next transactions while one logs, etc., so we should still see improvement vs single thread handling sequentially. We might see less scaling if log is bottleneck, but CPU usage of preparation could parallelize. If it doesn't scale much for writes, that might be okay if log I/O is limiting (we could consider group commit to mitigate, but not now). - **Latency test under load:** Choose a baseline throughput and measure average and 95th percentile latency of transactions with 1 thread vs multi-thread. Under concurrency, average latency might increase a bit due to contention, but hopefully not too much, and 95th percentile should not blow up massively. Ideally, even under load, latencies remain low because tasks share cores fairly. For fairness, we can also specifically test a scenario: one long query and many short queries concurrently. We measure the short queries' latencies and confirm they remain low (maybe only slightly higher than if no long query). This confirms that a heavy task doesn't stall others. E.g., if one thread doing a huge scan on core0, short query can run on core1 and finish nearly as normal - possibly hitting some shared resource but mostly unaffected. If we find short queries still get delayed significantly, we need to adjust scheduling or reduce resource contention (maybe heavy query hogging disk or locks? we find out). - **Correctness tests:** This includes: - **Atomicity & Isolation tests:** e.g., classic bank account transfer scenario: multiple threads move money between accounts. At end, total money should remain the same (atomic updates). We run many transfers concurrently and check final totals. If atomicity or isolation fails, totals would diverge or intermediate reads see partial transfer. We'll catch that if any. - **Dirty read/Write skew tests:** e.g., have two concurrent transactions update separate items based on reading each other's item (classic write skew). Under snapshot isolation, write skew can happen (not serializable) - if Geode aims for serializable, our system should prevent that. If just snapshot iso, it might allow it. We need to recall what isolation level Geode targets (the review hints at phantom reads partially addressed, likely snapshot isolation)[\[95\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L18-L20). We'll test if any surprising phenomena beyond expected (like if it's snapshot, write skew might occur which is known; if we promised serializable, then we need to enforce via some mechanism possibly not yet done). - **Deadlock handling:** If our locking strategy uses multiple locks, test scenarios that could deadlock if ordering not enforced. If we do detect a deadlock at runtime, ensure the system handles it gracefully (e.g., abort one transaction with an error). We can simulate by making two threads try to lock resources in opposite order (like thread1 locks A then B, thread2 locks B then A). If using global lock, deadlock not possible; if using fine locks, see if our code avoids or resolves it. We might incorporate a timeout to break it if it arises. - **Memory & stability:** Run a heavy concurrent workload for an extended time (e.g., 1 hour) to see if memory usage stays bounded (no leaks or unbounded growth in internal queues), and no threads crash or hang. - **Resource usage:** If we configure threads equal to cores, CPU should reach near 100% usage under heavy load (meaning we achieve parallelism). We'll measure CPU util and throughput scaling. If CPU doesn't saturate but throughput stops scaling, might be lock contention or I/O bound. We can profile to see where contention is (like if a lock is hot, maybe refine it). Acceptable scenario: maybe some moderate lock contention but still good scaling to say 8 cores. Ideally, locking overhead visible only at high thread counts and we can note it for future improvement. - **Effect on existing tests:** Run our full test suite with concurrency on (some tests might implicitly assume single threading, e.g., certain ordering or debug logs). We might adjust tests if they assume sequential behavior. But logically, nothing should break correctness tests except possibly timing differences. We'll ensure test harness accounts for concurrency (some timings might be nondeterministic now, but we generally avoid tests that rely on specific interleavings).

**Acceptance criteria:** - **Improved Throughput:** Multi-threaded Geode shows clear throughput gains on multi-core. For example, in our benchmarks, a read-heavy workload on 4 cores yields ~3-4x throughput vs single core (given near full CPU usage)[\[13\]](https://tidb-blog.vercel.app/posts/the-most-underrated-tidb-feature#:~:text=1,when%20comparing%20MySQL%20to%20TiDB), demonstrating we can use hardware efficiently. - **Preserved ACID semantics:** All concurrency tests pass - no lost updates, no dirty reads, consistent results as per isolation level. If we choose snapshot isolation, we allow phenomena like write skew, but in tests we should only see those expected ones and no worse (no dirty reads or lost update, etc.). If we aim for serializable, then none of those anomalies appear in tests (harder to guarantee, likely we are snapshot by design, as mention of partial phantom fixes suggests not full serializable yet[\[95\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L18-L20)). We'll clarify in docs what isolation we provide. - **No Deadlocks or App Hangs:** In torture tests, either none occur due to our design, or if a deadlock is possible, our system detects it (maybe via wait-for graph if we implement or via lock timeout). We consider it accepted if in repeated random tests we don't observe deadlocks causing indefinite hang. If one could theoretically happen, we implement a mechanism to handle it (like transaction abort). - **Better fairness:** The presence of a heavy query does not block others from making progress. This is qualitatively validated if short query latency under concurrency remains low (aside from slight CPU queuing). If one heavy job uses all cores, short tasks wait for OS timeslice but that still happens fairly. We may schedule tasks evenly across threads by queue, which inherently gives fairness (no thread sits idle if tasks waiting, and no task hogs all threads). - **Resource management:** The thread pool uses a reasonable number of threads (not oversubscribed by default), and overhead of idle threads is low. The system should not have significantly higher baseline CPU usage at idle (maybe a negligible increase for thread management, but ideally near zero when no work). - **Backward compatibility:** The new concurrency does not require user changes. It's internal, except we might expose a config for thread count. We ensure that if someone wants to run in single-thread mode (maybe for debugging or minimal resource env), they can set threads=1 and it effectively behaves as before. We'll include that option. - **Stability at scale:** We might attempt running with, say, 16-32 concurrent threads on test environment and see stable behavior (maybe diminishing returns after core count, but no crashes). If possible, test with many concurrent client connections (like 100 connections doing queries simultaneously) to see if queueing and scheduling handle it gracefully (some will queue but none lost or mis-processed). - **Documentation:** Update any tuning guide: e.g., how to configure thread pool size, potential effects (if heavy I/O, maybe thread per core is fine because more threads than cores can cause context switch overhead without benefit; we'll advise accordingly). Also mention any known limitations (e.g., if write concurrency is still limited by a global lock or log, etc., so users understand what to expect).

**Dependencies (SQ6):** - Possibly use Zig's standard thread APIs (like std.Thread.spawn). We might incorporate a work-stealing or any thread pool library if one exists for Zig, or implement a basic one (should not be too hard: a global job queue protected by a mutex or MPMC channel). - For atomic operations and locks, use Zig's std.atomic and std.Thread.Mutex/Cond etc. Evaluate if more fine-grained structures needed (like atomic reference counts etc. for memory mgmt). - If using condition variables or similar, ensure no spurious wake-ups cause issues. - Need to integrate with network handling: perhaps a dependency on a thread-safe or asynchronous networking library or adjust our existing event loop to multi-thread. If current network code is synchronous, maybe simpler: each accepted client connection could be handled by a worker thread (like how some servers accept then assign to thread). But then read/writes to same connection must happen from one thread to avoid interleaving lines. We can allocate each connection to a consistent thread (so that thread does both query exec and network send for that connection). That is thread-per-connection essentially. Could do that, or continue with one thread reading all and dispatch queries to pool, then pool returns result for writing. Many designs possible; we will choose one balancing complexity. Possibly easiest: main thread accepts and reads messages, then delegates execution to a pool thread, and that pool thread will send response back directly (meaning it would need to send on the socket, which might require thread-safe socket usage or handing the socket descriptor to the thread - which is fine if socket library supports concurrent sends, but safer to have one thread handle a connection entirely). - Actually, thread-per-connection might be simplest logically: each client runs on a dedicated thread, which picks from the pool? That yields many threads if many connections (less ideal). Instead, maybe a hybrid: limited pool but each query exclusively uses one thread at a time so it's fine - and network writing can be done by same thread that executed query, then it goes back to pool. But to know where to send, the thread needs the client socket handle. We can pass that in task context. That implies potential simultaneous writes on different threads to different sockets, which is fine (each socket used by at most one thread at a time). - If too complex to adjust network, we could temporarily say that network handling still single-threaded, which might be a bottleneck if many small responses. But if heavy queries are the main cost, it's okay. Possibly can postpone optimizing network multi-thread if time short, though ideally handle it for completeness. - Tools: use thread sanitizer and heavy multi-thread test frameworks if possible. If we had something like Jepsen, we might do a mini Jepsen run to verify consistency (maybe not needed now but could consider). - Possibly need to adjust memory management: we might use per-thread memory arenas for query temp memory to avoid contention. Zig's allocator could be global; maybe accept that overhead if minor. Or use std.Thread.LocalSlot for an allocator instance per thread if beneficial. - Also consider enabling parallel in our test rig (most tests are single-thread unit tests; we need integration tests that simulate concurrency which we will write).

**Rollout Plan (SQ7):** - **v0.18.0:** Introduce multi-threading as **enabled by default** if stable. Possibly hide it behind a config flag if we want to be cautious (like --experimental-multi-threading) during initial release, but given thorough testing we should be able to enable it outright. If we foresee risk, we might do a soft rollout: e.g., default thread count = 1 (single-thread mode) unless user opts in, but we clearly want concurrency so better to default to on if we trust it. We might at least thoroughly test in staging with variety of workloads to be confident before defaulting to >1. Perhaps we can mark it as "Beta: multi-threading can be toggled, default on for new deployments, but can be set off if any issues appear." Realistically, leaving it off by default means many might not know to turn it on and Geode would appear slow vs expectations. So I'd lean toward default on, with a fallback toggle threads=1 if needed. - We'll document how to configure thread count and any known quirks (like "currently, concurrent writes to the same record may result in one transaction abort for serialization" if that's how we handle it - which is fine and actually expected in serializable level). - We will closely monitor any bug reports around concurrency issues post-release. Possibly do a patch release if something major surfaces (like a rare race that we missed). - Through community or internal benchmarks, highlight the throughput improvement (this could be a big selling point - e.g., show near-linear scaling in a blog or whitepaper). - For future (v0.19+), we plan to build on this with more intra-query parallel execution (maybe parallel graph algorithm execution on multiple threads, etc., as hinted by "multi-threaded execution paths" in gap list). The ground work we lay now (thread pool, thread-safe data structures) will enable that. - Also future: transaction coordinator improvements to allow more parallel commits, possibly group commit for log to improve multi-thread write throughput (if log is bottleneck under many concurrent writes).

By releasing multi-threading, Geode will be able to handle modern workloads much more efficiently, aligning with user expectations of throughput and making it viable for higher volume use-cases. The careful testing and phased approach ensure we don't sacrifice stability for performance.

## Methodological Considerations

Our research and planning approach combined insights from internal Geode documentation with best practices reported in external sources. We prioritized evidence from authoritative and recent materials to ensure recommendations are grounded in reality: - **Evidence Strength & Bias:** We gave greatest weight to internal evidence (the v0.17 review and release notes) as it is the "source of truth" for Geode's status[\[1\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L9-L17)[\[96\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L34-L40). External comparators like CockroachDB's optimizer blog[\[86\]](https://www.cockroachlabs.com/blog/building-cost-based-sql-optimizer/#:~:text=So%20how%20do%20optimizers%20choose,common%20approaches%20to%20the%20problem), Neo4j's Fabric case[\[29\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=Sharding%20graph%20data%20is%20a,for%20the%20specific%20use%20case), and AWS/Azure guidelines[\[81\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=The%20logical%20place%20to%20start,your%20data%20in%20Amazon%20S3)[\[92\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=protecting%20data%20at%20rest,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS) served to validate that our identified gaps (e.g., need for global query ordering, backups, encryption) align with industry expectations. There is little dissent on these being necessary features; rather, the question was how to implement them in Geode's context. We cross-verified critical claims with multiple sources - e.g., the efficacy of multi-threading was corroborated by TiDB's documentation and general DBMS principles[\[13\]](https://tidb-blog.vercel.app/posts/the-most-underrated-tidb-feature#:~:text=1,when%20comparing%20MySQL%20to%20TiDB). This triangulation reduces risk of recommendations being off-mark. - **Data Recency:** All external references were 2021-2025, ensuring we incorporated the latest trends (like increasing emphasis on security and distributed operations). For instance, the Oracle 2025 announcement of DB MFA[\[9\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=The%20recent%20Oracle%20Database%2023ai,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c) and recent AWS storage best practices[\[81\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=The%20logical%20place%20to%20start,your%20data%20in%20Amazon%20S3) guided our security and backup proposals to be current. Older seminal concepts (like basic query optimizer theory) were implicitly known but not explicitly cited unless needed (we focused on recent applied discussions for practicality). - **Scope Boundaries:** We remained focused on the seven theme areas derived from the v0.17 gap analysis, avoiding digression into completed features. For example, we didn't revisit MATCH semantics or QUIC details except where they tangentially relate (like concurrency affecting Raft/QUIC operation). This ensured depth on target areas without re-litigating solved problems. - **Generality vs Specificity:** We tailored recommendations specifically to Geode's architecture. While we took cues from systems like Neo4j or CockroachDB, we adapted them to Geode's known design (Raft-based cluster, Zig implementation, etc.). For instance, our distributed query plan considered Geode's use of Raft for consistency (something unique to coordinate vs just copying Spanner's approach). This yields a plan that is realistic for Geode rather than generic one-size-fits-all. - **Testing and Measurement:** We emphasized concrete acceptance criteria and benchmarks for each theme. This methodological choice forces the design to be measurable and verifiable. It mitigates the risk of hand-wavy improvements - instead, we have defined what success looks like (e.g., "global ORDER BY yields deterministic pagination" or "throughput improves ~linear with cores"). This makes the plan actionable and allows the team to validate each improvement objectively. - **Bias and Assumptions:** One potential bias is optimism bias in new feature timelines (assuming everything can be done by v0.18/v0.19). We tried to temper this by identifying where a phased approach or limited initial scope is wise (notably, making CBO default only after proving it safe, or implementing simple concurrency before fine-grained parallelism). Another is confirmation bias in focusing on these gaps because they were listed - however, since they come from a comprehensive review, addressing them is likely correct priority. We cross-checked if any other gaps are critical that weren't listed (we touched on a few like need for analyzers in FTS or hinting at RBAC in security, but those indeed seem less urgent than listed ones, so we stuck to the list). - **Generality:** The recommendations are mostly general best practices being applied to Geode (like S3 backups, TDE), thus likely generalizable across many DB systems, but we contextualized them (like using Raft snapshots for backup consistency, which is specific to Geode's architecture). This ensures we're not applying a method unsuited to Geode's model (for example, our approach to distributed queries leverages existing Geode Raft consistency, rather than requiring a totally new distributed transaction protocol). - **Data Quality:** We assumed internal docs accurately reflect Geode's implementation status (some risk if, say, a feature was documented but not working fully - but since the CANARY system tracks tested features, we trust that info[\[16\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-24-Review.md#L4-L12)). For external data, we used official or reputable sources (AWS blog, Neo4j site, etc.) to avoid misinformation. - **Bias:** We attempted to present balanced rationales. For contentious decisions (like enabling CBO by default), we weighed pros (performance) and cons (risk of regressions) and built a gradual approach to mitigate risk. In security, we acknowledged user convenience vs security trade-off (e.g., MFA optional to avoid burdening all users).

**Generalizability & Limitations:** This plan is tailored to Geode v0.17's context. Some recommendations (like certain performance targets, or using S3 specifically) might not generalize identically to an on-premises environment with no object storage or to a future version of Geode with different architecture. However, the underlying principles (distributing queries, securing data, optimizing by default, etc.) are broadly applicable to distributed databases. Our evidence from similar systems suggests these improvements will yield positive outcomes. One limitation is that some proposed metrics (like 85% CBO accuracy) were somewhat arbitrary targets drawn from internal goals[\[37\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/NEXT.md#L19-L25); actual user satisfaction will be binary (planner either does the right thing most of time or not). Another is that our concurrency tests, while extensive, may not capture all real-world access patterns; we assume that by hitting known difficult patterns (bank transfers, etc.) we cover worst cases, but real workloads might reveal new challenges (like extremely heavy contention scenarios that require more sophisticated scheduling or isolation levels).

We also recognize that introducing these changes simultaneously (distributed queries, multithreading, etc.) can create complex interactions (like multi-threaded distributed query execution). We mitigated risk by modularizing proposals and ensuring each has clear acceptance criteria to test in isolation, as well as planning incremental rollout where possible.

In summary, our methodology ensured a grounded, evidence-led plan that addresses each priority gap with concrete, testable improvements, while being conscious of the interplay between features and the practical constraints of the team's capacity and timeline.

## Implications & Outlook

Implementing this update plan in v0.18-v0.19 will significantly strengthen Geode's position as a production-ready distributed graph database: - **Near-term (next two releases):** Geode will become far more capable in real deployment scenarios. Distributed query federation and S3 backups address reliability and scalability needs for immediate adoption - large graphs can be partitioned and still queried seamlessly, and operators can sleep knowing backups are off-site and restorable[\[3\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L37-L40)[\[81\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=The%20logical%20place%20to%20start,your%20data%20in%20Amazon%20S3). Security hardening (TDE, MFA) will satisfy enterprise infosec checklists, unlocking use in regulated industries where these are mandatory[\[92\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=protecting%20data%20at%20rest,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS)[\[62\]](https://fuzziebrain.com/content/enable-mfa-for-oracle-databases#:~:text=Database%20owners%20can%20now%20opt,has%20been%20backported%20to%2019c). Performance will get a two-fold boost: optimized plans by default mean less worry about query tuning, and multi-threading means Geode can handle high-throughput workloads and multiple concurrent clients without choking[\[13\]](https://tidb-blog.vercel.app/posts/the-most-underrated-tidb-feature#:~:text=1,when%20comparing%20MySQL%20to%20TiDB). Full-text enhancements make Geode more attractive for content-heavy applications by providing user-friendly search features out-of-the-box. The net effect: Geode v0.19 should be a compelling "1.0"-level release for broad usage, having closed the major correctness and feature gaps identified in v0.17. - **Mid-term (next year or so):** With these fundamentals in place, focus can shift to **refinement and secondary features**. For example, once federation is working for queries, we might tackle cross-shard constraints or global indexes to further optimize distributed queries (ensuring they can use indexes on shards, etc.). Backup/restore could be enhanced with incremental backups and integration with cloud backup services (like AWS Backup) for easier automation - the groundwork of S3 backup sets the stage for that[\[97\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=,the%20system%20also%20tracks%20changes). The CBO, once default, can be iteratively improved by adding more sophisticated cost factors (like accounting for network cost in distributed plans, or learning from execution feedback). We might even pursue automatically collecting stats or using adaptive optimization if needed. In CDC, one could add more sink types or finer control (like per-tenant webhooks, or ordering guarantees across shards). Security could expand to include fine-grained authorization (role-based access for specific graph elements) and auditing (maybe capturing who queried what). Full-text search might evolve by adding **stemming, synonyms, or more languages support** to appeal to international use-cases - perhaps integrating an existing library for analyzers (like Apache Lucene's analyzers) if license permits. - **Long-term (beyond):** With core stability and features solid, Geode can explore innovative differentiators. The mention of **GPU acceleration for graph algorithms**[\[6\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/docs/v0.17.0-RELEASE-NOTES.md#L164-L169) hints at leveraging heterogeneous hardware for big graph processing - multi-threading support will help utilize GPU pipelines effectively (by feeding data concurrently to GPU, etc.). Another long-term vector is **global federation** - extending query federation across multiple Geode clusters or across Geode and other data sources (this could align with trends in data virtualization). Also, as data grows, features like **automatic sharding/partitioning, rebalancing**, and perhaps **multi-region replication** might come into play (the roadmap's mention of multi-leader or sharding beyond our current single-shard federation suggests eventual scaling to multi-shard writes as well[\[3\]](https://github.com/spyderorg/geode/blob/1fda273b48808cc34f507c838b87ddd783dc8f74/kb/reviews/2025-09-21-Review.md#L37-L40)). In terms of query capabilities, implementing the full ISO GQL spec (including things like updates with complex subqueries, pattern matching across shards with ease) will be ongoing, but our distributed execution and CBO improvements pave the way for adding complexity there without sacrificing performance.

**Ecosystem and Adoption Implications:** These improvements also position Geode more favorably against competitors: - For example, Neo4j now supports multi-database sharding (Fabric) - with Geode offering federation, it can claim a similar ability to scale horizontally[\[29\]](https://neo4j.com/fosdem20/#:~:text=Sharding%20graph%20data%20is%20a,for%20the%20specific%20use%20case). - Amazon Neptune emphasizes cloud integration (automated backups to S3, encryption by default) - with our backup and TDE, Geode meets those checkboxes[\[81\]](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/designing-a-resilient-and-cost-effective-backup-strategy-for-amazon-s3/#:~:text=The%20logical%20place%20to%20start,your%20data%20in%20Amazon%20S3)[\[92\]](https://www.enterprisedb.com/blog/everything-need-know-postgres-data-encryption#:~:text=protecting%20data%20at%20rest,requirements%20such%20as%20PCI%20DSS), making it viable for cloud deployment in AWS/Azure. - The performance improvements (CBO, multi-threading) and features like FTS might even exceed some older graph DBs that lack those search capabilities or still rely on single-thread execution for certain operations. This could open new domains for Geode - e.g., doing real-time analytics on text-enriched graph data, or serving as a central graph query engine in microservice architectures where high concurrency is needed.

**Risks and Mitigations:** While the plan is comprehensive, there are risks: - _Execution complexity:_ Many moving parts are being developed in parallel (distributed queries, threading, etc.). That raises the risk of integration issues or delays. We mitigated by clearly prioritizing and phasing (e.g., ensuring baseline federation in v0.18 and refining it in v0.19). - _Stability vs new features:_ We must not compromise stability/correctness in pursuit of performance or features. Our acceptance criteria and heavy emphasis on testing in each theme addresses this - e.g., concurrency is only a win if we maintain ACID; we have specific tests for that and will keep a flag to disable if needed as a safety net initially. - _User adoption risk:_ If any of these changes significantly alter user-facing behavior (like requiring a key at startup, or query plans changing unpredictably), there could be pushback. We handled that by making security opt-in (so existing deployments not forced to use MFA overnight), and by thoroughly validating CBO so it should only surprise users by making things faster (and if not, they can still override in worst case). - _Performance tuning:_ Multi-threading and distributed execution introduce new tunables (thread counts, how to partition queries, etc.). We provide sensible defaults (threads = cores, coordinator chooses a naive plan initially) and can refine heuristics as we gather usage data. Possibly by v0.19/20 we might develop more intelligent auto-tuning (like query planner choosing number of threads for a query based on cost).

In conclusion, the outlook after implementing this plan is that Geode will have addressed the "post-review gaps" so comprehensively that focus can shift from catching up on fundamentals to innovating on unique features (like advanced graph analytics). The database will be more **robust (backups, security)**, more **scalable (federation, parallelism)**, and more **convenient/performant by default (CBO, search enhancements)**. This should translate into higher user confidence, broader adoption (including in enterprise settings that were previously blocked), and a stronger community as Geode matures from a promising project to a deployable product. The process of gathering evidence and setting measurable criteria also sets a precedent for Geode's development: feature work will be validated against real-world use-cases and standards, ensuring the project's ongoing evolution remains aligned with user needs and technical excellence.

## Open Questions & Research Gaps

During this deep-dive, a few open questions emerged that go beyond the immediate scope but are worth future investigation: - **Global Query Optimization:** Our plan implements distributed queries with a coordinator but uses relatively simple methods (e.g., merging sorted results). An open question is how to handle complex distributed joins optimally.
